For R&D teams adopting agentic workflows
FSAD / Full Stack Agentic Development
Methodology for R&D Teams

Full Stack Agentic Development

A spec-driven, agentic-first methodology where small cross-functional pods use markdown as a common language to direct teams of AI agents — collapsing cycle times from weeks to hours.

01 — What is FSAD

The Paradigm Shift

FSAD replaces the traditional handoff-heavy SDLC with a tight feedback loop where humans define what and why in markdown — and AI agents handle the how.

🎯

Spec-Driven, Not Code-Driven

The specification — written in markdown — is the primary artifact. It's a living document that evolves in real-time during collaborative sessions. Code is a compilation target, not the source of truth.

🤝

Cross-Functional Pods

Small teams of 2–4 (PM + UX + Engineer, or PM + 2 Engineers) sit together — physically or virtually — and co-author the spec in real-time. No handoffs. No waiting for the next sprint.

📝

Markdown as Common Language

Markdown is lightweight, version-controlled, diff-friendly, and — critically — the native instruction format for AI agents. Every role can read and write it. It bridges the gap between human intent and machine execution.

🤖

Agent-Delegated Execution

Once the spec is validated, teams of AI agents — running in parallel via tools like Claude Code, Codex, or Gemini CLI — implement the code. The human role shifts from writing code to reviewing, steering, and verifying agent output.

Models forget. Hooks don't. The Hooks principle — see Claude Best Practices › Hooks
02 — The FSAD Workflow

From Intent to Deployed Feature

A five-phase loop designed for speed, clarity, and human oversight. Each phase has a clear checkpoint — you don't advance until the current phase is validated.

Phase 1
Intent & Discovery
PMUX
→ problem.md
Phase 2
Live Spec Session
PMUXEng
→ spec.md
Phase 3
Implementation Planning
EngAgent
→ plan.md + tasks/
Phase 4
Agent Execution
AgentAgentAgent
→ code + tests
Phase 5
Human Review & Ship
EngUXPM
→ deploy

Phase 1 — Intent & Discovery

The PM and UX designer define the problem space and user outcomes. They collaborate — often with an AI research assistant — to surface user needs, competitive landscape, and constraints. The output is a concise problem statement in markdown that frames what we're solving and for whom. No solutions yet.

problem.mduser-stories.mdconstraints.md

Phase 2 — Live Spec Session

The entire pod convenes in a real-time collaborative session. The PM contributes business requirements, UX contributes interaction patterns and design decisions, and the Engineer contributes technical feasibility and architecture. They co-author the spec in markdown together — design decisions are embedded inline, not deferred to a separate Figma-to-Jira handoff. This is where FSAD breaks the handoff chain.

spec.mddesign-decisions.mdAGENTS.md

Phase 3 — Implementation Planning & Task Decomposition

The Engineer feeds the spec to an AI agent in "plan mode" (read-only). The agent analyzes the codebase, generates an implementation plan, and decomposes work into discrete, parallelizable tasks. The human reviews the plan against the spec — catching hallucinations, missed edge cases, and architectural misalignment before any code is written.

plan.mdtasks/*.mdtest-plan.md

Phase 4 — Parallel Agent Execution

Multiple AI agents execute tasks simultaneously in isolated git worktrees. Each agent works against the spec as its source of truth. A Coordinator agent (or human) routes tasks, and a Verifier agent checks each output against the spec's acceptance criteria. The engineer monitors, steers, and intervenes only when agents get stuck or drift.

src/**tests/**agent-logs/

Phase 5 — Human Review & Ship

The full pod reviews the implementation. The Engineer checks code quality, architecture adherence, and test coverage. UX validates the interface against design decisions in the spec. The PM validates against user stories and acceptance criteria. Reviews focus on spec compliance, not syntax — because agents produce consistent code from living specs. Then ship.

PR reviewspec-diff.mddeploy
Spec-driven, not code-driven. Structure is permanent; prompting is temporary. The FSAD thesis
03 — The FSAD Pod

Small Teams, Shared Context

📝spec.md
🎯PM
🎨UX
⚙️Eng
🤖Agents

The Markdown is the Meeting

In FSAD, the spec.md file replaces most of the ceremony of traditional development — the Jira tickets, the Figma handoffs, the sprint planning debates, the slack threads asking "what did we decide?"

PM contributes user intent, business constraints, success metrics, and acceptance criteria directly into the spec.

UX Designer contributes interaction patterns, component decisions, layout descriptions, and accessibility requirements — inline, not as a separate Figma file.

Engineer contributes technical constraints, data models, API contracts, and architecture decisions — and validates feasibility in real-time.

AI Agents consume the spec and execute implementation in parallel — the "workforce" that the pod directs.

04 — The Markdown Layer

Why Markdown Changes Everything

Markdown has evolved from passive documentation into an active instruction layer that governs AI behavior. It's the universal interface between human intent and agent execution.

spec.md — The Shared Source of Truth
# Feature: Notification Preferences

## User Story
As a mobile user, I want per-channel
notification controls so I can reduce
noise without missing critical alerts.

## UX Decisions
- Toggle pattern: iOS-style switches
- Grouping: By channel (push, email, SMS)
- Default state: All enabled on signup
- Quiet hours: Time-range picker
  <!-- UX: reviewed with accessibility team -->

## Technical Spec
- Data model: preferences JSONB column
- API: PATCH /users/:id/preferences
- Validation: At least 1 channel active
- Performance: < 200ms p95 response
AGENTS.md — Instructions for Agent Execution
# Project: NotifyPro

## Architecture
Monorepo. React frontend, Node API,
PostgreSQL. Follow existing patterns.

## Conventions
- Components: functional + hooks only
- Styling: Tailwind utility classes
- State: Zustand for client state
- Tests: Vitest. Min 80% coverage.

## Guardrails
- Never modify auth or billing modules
- Run `pnpm lint && pnpm test` before
  committing any change
- Each task = one atomic commit
  # Agent reads this on every session
05 — FSAD vs. Traditional Agile/Scrum

Two Worlds Compared

FSAD doesn't reject Agile values — it fulfills them by removing the ceremony that slowed teams down.

Traditional Agile/Scrum — Feature Delivery

Typical: 2–4 weeks per feature

FSAD — Feature Delivery

Typical: 1–3 days per feature
DimensionTraditional Agile / ScrumFSAD
Source of TruthJira tickets, Confluence docs, Figma files — scattered across tools. Often outdated or conflicting.A single spec.md in the repo. Version-controlled, diff-friendly, always current.
Team StructureSeparate PM, design, and engineering functions. Handoffs between roles. Scrum team of 5–9.Cross-functional pod of 2–4. PM, UX, and Engineer co-author the spec in the same session.
Planning CadenceSprint planning every 2 weeks. Backlog grooming. Story point estimation. Daily standups.Continuous flow. Features specced, planned, and executed in hours to days. No sprint boundaries.
Design ProcessUX creates mockups in Figma → handoff → engineers interpret → back-and-forth.UX writes design decisions directly into the spec. Agents consume them as instructions.
Code GenerationEngineers manually write all code. Speed limited by human throughput.AI agents generate code from spec in parallel. Engineers review and verify.
Cycle Time2–4 week sprints. Features may span multiple sprints.Hours to days. Spec-to-deployment loop measured in hours.
Knowledge RetentionDecisions live in Slack threads, meeting notes, people's heads.Every decision captured in markdown files committed to the repo.
Quality AssuranceSeparate QA phase. Manual + automated tests written by engineers.Tests generated alongside code from spec acceptance criteria. QA is embedded.
Role of EngineersPrimary code authors. Value = lines of code, story points.Architects, spec authors, agent orchestrators. Value = outcomes shipped.
ScalabilityAdding people adds communication overhead (Brooks's Law).Scaling = adding more agents, not more people. Pod of 3 directs N agents.
06 — Why FSAD Wins

The Advantages

Cycle Time Compression

Eliminating handoffs and delegating implementation to parallel agents compresses 2–4 week sprints into 1–3 days.

No Handoff Latency

No more Figma → Jira → Slack → Code chain. Design decisions reach code without translation loss.

Parallel Execution

Multiple agents in isolated worktrees implement different parts of the spec simultaneously.

Single Source of Truth

spec.md replaces the constellation of Jira, Confluence, Figma, Slack threads, and meeting notes.

Knowledge Compound

Better specs produce better agent output, which produces cleaner codebases, which produces even better agent output. A virtuous cycle.

Democratized Contribution

PMs and designers contribute directly to the artifact that drives implementation. No code required.

07 — Honest Tradeoffs

Strengths & Challenges

FSAD

✦ Strengths

Radical Cycle Time ReductionCompresses spec-to-ship from weeks to days via agent parallelism.
Eliminates Interpretation DriftThe spec humans validate is the same artifact agents read.
Scales Without HeadcountA 3-person pod outputs at the rate of a 10–15 person traditional team.
Built-in Institutional KnowledgeSpecs and decision logs live in the repo. Onboarding = reading markdown.
Higher-Quality First DraftsAgent-generated code from structured specs is more consistent than code under sprint pressure.

✦ Challenges

High Spec Quality BarGarbage in, garbage out. Vague specs produce hallucinated code.
Non-Deterministic AgentsAgents can ignore, duplicate, or over-interpret specs. Human review is critical.
Cultural Shift RequiredEngineers shift to orchestration; PMs and designers must learn structured markdown.
Tooling Is MaturingThe ecosystem evolves rapidly. Teams must stay current.
Not for All ProblemsNovel research and deeply creative work still require human coding.

Traditional Agile/Scrum

✦ Strengths

Battle-TestedDecades of refinement. Clear roles, ceremonies, massive training ecosystem.
Predictable CadenceSprint rhythm gives stakeholders reliable forecasting.
Human CraftsmanshipEngineers deeply understand code they write. No black-box risk.
No AI DependencyWorks without AI tooling, model quality, or spec-writing discipline.

✦ Challenges

Handoff OverheadPM → Design → Engineering chain introduces latency and lost context.
Ceremony OverheadPlanning, grooming, standups, retros consume hours without producing value.
Brooks's LawAdding engineers adds communication overhead. Doesn't scale linearly.
Knowledge Trapped in PeopleContext leaves when team members leave.
The Bottom Line

FSAD is not post-Agile.
It's Agile fulfilled.

The Agile Manifesto valued "individuals and interactions over processes and tools" and "working software over comprehensive documentation." FSAD delivers on both — by making the spec a live interaction between individuals, and by compiling that spec into working software through AI agents in hours, not weeks.

Full Stack Agentic Development

A methodology for R&D teams building with AI agents.

Team Design for Agentic Development

Pod Compositions That Work

Not every feature needs the same team shape. FSAD defines six pod configurations — each optimized for a different type of work, all unified by the same spec-driven workflow.

6Pod Configs
2–4Humans
NAgents
1spec.md
01 — Pod Explorer

Choose the Right Pod for the Work

Click a configuration to explore its composition, when it works best, and the artifacts it produces.

📝spec.md
🎯PM
🎨UX
⚙️Eng
🤖Agents
Configuration A
The Triad
3 humans + N agents

The default FSAD pod. One Product Manager, one UX Designer, and one Engineer work as a single unit — co-authoring the spec in real-time, with no handoffs. This covers the full spectrum of product intent, user experience, and technical execution in the smallest group that can make complete decisions.

Role Responsibilities
🎯

Product Manager

Owns problem statement, user stories, acceptance criteria, and success metrics. Frames the "what" and "why." Validates agent output against business intent.

🎨

UX Designer

Contributes interaction patterns, component choices, layout, and accessibility as structured markdown. Replaces the Figma-then-handoff pattern with inline design decisions.

⚙️

Engineer

Contributes architecture, data models, API contracts, and constraints. Orchestrates agents during execution. Reviews code quality and architectural coherence.

🤖

AI Agents

Consume spec.md and AGENTS.md to generate implementation in parallel via isolated git worktrees. Directed by the Engineer, constrained by the spec.

When To Use

  • User-facing features with both UI and backend work
  • New product areas requiring full alignment
  • Features where UX decisions impact architecture
  • Your default pod for standard product development

Key Artifacts

  • spec.md — Full feature spec with business, UX, and technical sections
  • AGENTS.md — Project-level agent instructions and guardrails
  • plan.md — Agent-generated implementation plan
  • tasks/*.md — Decomposed, parallelizable work units
📝spec.md
🎯PM
⚙️Eng
🤖Agents
Configuration B
The Builder Duo
2 humans + N agents

The leanest FSAD pod. PM and Engineer work directly together when the feature is API-centric, backend-heavy, or follows established design patterns. Maximum speed — from spec to deployment in hours.

Role Responsibilities
🎯

Product Manager

Defines business requirement, API expectations, success criteria, and edge cases. Also makes lightweight UX decisions for admin interfaces or existing patterns.

⚙️

Engineer

Full ownership of technical spec, architecture, and agent orchestration. Co-authors spec with PM, then immediately enters plan mode and begins execution.

When To Use

  • API development, backend services, data pipelines
  • Admin/internal tools with standard UI patterns
  • Bug fixes and refactors with clear requirements
  • Rapid prototypes to validate feasibility

Superpower

  • Fastest spec-to-ship of any pod configuration
  • Minimal coordination — two people, one conversation
  • Ideal entry point for teams new to FSAD
📝spec.md
🎨UX
⚙️FE Eng
⚙️BE Eng
🤖Agents
Configuration C
The Experience Pod
3 humans + N agents

Design-led for work where the UI is the product. UX Designer leads the spec; two engineers (frontend + backend) ensure the experience is technically achievable. PM role absorbed by UX for interaction-driven features.

Role Responsibilities
🎨

UX Designer (Pod Lead)

Leads the spec. Defines interaction models, animation, responsive breakpoints, component structure, and accessibility. Owns "what" and "why."

⚙️

Frontend Engineer

Translates UX decisions into component architecture and state management. Orchestrates frontend agents. Reviews rendered output against spec.

⚙️

Backend Engineer

Provides API contracts, data shapes, and performance guarantees. Orchestrates backend agents in parallel.

When To Use

  • Onboarding flows, wizards, multi-step journeys
  • Data visualization dashboards
  • Design system component development
  • Consumer-facing experiences where polish is differentiating

Key Artifacts

  • spec.md — UX-led with detailed interaction patterns
  • components.md — Component tree and state descriptions
  • api-contract.md — Backend data shapes
  • accessibility.md — WCAG checklist
📝spec.md
🎯PM
🎨UX
⚙️Lead
⚙️Eng 2
🤖Agents
Configuration D
The Full Stack Pod
4 humans + N agents

Maximum-size FSAD pod for large, cross-cutting features spanning multiple services, requiring deep UX work and significant business risk. Two engineers split: one leads architecture, the other owns a specialized domain (security, performance, data).

Role Responsibilities
🎯

Product Manager

Owns business case, cross-team dependencies, and phased acceptance criteria for incremental shipping.

🎨

UX Designer

Full design authority. May produce lightweight wireframes alongside spec for large initiatives.

⚙️

Lead Engineer

Owns architecture, agent orchestration strategy, and cross-service decomposition.

⚙️

Specialist Engineer

Owns specialized domain — security, database migration, performance. Orchestrates own agent set in parallel.

When To Use

  • Features spanning multiple services or repositories
  • High-stakes initiatives (payments, auth, compliance)
  • Features requiring specialized expertise
  • Quarterly "big bets"

Watch Out For

  • Don't default to 4 — use only when complexity demands it
  • Two engineers must have distinct domains
  • If the pod feels slow, it's a Triad wearing a 4th person
📝spec.md
⚙️Eng 1
⚙️Eng 2
🤖Agents
Configuration E
The Platform Pod
2 humans + N agents

Pure engineering pod for infrastructure, platform, and developer experience work. No PM, no designer. Two engineers co-author a technical spec. This pod handles the work that makes other pods faster — CI/CD, shared libraries, migrations, monitoring.

Role Responsibilities
⚙️

Engineer 1 (Architecture)

Owns system design, API contracts for internal consumers, and architectural decisions. Orchestrates agents for infrastructure-as-code and integration.

⚙️

Engineer 2 (Reliability)

Focuses on monitoring, alerting, performance testing, rollback strategies, and security. Reviews for production readiness.

When To Use

  • CI/CD pipeline work and deployment automation
  • Shared libraries, SDKs, internal tooling
  • Database migrations and schema evolution
  • Monitoring, observability, and security hardening

Key Artifacts

  • spec.md — Technical spec with Mermaid diagrams
  • adr/*.md — Architecture Decision Records
  • runbook.md — Operational runbook
  • perf-baseline.md — Performance benchmarks
📝spec.md
🎯PM
🎨UX
⚙️Eng
🔬Research
Configuration F
The Discovery Pod
3 humans + N agents (research mode)

Same Triad composition but in research mode. Not trying to ship — trying to find the right thing to ship. Spec is a hypothesis, agents build throwaway prototypes, output is a validated spec handed to a delivery pod. FSAD's answer to Dual Track Agile.

Role Responsibilities
🎯

Product Manager

Drives hypothesis. Defines customer problem, assumptions, and success criteria. Uses AI research agents for competitor analysis and customer synthesis.

🎨

UX Designer

Rapidly explores interaction models. Uses agents to generate multiple UI variants for testing with users.

⚙️

Engineer

Builds rapid throwaway prototypes. Validates technical feasibility. Explicitly not building production code.

When To Use

  • New product areas with high uncertainty
  • Exploring multiple solution approaches
  • Customer-facing prototypes for usability testing
  • Technical spikes to validate feasibility

Output

  • hypothesis.md — Problem hypothesis with assumptions
  • spec.md — Validated spec ready for delivery pod
  • prototype/ — Throwaway prototype
  • findings.md — What was learned and invalidated
🧪test-plan.md
⚙️QA Lead
⚙️Test Eng
🤖QA Agents
Configuration G
The QA Pod
2 humans + N QA agents

Specialist engineering pod that builds the testing infrastructure used by every other pod — agents for test generation and code review, skills for coverage reporting, hooks for pre-commit and post-deploy verification gates, and MCP tools for fixture management. Its output is reusable tooling, not features.

Role Responsibilities
⚙️

QA Lead

Owns test strategy, verification gate standards, and acceptance criteria frameworks. Reviews agent-generated test output for coverage gaps. Defines the quality contract other pods must meet.

⚙️

Test Engineer

Builds custom agents, skills, hooks, and MCP tools for the team's testing stack. Maintains fixture libraries, regression suites, and test scaffolding.

🤖

QA Agents

Generate unit, integration, and e2e tests; run coverage analysis; perform spec-compliance validation; surface regressions across the codebase.

When To Use

  • Establishing QA infrastructure for a new product or team
  • Building custom test-generation agents and skills for specialized domains
  • Creating shared hooks and verification gates for CI/CD pipelines
  • Reducing testing tooling debt — standardizing test patterns across pods

Key Artifacts

  • test-plan.md — QA strategy, coverage targets, and acceptance gates
  • agents/ — Custom test generation, code review, and validation agents
  • skills/ — Coverage reporter, regression runner, fixture builder
  • hooks/ — PreToolUse / PostToolUse verification gates
02 — Pod Selection Matrix

Matching Work to Pod Shape

The goal is always the smallest effective pod — complexity should justify size.

Work TypeRecommended PodSizeWhy This Shape
Standard user-facing featureTriad3Full coverage of product, design, and engineering.
API / backend serviceBuilder Duo2No UI decisions. PM + Engineer. Maximum speed.
Complex onboarding flowExperience Pod3UX-led. Interaction design is the product.
Payments integrationFull Stack Pod4High stakes, cross-service, needs specialist.
CI/CD pipeline overhaulPlatform Pod2Pure engineering. Architecture + reliability split.
New product explorationDiscovery Pod3High uncertainty. Validate before committing.
Bug fix / refactorBuilder Duo2Requirements clear. Fast turnaround.
Design system componentExperience Pod3UX owns component API and behavior.
Database migrationPlatform Pod2Deep technical work. No user-facing impact.
Cross-cutting auth overhaulFull Stack Pod4Touches every service. Needs security + UX + PM.
QA / test infrastructureQA Pod2Build reusable agents, skills, hooks. Engineering-only scope.
03 — Pod Anti-Patterns

Compositions That Don't Work

🚫

The Solo Hero

One engineer doing everything. No second perspective means spec blind spots go unchallenged. Agents amplify mistakes at scale.

🚫

The Oversized Pod

5+ humans on a single spec. Communication overhead grows quadratically. Split into two pods with a shared API contract.

🚫

The Handoff Pod

PM writes spec → throws to designer → throws to engineer. Same sequential process. If you aren't co-authoring, it's not FSAD.

🚫

The Agent-Only Pod

Fully autonomous agents, no human review. Agent reliability is non-deterministic. Every pod requires human validation gates.

🚫

The Part-Time Pod

Members at 20% allocation across multiple pods. FSAD's speed comes from real-time collaboration. Async stalls the spec session.

🚫

The Duplicate Pod

Two engineers with identical skills. The second must own a distinct domain — otherwise use a Triad and let agents parallelize.

The Principle

Smallest Effective Pod.
Maximum Agent Leverage.

The right pod is the smallest group of humans who can make complete decisions about a feature without external dependencies — then direct an arbitrarily large fleet of agents to execute in parallel.

Full Stack Agentic Development

Pod Compositions Guide — for R&D teams adopting FSAD.

Example Workflows for Teams Getting Started

Workflows in Practice

Concrete, end-to-end walkthroughs of the skills and rituals an FSAD pod uses day-to-day. Start here when you want to see how a skill actually runs — not just what it promises.

01 — Inside the Project Initialize-Context Skill

/project:initialize-context

This is how a project's context gets built. The skill explores your actual repos, classifies what it finds against platform defaults, and writes the docs. Click each phase.

Load the hierarchy

Skill reads the full context chain

  • Pick project (or auto-detect from path)
  • Read root CLAUDE.md → platform defaults
  • Read pod CLAUDE.md → shared architecture
  • Read project CLAUDE.md → current state
  • Assess what's populated vs. template stubs
The key concept: classification

Everything the skill finds gets classified against what the platform already defines:

conforms supplements overrides

Project docs never repeat what's in the parent docs. They only document what's specific to the project — additions and deviations.

Option A: deep repo exploration

Claude explores your actual code

Give it GitHub repo names. It launches parallel subagents that read package.json, tsconfig, component directories, state management imports, test configs, CI pipelines — and returns structured findings.

What repo exploration actually reads

Each repo gets its own subagent that reads:

package.json / *.csproj  → dependencies
src/ structure           → component organization
grep for @Global, Autoload, redux + UI + state
webpack.config / vite.config → build tooling
Dockerfile / CI.yml      → deployment
.storybook/              → design system presence
test configs             → testing patterns

Findings are merged across repos, deduplicated, and classified as conforms / supplements / overrides.

Option B: quick dump

Paste anything

Confluence pages, README excerpts, architecture notes, Slack threads. Claude structures and classifies against platform defaults.

What gets drafted 
architecture/README.md       → full architecture doc
design-standards/README.md   → UI patterns + conventions
CLAUDE.md architecture section → inline summary + diagram
CLAUDE.md design standards   → inline summary
Product overview + tech stack → if full setup mode
Architecture README includes

System overview and design principles. ASCII service diagram (auto-generated from findings). Per-service docs with repo links and tech stack. Data flow descriptions. External-dependencies table. Infrastructure and deployments. Cross-cutting concerns — classified as conforms, supplements, or overrides. Relevant ADR references.

Each section: Accept / Edit / Redo — same review loop as the spec skill.

Write & update
  • Writes architecture/README.md
  • Writes design-standards/README.md
  • Updates CLAUDE.md (targeted sections only)
  • Preserves existing content not in scope
  • Verifies all files after writing
Incremental by design

The skill detects which sections are already populated. Re-run it to update specific sections without overwriting previous work. A project's context builds up over time — each run adds to it.

Like the spec skill, it's a single markdown file in .claude/commands/. The entire project context initialization process — repo exploration, classification, drafting, writing — encoded in plain English.

02 — Inside the Project Create-Spec Skill

/project:create-spec

A skill is just a markdown file with instructions. Here's what this one does — click each phase to see the flow.

Regular context

The skill loads your world

  • Pick project
  • Pick pod
  • Read pod CLAUDE.md
  • Follow context chain upward
  • Read 2–3 existing specs for conventions
  • Detect author from git config
Why this matters

Before a single word is drafted, Claude has the full product context: architecture, services, design standards, platform fundamentals, and examples of how this pod writes specs. The skill does the context loading — the human doesn't have to.

Option A: brain dump

Paste anything

Meeting notes, Slack threads, Gong snippets, half-formed thoughts. Claude structures them into the spec template. You review section by section.

The key insight

Both paths converge on the same output — a structured spec with problem framing, competitive lens, use cases, architecture context, and acceptance criteria.

The skill's job is to solicit the right inputs and load the right context. Then it hands off to Claude's plan mode for the actual writing. We built the funnel. Claude does the work.

Option B: guided

Step-by-step questions

Claude walks through each section with targeted prompts. Probes for the right inputs: user analytics, customer feedback, competitive signals, success criteria.

Guided mode sections 
Problem / Description  → what + why inputs
Competitive Lens       → web search, pattern detection
Use Cases              → generated from context
Architecture           → auto-populated from CLAUDE.md
Preconditions          → flags, roles, regions
Acceptance Criteria    → 2–3 rounds of iteration
Future Scope           → planned / possible / out
Each section: review loop

Every section gets presented to you with three options:

looks good edit redo

The skill stays in the loop until you're satisfied. Competitive lens auto-searches the web. Architecture auto-populates from the context chain. You're directing — not writing.

Write & publish
  • Assembles full spec
  • Writes to pod/dir/specs/feature-name.md
  • Regenerates pod roadmap
  • Generates next steps (open PR, refine TODOs)
Then what?

Open a PR. Start coding. The spec and code evolve together from here. When the feature ships, clean up the spec as a record of what was actually built.

The entire skill is a single markdown file in .claude/commands/ — ~300 lines. It encodes the process in plain English. Anyone can read it, anyone can PR changes to it.

03 — Inside the Add-Task Skill

/add-task

A project-specific skill that encodes this repo's task-tracking convention — sequential numbering, todo.md entries, and the task-NNN.md template. Click each phase to walk the flow.

Read the tracker

Find the next task number

The skill opens markdown/to do/todo.md, scans every TASK-### already taken, and increments the highest by one. Always 3 digits with leading zeros.

TASK-037 → TASK-038
Capture the title

One-liner, no form

If the user ran /add-task <title>, that becomes the title. Otherwise the skill asks once and waits — no multi-field modal, no upfront ceremony.

Append the entry first

Single-line commit to the tracker

- [ ] `TASK-NNN` [Title] → [task-NNN.md](task-NNN.md)

Inserted before any Bug Details section, or at the end of the list. The entry lands before any details are gathered, so the task is already real.

One question at a time

No form-dumps on the user

The skill asks in sequence, and accepts "use your best judgment" as a valid answer at any step:

source summary assessment plan acceptance
Write the task file

Canonical template, every time

# TASK-NNN — [Task Title]

## Source
## Summary
## Assessment
**Location:** `[file path]`
## Plan
## Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] …
Why a template matters

Section names, em-dash in the heading, checkbox-style acceptance criteria — all the small formatting decisions that would otherwise drift across contributors. The skill encodes them once so every task file reads the same.

Location hint (**Location:**) keeps implementation grounded in a real file path from day one.

Report back

Three things, every time

  • The new task number
  • That the entry was added to todo.md
  • The path to the new task file
What this skill really is

Not a form. Not a tracker UI. Just a markdown file in .claude/skills/ that encodes one contributor's tribal knowledge — numbering scheme, filename conventions, interview order, section headings — so every future session produces identical output without anyone having to remember it.

Small skill, but it's the pattern: any repeatable workflow becomes a markdown file.

Interactive playground

Fill sample task inputs on the left, watch the generated todo.md entry and task-NNN.md file update live on the right, and copy a ready-to-paste prompt that invokes the skill.

Prefer a new tab? Open the playground standalone →

04 — Inside the Commit-Changes Skill

/commit-changes

The counterpart to /add-task. When a batch of tasks is done, this skill writes the CHANGELOG entry, bumps the version, and commits — in the exact sequence that keeps the artifact coherent.

Find the shipping set

Completed, but not yet in CHANGELOG

The skill reads markdown/to do/todo.md for items marked [x], then cross-references CHANGELOG.md to filter out anything already shipped. If the set is ambiguous — multiple newly-completed tasks, only some meant to go — it asks the user before writing a single line.

Detect the next version

Read the top of CHANGELOG

### vN — YYYY-MM-DD
↓
### v(N+1) — <today>

Explicit $ARGUMENTS (e.g., v24) override auto-increment. Today's date is pulled from context, never guessed.

Pull per-task summaries

For each shipping task, the skill opens task-NNN.md and extracts the title + 1–4 concrete, past-tense bullets. If a task file is missing or opaque, it stops and asks — rather than inventing changelog prose.

CHANGELOG.md — new block

Insert above the previous version

### vN — YYYY-MM-DD

**[Rollup line for multi-task batches]**

**[Task title] (TASK-NNN)**
- [Change bullet]
- [Change bullet]

Sub-tasks (e.g., TASK-005a) group under their parent. Single-task ships skip the rollup line.

README.md + HTML title

Two surgical edits

| Current version | v(N+1) |
| Date updated    | YYYY-MM-DD |

And the <title> tag in fsad-playbook.html — but only the live-version occurrences. Historical vN references in the playbook's own changelog section stay untouched.

Sanity-check the diff

Before anything is staged

git status
git diff --stat
CHANGELOG top renders clean README version matches no stray edits
Why verify before committing

The playbook is shared with engineering teams. A silent typo in the changelog or a version mismatch between README and HTML title ships to every reader and erodes trust in the artifact. The skill stops on any inconsistency instead of powering through.

Scoped stage — never -A

Only files the skill intentionally changed

git add CHANGELOG.md README.md \
        fsad-playbook.html \
        "markdown/to do/todo.md" \
        "markdown/to do/task-NNN.md"

markdown/ often contains WIP notes. Using git add -A would catch them by accident.

Commit, push, report

Match the project's message style

vN: <rollup> (TASK-NNN, TASK-NNN)

- TASK-NNN: <terse bullet>
- TASK-NNN: <terse bullet>

Then git push. If it's non-fast-forward, auth-fail, or hook-blocked — the skill stops and reports. No force-push, ever. Final message to the user: version shipped, tasks included, short SHA, push status.

Try it yourself

Interactive playground

Build a batch of shipping tasks on the left, watch the generated CHANGELOG.md block, README.md version rows, commit message, and shell commands update live on the right.

Prefer a new tab? Open the playground standalone →

Why these examples

Four markdown files.
The connective tissue of a working pod.

Each skill is a concrete answer to "what does agentic work actually look like?" — not frameworks, not diagrams. Just English instructions in a file, run by a pod: one builds the context every future agent inherits, one turns raw intent into a reviewable spec, one captures the rituals of adding new work, one ships the batch coherently. The rituals compound.

Full Stack Agentic Development

Workflows — example skills and rituals for FSAD pods.

Installable Skills for FSAD Pods

The fsd: Skill Library

Eleven installable skills for task management, code review, and security. Install once via the Claude Code plugin system; invoke anywhere with /fsd:<name>.

01 — Skills in this Playbook

The fsd: Skill Library

Eleven installable skills for FSAD pods — task management, code review, and security. Each skill lives in this repo's skills/ directory as a SKILL.md file. Install once via the Claude Code plugin system; invoke anywhere with /fsd:<name>.

Installation

Install via the Claude Code plugin system

claude plugin install https://github.com/tbeack/fsad_playbook

Skills install under the fsd: namespace. Invoke any skill with /fsd:<name>. The plugin exposes all skills to the current Claude Code session via the standard plugin cascade.

Workflow Management
/fsd:do-task <ID>

Plan or execute any task

Mode-switching skill: if no task file exists it interviews you and writes the plan; if a plan exists it implements it, verifies every acceptance criterion, and marks the task complete.

plan mode execute mode AC verification
/fsd:add-task [title]

Capture a new task

Auto-detects the current project, finds the next task number, and adds a numbered entry to the todo file — then interviews you one question at a time to write the task detail file.

auto-numbered one-at-a-time interview
/fsd:ship-it

Wrap up a finished batch

Four sequential phases: README check, CHANGELOG versioning, completed-task cleanup, then commit + push + PR. Run after do-task marks one or more tasks done.

version bump commit PR
/fsd:next

Pick the next open task and run it

Reads the project's todo file, finds the first unchecked item, and hands it straight to fsd:do-task. No arguments needed.

hands off to do-task
/fsd:sync

Pre-flight sync check

Fetches from the remote and reports local branch state before you start work. Handles all four states: up-to-date, ahead, behind (auto-pulls), and diverged (warns).

up-to-date ahead behind diverged
/fsd:ac <ID>

Verify acceptance criteria

Walks every unchecked AC in a task file, inspects the codebase for evidence, and marks each passing criterion immediately. Useful for re-running ACs after implementation or in a follow-up session.

progressive marks evidence-first
/fsd:estimate

Story point estimation

Estimates work items on a Fibonacci scale (1–21) using a three-factor rubric: complexity, effort, and risk. Accepts bullet lists, numbered lists, or free prose as input.

Fibonacci scale complexity + effort + risk
/fsd:init

Initialize a new project

Creates the standard FSAD folder structure, writes template files, and initializes a local + remote git repository — all in one shot from the current directory.

folder structure templates git repo
Review & Security
/fsd:code-review-team

Multi-agent code review

Dispatches 6 specialist reviewers in parallel (correctness, design, performance, maintainability, testing, API contract), then consolidates findings into a severity-ranked REVIEW-REPORT.md with a merge recommendation.

6 specialists REVIEW-REPORT.md
/fsd:sec-review-team

Multi-agent security review

Picks a specialist roster based on detected stack signals (webapp, IaC, LLM-agent, container, backend, etc.) from a library of 13 specialists. Produces a severity-ranked REPORT.md with deduped root issues.

13 specialists stack-signal detection REPORT.md
/fsd:sec-review-fixes

Apply security fix PRs

Companion to sec-review-team. Takes a REPORT.md or findings.jsonl and produces fix PRs per finding — each with a regression test. Safeguards: branch-only commits, no force-push, clean working tree required.

per-finding PRs regression tests
02 — Skill Definitions

Skill Definitions

Each card mirrors the live skill file under skills/ in the repo. Open a card to read the full skill source — click the pre block to copy it directly.

Workflow Management

/fsd:do-task <ID>

▼

Execute or plan a task in any of your local projects. Auto-detects the current project from the working directory, reads conventions from `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`, and either drafts a missing task plan (plan mode) or implements the existing one (execute mode). Always creates a task-detail file before executing — even for lightweight projects. Use when the user says "do CBP-087", "work on FSD-031", "execute task 12", or similar.

Definition file

---
description: Execute or plan a task in any of your local projects. Auto-detects the current project from the working directory, reads conventions from `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`, and either drafts a missing task plan (plan mode) or implements the existing one (execute mode). Always creates a task-detail file before executing — even for lightweight projects. Use when the user says "do CBP-087", "work on FSD-031", "execute task 12", or similar.
argument-hint: `<PREFIX-NNN | NNN> [PREFIX-NNN | NNN...]`
---

# fsd:do-task — multi-project task executor

You help the user make progress on a single task in any registered project. The skill is **mode-switching**:

- **Plan mode** — task entry exists in the todo file but no task-detail file exists yet. Draft the plan; stop.
- **Execute mode** — both the entry and the task file exist. Implement the plan, verify ACs, update CHANGELOG, mark complete.

A task-detail file is **always required** before executing — even for projects with `use_full_template: false`. Plan mode runs first, execute mode second.

## Step 0 — Detect the project

1. Determine the current working directory.
2. Read `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`.
3. Match cwd against each project's `match_paths` (expand `~`). Prefer the longest (most specific) match if multiple match.
4. If a project matches, refer to its entry as `cfg` and the resolved project root as `project_root`. Proceed.
5. If **no project matches**:
   - Tell the user the project is not registered.
   - Suggest running `/fsd:add-task` from the project to register it, or offer to register it now.
   - Stop.

## Step 0.5 — Multi-task dispatch

Split `$ARGUMENTS` on whitespace to get a list of tokens.

- If there is **only one token**: skip this step entirely. Continue to Step 1 with that single token as `$ARGUMENTS`.
- If there are **two or more tokens**:
  1. Tell the user: "Dispatching N tasks concurrently: TOKEN1, TOKEN2, …"
  2. Write an aggregate badge to the main project root so the statusline shows all dispatched IDs immediately. Use the absolute `project_root` path resolved in Step 0 (not the literal string, not a relative path) and quote it to handle spaces:
     ```
     echo "orange:TOKEN1+TOKEN2+…" > "{project_root}/.pmon-session-task"
     ```
  3. For each token, spawn one Agent using the `Agent` tool with `isolation: "worktree"` so each agent works on its own branch. Each agent's prompt must include:
     - The current working directory (project root).
     - The single task ID assigned to that agent.
     - The full instruction to run the complete `fsd:do-task` flow for that ID — entering plan mode if no task-detail file exists yet, or execute mode if it does. The agent must follow all steps (Step 0 through the relevant terminal step) exactly as this skill describes, operating on its one assigned ID only.
     - An explicit note: **do NOT write or delete `.pmon-session-task`** — badge ownership belongs to the orchestrator.
  4. Send **all Agent calls in a single message** so they run concurrently.
  5. After all agents return, print a summary table:
     ```
     TBS-012   plan mode    task file written — changes isolated in worktree branch
     TBS-013   execute mode  ACs verified, CHANGELOG updated, marked complete — changes isolated in worktree branch
     ```
     Then remove the badge file and tell the user: "Each agent's changes are in an isolated worktree branch. Run `fsd:ship-it` to merge them sequentially into your working tree before committing."
     ```
     rm -f "{project_root}/.pmon-session-task"
     ```
  6. **Stop here.** Do not continue to Step 1.

## Step 1 — Resolve the task identifier

Read `$ARGUMENTS` and normalize to canonical form `PREFIX-NNN`:

| Input | Canonical (CBP project) |
|-------|-------------------------|
| `CBP-087` | `CBP-087` |
| `cbp-87`  | `CBP-087` |
| `087`     | `CBP-087` |
| `87`      | `CBP-087` |

Rules:
- Prefix: `cfg.prefix` (preserve exact casing — e.g., `FSD_Train`, `KHB-Todo`).
- Number: zero-pad to `cfg.number_digits` digits.
- If `$ARGUMENTS` is empty, ask: `"Which [cfg.prefix] task? (e.g., [cfg.prefix]-001)"`

Do not guess. Do not pick "the next open one" without being asked.

## Step 2 — Verify the task exists in the tracker

Read `cfg.todo_file` (resolved relative to `project_root`). Find the line containing the canonical identifier.

- **Not found** — Tell the user the task isn't in the tracker. Suggest `/fsd:add-task [title]` to create it first. Stop.
- **Already checked (`- [x]`)** — Tell the user it's already marked complete. Ask whether to re-execute (rare). Default: stop.
- **Open (`- [ ]`)** — Continue.

## Step 3 — Branch on whether the task file exists

Resolve the task file path using the same token substitution as `add-task`:
- `{nnn}` = zero-padded number
- `{prefix_lower}` = `cfg.prefix_in_filename` if set, else `cfg.prefix` lowercased
- `{ID}` = canonical identifier
- `{slug}` = title lowercased, non-alphanumerics → `_`, collapsed, trimmed (only needed if template uses it)

```
{project_root}/{cfg.task_dir}/{rendered cfg.task_filename_template}
```

- **File missing** → go to Step 4 (Plan mode).
- **File exists** → read it end-to-end. If it has no `## Plan` section or no `## Acceptance Criteria`, treat it as incomplete and ask the user to confirm before overwriting. Otherwise, go to Step 5 (Execute mode).

State the mode before continuing: "Task file missing — entering plan mode." or "Task file found — entering execute mode."

## Step 4 — Plan mode: draft the workup

Ask questions **one at a time** (never dump a form):

1. **Source** — Where did this task come from? (skip if not applicable)
2. **Summary** — What needs to change and why? 1–3 sentences.
3. **Assessment** — Current state? Where does the relevant code/content live? Inspect the repo before asking — don't ask things you could verify yourself.
4. **Plan** — Step-by-step implementation. File paths and line numbers where possible. Group into phases if non-trivial.
5. **Acceptance Criteria** — `- [ ]` checkboxes. Falsifiable ("button renders at 44px touch target on mobile"), not vague ("looks right").

If the user says "use your best judgment" or "fill it in", proceed without blocking — infer from the title and project context.

Write the task file at the resolved path:

```markdown
# {ID} — {title}

## Source
[Where this came from — or omit this section if not applicable]

## Summary
[1–3 sentences: what's changing and why]

## Assessment
[Current state. Does it exist? Where? What needs to change?]

**Location:** `[file path]` — [section/line reference]

## Plan

1. [Step one]
2. [Step two]

## Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] [Verification step 1]
- [ ] [Verification step 2]
```

Then update the todo entry to link the new task file. Match the existing linked-entry style in `cfg.todo_file` (em-dash, backtick-wrapped identifier, parenthesized link). Use `Edit` with unique surrounding context. Do not rewrite the whole file.

If `cfg.notes` mentions style cues (e.g. "reference task-cbp-030.md"), peek at that file first and match its tone.

**Stop here.** Tell the user the plan is ready and that they can re-invoke `/fsd:do-task {ID}` to execute it. Do **not** start implementing.

## Step 5 — Execute mode: implement the plan

### 5a. Review critically before touching anything

Read the task file end-to-end. Identify concerns — ambiguous steps, missing context, factual claims about the codebase that no longer hold (file paths moved, exports renamed, etc.). Raise anything unclear before starting. Do not guess.

### 5b. Build a working task list

Before creating any tasks, write the session badge so the task-id appears as an orange badge in the Claude Code statusline. Use the absolute `project_root` path resolved in Step 0 — not the literal string `{project_root}` and not a relative path. Quote the path to handle directory names with spaces:

```bash
echo "orange:{taskId}" > "{project_root}/.pmon-session-task"
```

Then use `TaskCreate` to add one task per phase or major step in the plan. Mark each `in_progress` before starting and `completed` when done — don't batch.

At step 5i (hand off), clean up the tag file using the same absolute path:

```bash
rm -f "{project_root}/.pmon-session-task"
```

### 5c. Sync and create worktree

Before touching any files, ensure the branch is up to date and create an isolated worktree:

1. **Sync check** — run `git fetch origin` then `git status -uno` to determine the local branch's state relative to the remote:
   - **Up to date or ahead** — proceed.
   - **Behind** — run `git pull --ff-only`. If the pull fails (uncommitted changes, non-fast-forward), stop and ask the user to resolve before continuing.
   - **Diverged** — stop. Warn the user and do not create the worktree.

2. **Create worktree** — call `EnterWorktree` with `name` set to the task ID slugified (e.g., `task-tbs-024`). This creates a new branch (`worktree-task-tbs-024`) inside `.claude/worktrees/` and switches the session into it. All file writes from this point forward happen inside the worktree — not in the main working tree.

Note the worktree branch name; report it in the handoff (Step 5i).

### 5d. Implement the plan steps

Follow the plan's steps in order. If the plan turns out to be wrong, **stop and re-plan with the user** rather than silently deviating. Authorization stands for the scope specified, not beyond.

### 5e. Verify acceptance criteria one-by-one

Walk through every AC in the task file and prove it with evidence (file location, output, browser inspection, etc.). Print a verdict per AC: PASS with evidence, or FAIL with what broke.

As each AC passes, edit the task file to flip `- [ ]` to `- [x]`. **Mark progressively** — a partial failure leaves an honest record.

Use enough surrounding AC text in `old_string` to make `Edit` matches unambiguous. For ≥3 ACs verified together at the end of a phase, replacing the entire AC block in one `Edit` is acceptable — provided you proved each individually.

When all ACs are `[x]`, add a note immediately above the AC list:
```
All criteria verified YYYY-MM-DD before commit.
```

If any AC fails, stop and report. Never edit AC text to make it pass.

### 5f. Optional code review

All ACs have passed. Ask the user exactly once:

> "All acceptance criteria passed. Would you like to run `/fsd:code-review-team` on this diff before wrapping up?"

- **Yes** — Invoke the `fsd:code-review-team` skill (via `Skill` tool). Wait for it to complete. The skill writes `REVIEW-REPORT.md`; note any critical findings in the handoff message. Then continue to 5g.
- **No / no response** — Skip directly to 5g. Do not run the review.

Do not run the review without explicit user confirmation.

### 5g. Update CHANGELOG and version

**Locate the CHANGELOG:**
1. Check `cfg.changelog_file` in the YAML (if set, resolve relative to `project_root`). Otherwise look for `CHANGELOG.md` at `project_root`.
2. If no CHANGELOG exists, **create `{project_root}/CHANGELOG.md`** using Keep-a-Changelog format with an `## [Unreleased]` header and `### Added` / `### Changed` / `### Fixed` subsections.

**Detect the versioning scheme:**
1. Check `cfg.version_files` in the YAML (if set) — these are the authoritative version locations, relative to `project_root`.
2. If not set, scan `project_root` for common version carriers: `package.json` (`.version`), `plugin.json` / `plugin/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` (`.version`), `*.html` (version string in `<title>`), `README.md` (version badge or header line).
3. If a scheme is found but not configured in the YAML, show the user what you found and ask for confirmation before bumping.
4. If **no versioning is in use**, propose a scheme before writing anything. Default recommendation: **semver (`v1.0.0`)** for software; **CalVer (`YYYY.MM.DD`)** for documentation-only projects. Explain the tradeoff (semver = intent-driven, CalVer = timeline-driven) and ask the user to choose.

**Write the CHANGELOG entry:**
- If the task plan specifies CHANGELOG content, write it exactly as specified.
- If not, propose a one-paragraph entry (placed above the most recent version block) and wait for user approval before writing.
- Match the existing format in the file. For projects using integer versioning (e.g. `v36`), continue that pattern.

**Bump the version:**
- Only bump when the task plan explicitly calls for it.
- When bumping, update every file identified by `cfg.version_files` (or the scan). Keep all sources aligned — never leave one file on the old version.
- Apply the project's bump type (patch/minor/major for semver; integer increment for vNN; new date for CalVer) as specified in the plan or by the user.
- If the plan does not call for a version bump, skip it and note that in the handoff message.

### 5h. Mark the task complete in the todo file

> **Write directly to the main working tree.** Use the absolute path `{project_root}/{cfg.todo_file}` — not a relative path. The CWD is the worktree; a relative path would write to the worktree copy and create a merge conflict at ship time. The `[x]` mark is intentionally committed from main, not included in the worktree branch.

Change the entry's `- [ ]` to `- [x]` at `{project_root}/{cfg.todo_file}`. Use `Edit` with unique surrounding context. Do not reorder lines or touch other entries.

### 5i. Hand off — do not auto-commit

Call `ExitWorktree` with `action: "keep"` to return the session to the original working directory while leaving the worktree branch intact on disk.

Open the handoff message with a bold header on its own line:

```
**{ID} — {title}**
```

Then provide a concise summary covering: what was implemented, which ACs were verified, which files changed, whether the CHANGELOG was updated, whether the todo entry was marked done, whether a version was bumped (or why it was skipped), whether the code review was run (e.g., "Code review report written to `REVIEW-REPORT.md`") or skipped. Include the worktree branch name (e.g., `worktree-task-tbs-024`) and tell the user their changes are isolated on that branch. Suggest running `fsd:ship-it` (or `git merge <branch>`) to bring the changes into the main branch before pushing. **Wait for the user to say "commit"** before doing so.

## Conventions to honour

- **Identifier format:** always canonical `PREFIX-NNN` (zero-padded, exact prefix casing) in messages, file names, and edits.
- **Task file location:** `{project_root}/{cfg.task_dir}/{cfg.task_filename_template}` — even for lightweight projects.
- **Heading separator:** em-dash (`—`), not hyphen.
- **AC checkbox style:** `- [ ]` / `- [x]`.
- **One question at a time** in plan mode.
- **Trust `cfg.notes`** — if it calls out style cues, honour them.

## Guardrails

- **Always read the todo file first** — never infer task state from memory.
- **Always create a task file before executing** — plan mode first, execute mode second; no exceptions for lightweight projects.
- **Don't switch tasks mid-flow** — re-invoke with the new identifier if the user changes their mind.
- **Plan mode never writes code** — only the task file and the todo link update.
- **Execute mode never edits the plan to match the implementation** — if reality drifts, stop and re-plan.
- **Never mark an AC complete without evidence.**
- **Mark ACs progressively, not in a batch.**
- **Don't propose CHANGELOG content not anticipated by the plan without asking.** Surprise changelog churn is hard to undo.
- **Don't bump versions opportunistically** — only when the plan explicitly calls for it. When bumping, keep all version sources aligned.
- **Don't auto-commit or push.** The user owns the release decision.
- **Don't run destructive git operations** at any point.
- **Always enter a worktree in single-task execute mode** — call `EnterWorktree` in Step 5c before any file modifications. Never modify the main working tree directly during execution.

/fsd:add-task [title]

▼

Add a new task to any of your local projects. Auto-detects the current project (fsad_playbook, fsad_training, hangman, KHB, fsd, etc.) from the working directory and conforms to that project's conventions — prefix, numbering, todo-file layout, task-file template. Reads conventions from `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`. Use when the user wants to add a task, capture a TODO, or track a new idea in a local project.

Definition file

---
description: Add a new task to any of your local projects. Auto-detects the current project (fsad_playbook, fsad_training, hangman, KHB, fsd, etc.) from the working directory and conforms to that project's conventions — prefix, numbering, todo-file layout, task-file template. Reads conventions from `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`. Use when the user wants to add a task, capture a TODO, or track a new idea in a local project.
argument-hint: `[brief task title]`
---

# fsd:add-task — multi-project task adder

You add a new task entry (and, where the project warrants it, a per-task detail file) in whichever local project the user is currently working in. Project-specific conventions are defined in `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml` — always read that file before doing anything else.

`$ARGUMENTS` (if present) is the brief task title.

## Step 0 — Detect the project

1. Determine the current working directory.
2. Read `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`.
3. Match cwd against each project's `match_paths` (expand `~`). The cwd may be a descendant of a match path. If multiple match, prefer the longest (most specific) match.
4. If a project matches, refer to its entry as `cfg` and proceed.
5. If **no** project matches:
   - Tell the user the project is not registered.
   - Show the values you'd use from the `default` block.
   - Ask whether to (a) proceed using the defaults this once, (b) register the project now, or (c) abort.
   - If (b), collect the following fields one at a time, showing the suggested default for each:
     - `name` — project key in the YAML (e.g. `my_project`)
     - `match_paths` — root path (default: current working directory)
     - `prefix` — task identifier prefix (e.g. `MYP`)
     - `number_digits` — zero-pad width (default: `3`)
     - `todo_file` — relative path to the todo file (default: `planning/to do/todo.md`)
     - `task_dir` — relative path to the task detail directory (default: `planning/to do`)
     - `use_full_template` — whether to create per-task detail files (default: `true`)
     - `version_scheme` — versioning convention: `semver`, `integer`, `calver`, or `none` (default: `semver`)
     - `version_files` — file(s) that carry the version string (default: `README.md`; user may specify `package.json` or others)
   - Write all collected fields as a new entry under `projects:` in the YAML before continuing.

## Step 1 — Compute the next number

1. Read `cfg.todo_file` (resolved relative to the matched project root).
2. **Clean up empty placeholder entries** — scan for lines that match the pattern `- [ ] \`{prefix}-NNN\`` where the title portion is empty (nothing or only whitespace after the closing backtick). These are artifacts left by the `init` template. If any are found, remove them from the file using `Edit` before proceeding. If the removed entry was the only one in the file (no real tasks yet), treat the file as empty and start numbering from 1 — so the first real task gets 001.
3. Find every line containing the prefix and extract the highest number.
4. Increment by 1; zero-pad to `cfg.number_digits` digits → `nnn`.
5. Build:
   - `ID = "{prefix}-{nnn}"` (e.g. `CBP-031`, `KHB-Todo-0010`, `FSD_Train-012`).
   - `prefix_lower` = `cfg.prefix_in_filename` if set, otherwise the prefix lowercased.
   - `slug` (only if the filename template uses it) = title lowercased, non-alphanumerics → `_`, collapsed runs of `_`, trimmed.

## Step 2 — Get the title

If `$ARGUMENTS` is non-empty, use it. Otherwise ask:
> What's the task title? (one-liner — I'll prompt for details next.)

Before adding, scan the todo file for an existing entry with a similar title. If one exists, surface it and ask whether to proceed, edit the existing one, or abandon.

## Step 3 — Add the bullet to the todo file

Render `cfg.todo_entry_template` by substituting `{ID}`, `{title}`, `{nnn}`, `{prefix_lower}`.

Insertion point:
- If `cfg.insert_before_section` is set → insert the bullet immediately before that heading line.
- Else if `cfg.insert_under_section` is set → insert as the last bullet of that section (just before the next heading or the end of the file, whichever comes first).
- Else → append after the last existing bullet in the top-level list.

Use the `Edit` tool with enough surrounding context to be unique. Don't rewrite the whole file.

## Step 4 — Gather task details

Skip this step if `cfg.use_full_template` is `false`.

Ask one question at a time (don't dump a form):

1. **Source** — Where did this task come from? (release note, feedback, bug report, own idea). Omit the section if not applicable.
2. **Summary** — What's changing and why? 1-3 sentences.
3. **Assessment** — Current state? Where does the relevant content live? File paths and line numbers if you have them.
4. **Plan** — Step-by-step implementation.
5. **Acceptance Criteria** — `- [ ]` checkbox list of verification steps.

If the user says "use your best judgment", "fill it in", or similar, proceed without blocking — infer from the title and project context.

## Step 5 — Write the task-detail file

Skip this step if `cfg.use_full_template` is `false`.

Resolve the path:

```
{project_root}/{cfg.task_dir}/{rendered cfg.task_filename_template}
```

Write this content (em-dash, not hyphen, in the H1):

```markdown
# {ID} — {title}

## Source
[Where this came from — or omit this section if not applicable]

## Summary
[1-3 sentences: what's changing and why]

## Assessment
[Current state of the relevant content. Does it exist? Where? What needs to change?]

**Location:** `[file path]` — [section/line reference]

## Plan

[Step-by-step implementation]

1. [Step one]
2. [Step two]

## Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] [Verification step 1]
- [ ] [Verification step 2]
```

If `cfg.notes` mentions style cues (e.g. "reference task-cbp-030.md"), peek at that file first and match its tone.

## Step 6 — Confirm completion

In one short response, report:
- The project that was matched (and its root path).
- The new `ID`.
- That the bullet was appended to `cfg.todo_file`.
- The task-detail file path (if one was created).


## Conventions to honour

- **Number format:** always zero-pad to `cfg.number_digits` digits.
- **Filename casing:** lowercase the prefix unless `cfg.prefix_in_filename` overrides.
- **Heading separator:** em-dash (`—`), not hyphen.
- **Identifier casing in IDs:** preserve `cfg.prefix` exactly (e.g. `FSD_Train-012`, `KHB-Todo-0010`).
- **One question at a time** when gathering details.

## Guardrails

- **Always read the todo file first** — never guess the next number.
- **Never duplicate titles** — scan existing entries before creating.
- **Trust the file over the cfg.** If the file's existing entries clearly use a different format than `cfg.todo_entry_template`, follow the file and flag the drift to the user (the cfg may be stale).
- **Don't modify other projects.** Only touch the matched project's files.
- **Don't implement the task.** Only create planning artifacts; the user will do the work separately.
- **Don't add a task-detail file when `cfg.use_full_template` is false.** Lightweight projects (e.g. hangman) intentionally keep the bullet alone.

/fsd:ship-it

▼

Wrap up a finished round of work — verify README and CHANGELOG are current, cut a version if warranted, move completed task files into the completed/ folder, then create a feature branch if needed, commit, push, and open a PR. Run after `/fsd:do-task` has marked one or more tasks done. Takes no arguments.

Also available as /fsd:ship (alias — same behaviour, shorter to type).

Definition file

---
description: Wrap up a finished round of work — verify README and CHANGELOG are current, cut a version if warranted, move completed task files into the completed/ folder, then create a feature branch if needed, commit, push, and open a PR. Run after `/fsd:do-task` has marked one or more tasks done. Takes no arguments.
---

# fsd:ship-it — wrap up and ship

You run four sequential phases to close out a batch of completed work: README check, CHANGELOG versioning, completed-task file cleanup, and commit + push + PR. Do not skip phases or reorder them.

## Step 0 — Detect the project

1. Determine the current working directory.
2. Read `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`.
3. Match cwd against each project's `match_paths` (expand `~`). Prefer the longest (most specific) match if multiple match.
4. If a project matches, refer to its entry as `cfg` and the resolved project root as `project_root`. Proceed.
5. If **no project matches**:
   - Tell the user the project is not registered.
   - Suggest running `/fsd:add-task` from the project to register it.
   - Stop.

## Step 0.5 — Merge pending worktrees

1. Run `git worktree list --porcelain` from `project_root`. Parse the output to get all worktrees (each block starts with `worktree <path>`).
2. Filter out the main worktree — the one whose path equals `project_root`.
3. If **no additional worktrees** exist: skip this step entirely and proceed to Step 1.
4. If additional worktrees exist:
   - List them for the user: path and branch name (the `branch` field from the porcelain output).
   - Ask: "Merge these worktree branches sequentially into the main worktree? (y/n)"
   - If **no**: leave them in place and proceed to Step 1.
   - If **yes**: for each worktree in order:
     a. Check for uncommitted changes: `git -C <path> status --porcelain`. If any are present, commit them: `git -C <path> add -A && git -C <path> commit -m "<branch-name>: parallel do-task changes"`.
     b. Merge the branch into the main worktree: `git merge --no-ff <branch>`.
     c. If the merge produces a conflict: **stop immediately**. Tell the user which file(s) conflict. Ask them to resolve the conflicts and run `git commit` before re-running `fsd:ship-it`. Do not proceed to Step 1.
     d. On clean merge: remove the worktree (`git worktree remove --force <path>`) and delete the branch (`git branch -d <branch>`).
5. After all worktrees are merged and removed: tell the user "Worktrees merged." and proceed to Step 1.

## Step 1 — Pre-flight: README check

1. List all subdirectories in `{project_root}/skills/`. Each directory name is a skill.
2. Read `{project_root}/README.md`.
3. For every skill directory, check that a corresponding row exists in the README skills table (match on the directory name).
4. If any skill is **missing** from the README:
   - Tell the user which skills are absent (e.g. `ship-it`, `next`).
   - Ask them to update the README before continuing.
   - Stop. Do not proceed to Step 2 until the user says it's done and re-invokes.
5. If the README is current: tell the user "README check passed." and proceed.

## Step 2 — Pre-flight: CHANGELOG + version

1. Read `{project_root}/CHANGELOG.md`.
2. Find the `## [Unreleased]` section. Collect everything between that heading and the next `## [` heading — that is the pending content.
3. If the `[Unreleased]` section is **empty** (no bullet points or subsections):
   - Tell the user: "The [Unreleased] section has no content — there may be nothing to release. Proceed anyway? (y/n)"
   - If the user says no: stop.
   - If the user says yes: skip the version-bump sub-steps and go to Step 3.
4. If the `[Unreleased]` section **has content**:
   - Show the user the pending content.
   - **2a — Read version config:** Read `cfg.version_scheme` and `cfg.version_files` from the project config (loaded in Step 0).
   - **2b — Derive current version:** If `cfg.version_files` is set, read the first file in the list and extract the current version string:
     - JSON file (e.g., `package.json`, `plugin.json`): read the `"version"` field.
     - `README.md`: find the version table row (e.g., `| **Current version** | v3.2.0 |`).
     - HTML file: find the version string in the `<title>` tag.
   - **2c — Propose next version** based on `cfg.version_scheme`:
     - `semver`: parse the current version and bump the **patch** component by default (e.g., `v3.2.0 → v3.2.1`). Tell the user they can type `minor` or `major` to request a different bump level instead.
     - `integer`: increment the integer suffix by 1 (e.g., `v36 → v37`).
     - `calver`: use today's date in `YYYY.MM.DD` format.
     - **No `version_scheme` configured:** ask the user: "Cut a new version? If yes, what tag? (e.g. v4)" and use whatever they provide.
   - **2d — Confirm:** Present: "Suggested version: **[tag]** — confirm, type a different tag, or type `no` to skip."
     - If the user says `no` or skips: proceed to Step 3 without modifying the CHANGELOG or version files.
     - If the user says `minor` or `major` (semver only): recompute the tag at that level and re-present 2d.
     - If the user confirms or provides a different tag: use that tag and continue.
   - **2e — Write version files:** Update every file in `cfg.version_files` with the new version string, preserving the format already in each file:
     - JSON: update the `"version"` field value in-place (string replacement, preserve surrounding formatting).
     - `README.md` version table: replace the old version string in the table row only.
     - HTML `<title>`: replace the old version string in the title tag only.
   - **2f — Update CHANGELOG:**
     - Replace the `## [Unreleased]` heading with `## [vN] — YYYY-MM-DD` using today's date.
     - Insert a fresh empty `## [Unreleased]` block above it so future entries have a home.
     - Write the updated file.
     - Tell the user: "CHANGELOG updated — [vN] cut."

## Step 3 — Move completed task files

1. Scan `{project_root}/{cfg.task_dir}/` for files matching the task filename pattern (e.g. `task-tbs-NNN.md`) that are **not** already inside the `completed/` subdirectory.
2. For each file found, look up the corresponding task identifier in `cfg.todo_file`.
   - If the entry is `- [x]` (done): mark the file as a candidate to move.
   - If the entry is `- [ ]` (still open) or not found: leave the file in place.
3. If **no candidates** exist: tell the user "No completed task files to move." and skip to Step 4.
4. If candidates exist:
   - List them for the user.
   - Ask: "Move these to `completed/`? (y/n)"
   - If yes: `git mv` each file into `{project_root}/{cfg.task_dir}/completed/`.
   - If no: leave them and proceed.

## Step 4 — Commit, push, PR

### 4.0 — Branch guard

1. Run `git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD` to get the current branch name.
2. If the branch is `main` or `master`:
   - Propose a feature branch name:
     - If a version was cut in Step 2: suggest `release/{version}` (e.g. `release/v4`).
     - Otherwise: suggest `task/{task-ids}` using any task IDs marked done during this session (e.g. `task/tbs-007`), or `ship/{YYYY-MM-DD}` if no IDs are in scope.
   - Ask the user: "Create and switch to `{branch}`? (y/n or type a different name)"
   - On confirmation: run `git checkout -b {branch}`.
3. If already on a non-default branch:
   - Tell the user "Already on branch `{branch}`."
   - If a version was cut in Step 2 **and** the current branch matches the pattern `release/vX.Y.Z` **and** `vX.Y.Z` does not equal the newly cut version:
     - Propose renaming: "Branch name `{branch}` is stale — rename to `release/{version}`? (y/n)"
     - If yes:
       a. `git branch -m {branch} release/{version}` — rename locally.
       b. `git push origin release/{version}` — push the new name.
       c. `git push origin --delete {branch}` — delete the old remote branch (ignore error if it didn't exist remotely).
       d. `git branch -u origin/release/{version}` — update tracking ref.
       e. Tell the user "Branch renamed to `release/{version}`."
     - If no: continue on the current branch name.
   - Otherwise: continue without renaming.

1. Run `git status` to see what's staged and unstaged. Stage all relevant changed files by name (do **not** use `git add -A` or `git add .`):
   - Modified: `CHANGELOG.md`, `README.md`, todo file, any other files changed during this work session.
   - Moved task files (already staged by `git mv`).
2. Run `git log --oneline -5` to read recent commit message style.
3. Draft a commit message:
   - If a version was cut in Step 2: use `release [vN] — <one-line summary of what's in the release>`.
   - If no version was cut: summarize the batch of completed work in one line.
   - Append: `Co-Authored-By: Claude Sonnet 4.6 <noreply@anthropic.com>`
   - Pass via heredoc to avoid shell quoting issues.
4. Show the user the draft commit message and ask for confirmation before committing.
5. On confirmation: create the commit.
6. Push: `git push -u origin HEAD`.
7. Create PR with `gh pr create`:
   - Detect the default branch: `git remote show origin | grep 'HEAD branch' | awk '{print $NF}'` (typically `main` or `master`).
   - Title: same one-liner used in the commit.
   - Include `--base {default_branch}` so the PR always targets the default branch.
   - Body (heredoc):
     ```
     ## Summary
     <CHANGELOG content for this version, or a bullet-list summary if no version was cut>

     ## Test plan
     - [ ] Skill files deployed and appear in `/fsd:*` command list
     - [ ] README skills table is accurate
     - [ ] CHANGELOG entry is present and correctly formatted

     🤖 Generated with [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code)
     ```
8. Return the PR URL to the user.

/fsd:next

▼

Pick the next open task from the current project's todo file and invoke `/fsd:do-task` with that task ID. Auto-detects the current project from the working directory using the same YAML config as other fsd skills. Use when the user says "do the next task", "what's next", "next task", or similar.

Definition file

---
description: Pick the next open task from the current project's todo file and invoke `/fsd:do-task` with that task ID. Auto-detects the current project from the working directory using the same YAML config as other fsd skills. Use when the user says "do the next task", "what's next", "next task", or similar.
---

# fsd:next — pick the next open task and hand off to do-task

You find the first unchecked task in the current project's todo file and invoke `fsd:do-task` with that task ID. There are no arguments.

## Step 0 — Detect the project

1. Determine the current working directory.
2. Read `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`.
3. Match cwd against each project's `match_paths` (expand `~`). Prefer the longest (most specific) match if multiple match.
4. If a project matches, refer to its entry as `cfg` and the resolved project root as `project_root`. Proceed.
5. If **no project matches**:
   - Tell the user the project is not registered.
   - Suggest running `/fsd:add-task` from the project to register it.
   - Stop.

## Step 1 — Find the next open task

Read `cfg.todo_file` (resolved relative to `project_root`).

Scan the lines top-to-bottom. Find the **first** line that:
- Starts with `- [ ]` (unchecked), and
- Contains the project's `cfg.prefix` (e.g. `TBS`, `CBP`, `FSD`)

Extract the full canonical task identifier from that line (e.g. `TBS-006`).

## Step 2 — No open tasks

If no matching unchecked line is found, tell the user:

> No open tasks found in `[cfg.todo_file]`. All done!

Then stop.

## Step 3 — Hand off to do-task

Tell the user which task was selected, e.g.:

> Next task: **TBS-006** — "Add a new skill `fsd:next`". Handing off to `fsd:do-task`…

Then invoke `fsd:do-task` via the Skill tool, passing the canonical task identifier as the argument.

/fsd:sync

▼

Pre-flight sync check for registered projects. Fetches from remote, reports uncommitted changes, and handles four states — up-to-date, ahead, behind (warns + pulls), or diverged (warns, no pull). Supports single-project (cwd detection), named-project (fsd:sync key1 key2), and full-sync (fsd:sync ALL) modes.

Definition file

---
description: Pre-flight sync check for registered projects. Fetches from remote, reports uncommitted changes, and handles four states — up-to-date, ahead, behind (warns + pulls), or diverged (warns, no pull). Supports single-project (cwd detection), named-project (fsd:sync key1 key2), and full-sync (fsd:sync ALL) modes.
---

# fsd:sync — pre-flight sync check

Run this before starting any work session to confirm your local repo(s) are in sync with the remote.

**Invocation:**
- `fsd:sync` — sync the project for the current working directory
- `fsd:sync ALL` — sync every project in `projects.yaml`
- `fsd:sync <key1> <key2> …` — sync one or more named projects by YAML key

## Step 0 — Parse arguments

1. Inspect the arguments passed to the skill.
2. **No arguments** → single-project mode. Proceed to Step 1 to detect the project from cwd.
3. **Single argument `ALL`** (case-insensitive) → multi-project mode. Load `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`. For every project key, resolve `match_paths[0]` (expand `~`) as that project's `project_root`. Build the full project list. Proceed to Step 2.
4. **One or more project keys** → multi-project mode. Load `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`. For each key:
   - If found: add `(key, expand(match_paths[0]))` to the list.
   - If **not found**: print `Unknown project: '<key>' — skipping.` and continue.
   - If all keys are unknown (list is empty after skipping): print `No valid projects to sync.` and stop.
   Proceed to Step 2 with the resolved list.

## Step 1 — Detect the project (single-project mode only)

1. Determine the current working directory.
2. Read `~/.claude/commands/fsd/projects.yaml`.
3. Match cwd against each project's `match_paths` (expand `~`). Prefer the longest match.
4. If a project matches, note `project_root`. Build a one-item list: `[(matched_key, project_root)]`. Proceed to Step 2.
5. If **no project matches**: use cwd as `project_root` — sync is useful in any git repo. Build a one-item list: `[("(unregistered)", cwd)]`. Proceed to Step 2.

## Step 2 — Sync each project in the list

For each `(project_key, project_root)` in the list:
- If there is **more than one project**, print a separator header before each:
  ```
  ─── Syncing <project_key> (<project_root>) ───
  ```
- Run Steps 3–6 for this project.
- Collect the one-line status result from Step 6 for the final summary.

## Step 3 — Fetch

Run `git fetch` from `project_root`.

If `git fetch` fails (no remote, no network): record `✗ Fetch failed — <error>` for this project, skip Steps 4–6 for this project, and continue to the next project.

## Step 4 — Branch name check

1. Run `git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD` to get the current branch name.
2. If the branch matches the pattern `release/vX.Y.Z` (starts with `release/v` followed by a version string):
   a. Read the project version from `cfg.version_files` (first file). If the project is unregistered or has no `version_files`, skip the rest of this step.
   b. Derive the expected branch name from `cfg.release_branch_pattern` (default: `release/{version}`).
   c. If the local branch name does not equal the expected name, warn:
      > "Branch `<local>` is stale — current version is `<version>`, expected `<expected>`. Run `/fsd:ship` to rename it."
   d. Run `git ls-remote --heads origin <expected>` to check remote presence.
      - Not found: note "Expected branch `<expected>` has not been pushed to the remote yet."
      - Found and name is already correct: no action.
3. If the branch does not match `release/vX.Y.Z`: skip this step entirely.

## Step 5 — Check uncommitted changes

Run `git status --porcelain` from `project_root`.

If output is non-empty: list the dirty files, prefixed with "Uncommitted changes detected:". Informational only — do not stop.

## Step 6 — Compare local vs remote

```bash
git rev-list --count HEAD..@{u}   # commits behind remote
git rev-list --count @{u}..HEAD   # commits ahead of remote
```

| State | behind | ahead | Action |
|-------|--------|-------|--------|
| **Up-to-date** | 0 | 0 | Report "Repo is up to date with remote." |
| **Behind** | > 0 | 0 | Warn: "Local is N commit(s) behind remote." → run `git pull`. Confirm "Pulled N commit(s). Repo is now up to date." |
| **Ahead** | 0 | > 0 | Report "Local is N commit(s) ahead of remote — ready to push." |
| **Diverged** | > 0 | > 0 | Warn: "Local and remote have diverged (N behind, M ahead). Manual resolution required — do not start work until resolved." Do NOT pull. Tell the user to run `git pull --rebase` or `git merge origin/<branch>`. |

## Step 7 — Print summary
