08 — How to Get Started

From Zero to Spec-Driven Development

Six steps to adopt FSAD on your next feature. Each step builds on the previous one.

1

Start in Planning Mode

Use existing Figma mockups and PRDs as initial context. Feed Claude your existing documentation before writing new specs.

claude --conversation-mode plan
Tip

Plan mode is read-only — Claude can analyze your codebase and help write specs without modifying any files.

2

Create spec.md

Iterate with Claude until the spec covers everything needed to build. Include user stories, acceptance criteria, technical constraints, and UX decisions.

# Feature: [Name]

## Problem Statement
What problem are we solving and for whom?

## User Stories
- As a [role], I want [action] so that [outcome]

## Technical Spec
- Data model, API contracts, performance requirements

## UX Decisions
- Component choices, interaction patterns, responsive behavior

## Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] Criterion 1 — testable, specific
- [ ] Criterion 2 — measurable outcome
3

Build roadmap.md

Organize implementation into phases. Track what's done, what's next, and what's blocked.

# Roadmap

## Phase 1: Foundation ✅
- [x] Database schema
- [x] API scaffolding

## Phase 2: Core Feature 🔄
- [x] Backend logic
- [ ] Frontend components
- [ ] Integration tests

## Phase 3: Polish
- [ ] Error handling
- [ ] Loading states
- [ ] Accessibility audit
4

Build state.md

Track current project state for session resumption. What's been built, what decisions were made, current blockers.

# Project State

## Current Phase: 2 — Core Feature
## Status: In Progress

## Completed
- Database schema with migrations
- REST API endpoints (CRUD)

## In Progress
- Frontend form components

## Key Decisions
- Chose Zustand over Redux for state management
- Using server-side validation only (no client dupe)

## Blockers
- Waiting on design review for mobile layout
5

Break Into Phases

Each phase should fit within a context window. One phase = one focused area of work. If a phase feels too big, split it.

Tip

Context window limits are real. A phase that tries to do too much will result in agents losing track of requirements. Keep phases focused and atomic.

6

Create architecture.md

Describe overall architecture and tech stack. Include system diagram, data flow, and key dependencies.

Client (React) ──▶ API Gateway (Express) ──▶ Auth Service
                                           ──▶ Core Service ──▶ PostgreSQL
                                                             ──▶ Redis Cache
                                                             ──▶ Job Queue (Bull)
Best Practice

The document-driven workflow means your specs, roadmap, and state files ARE your project management system. No separate tool needed. Everything is version-controlled, diffable, and readable by both humans and agents.

09 — Anatomy of a Claude Code Project

Claude Needs 4 Things

The why, the map, the rules, the workflows. Structure is permanent; prompting is temporary.

Key Insight

Prompting is temporary. Structure is permanent. A well-organized project gives Claude context automatically — every session, every agent, every teammate.

Pillar 1: CLAUDE.md = Repo Memory

▼

Contains WHY (purpose), WHAT (repo map), HOW (rules + commands). Claude reads this file automatically at the start of every session. Keep it short — if too long, the model misses important context.

# CLAUDE.md

## Project Overview
E-commerce API — Node.js + PostgreSQL. Handles product catalog,
orders, and payments.

## Tech Stack
- Runtime: Node.js 20, TypeScript
- Database: PostgreSQL 15 + Prisma ORM
- Testing: Vitest, coverage > 80%

## Commands
- `npm run dev` — start dev server
- `npm test` — run tests
- `npm run lint` — lint + format

## Rules
- Never modify migration files after they've been applied
- All API endpoints require authentication middleware
- Use Zod for request validation, not manual checks
Tip

Use /init to auto-generate a starting CLAUDE.md from your existing project. Keep it under 100 lines.

Example: A Production CLAUDE.md

▼

A real-world CLAUDE.md covering workflow orchestration, task management, and core principles. Copy and adapt for your team.

## Workflow Orchestration

### 1. Plan Mode Default
- Enter plan mode for ANY non-trivial task (3+ steps or architectural decisions)
- If something goes sideways, STOP and re-plan immediately — don't keep pushing
- Use plan mode for verification steps, not just building
- Write detailed specs upfront to reduce ambiguity

### 2. Subagent Strategy
- Use subagents liberally to keep main context window clean
- Offload research, exploration, and parallel analysis to subagents
- For complex problems, throw more compute at it via subagents
- One task per subagent for focused execution

### 3. Self-Improvement Loop
- After ANY correction from the user: update `tasks/lessons.md` with the pattern
- Write rules for yourself that prevent the same mistake
- Ruthlessly iterate on these lessons until mistake rate drops
- Review lessons at session start for relevant project

### 4. Verification Before Done
- Never mark a task complete without proving it works
- Diff behavior between main and your changes when relevant
- Ask yourself: "Would a staff engineer approve this?"
- Run tests, check logs, demonstrate correctness

### 5. Demand Elegance (Balanced)
- For non-trivial changes: pause and ask "is there a more elegant way?"
- If a fix feels hacky: "Knowing everything I know now, implement the elegant solution"
- Skip this for simple, obvious fixes — don't over-engineer
- Challenge your own work before presenting it

### 6. Autonomous Bug Fixing
- When given a bug report: just fix it. Don't ask for hand-holding
- Point at logs, errors, failing tests — then resolve them
- Zero context switching required from the user
- Go fix failing CI tests without being told how

## Task Management

1. **Plan First**: Write plan to `tasks/todo.md` with checkable items
2. **Verify Plan**: Check in before starting implementation
3. **Track Progress**: Mark items complete as you go
4. **Explain Changes**: High-level summary at each step
5. **Document Results**: Add review section to `tasks/todo.md`
6. **Capture Lessons**: Update `tasks/lessons.md` after corrections

## Core Principles

- **Simplicity First**: Make every change as simple as possible. Impact minimal code.
- **No Laziness**: Find root causes. No temporary fixes. Senior developer standards.
- **Minimal Impact**: Changes should only touch what's necessary. Avoid introducing bugs.

Pillar 2: .claude/skills/ = Reusable Expert Modes

▼

Turn common workflows into skills: code review checklist, refactor playbook, release procedure, debugging flow. Provides consistency across sessions and teammates.

---
name: code-review
description: Structured code review with security and quality checks
---

# Code Review

## Process
1. Check for security vulnerabilities (OWASP Top 10)
2. Verify error handling covers edge cases
3. Check test coverage for new code paths
4. Review naming conventions match project style
5. Verify no secrets or credentials in code

## Checklist
- [ ] No SQL injection vectors
- [ ] Input validation on all endpoints
- [ ] Tests cover happy path + error cases
- [ ] No console.log statements left behind

Pillar 3: .claude/hooks/ = Guardrails

▼

For deterministic actions: run formatter after edits, run tests on core changes, block unsafe directories. Models forget. Hooks don't.

{
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Edit",
        "command": "npx prettier --write $file"
      }
    ],
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Edit",
        "command": "echo $file | grep -v 'node_modules\\|dist\\|.env'"
      }
    ]
  }
}
Important

Hooks run shell commands automatically. Use them for formatting, linting, and safety checks — not for business logic. See the Hooks Deep Dive for configuration, exit codes, and more recipes.

Pillar 4: docs/ = Progressive Context

▼

Architecture overview, ADRs (engineering decisions), operational runbooks. Don't bloat prompts — put detailed docs in files Claude can read when needed.

docs/
├── architecture.md        # System design overview
├── adr/                   # Architecture Decision Records
│   ├── 001-use-prisma.md
│   ├── 002-auth-strategy.md
│   └── 003-caching-layer.md
├── runbooks/              # Operational procedures
│   ├── deploy.md
│   └── incident-response.md
└── api/                   # API documentation
    └── endpoints.md

Claude discovers and reads these files on demand when working in related areas. The key is progressive context — only loaded when relevant.

Pillar 5: Local CLAUDE.md for Risky Modules

▼

Place small CLAUDE.md files near sharp edges. Claude sees gotchas exactly when working in that directory.

# src/auth/CLAUDE.md

## Critical: Authentication Module

- NEVER modify the JWT signing key rotation logic
- Token refresh uses sliding window — do not change to fixed expiry
- Rate limiting is per-IP, configured in middleware (not here)
- All changes to this module require security review
- Test with: `npm test -- --grep "auth"`

Good candidates: src/auth/, src/billing/, src/persistence/, infra/

Project Structure Map

my-project/
├── CLAUDE.md                    # Repo memory (auto-loaded)
├── .claude/
│   ├── settings.json            # Project settings (committed to git)
│   ├── skills/                  # Reusable expert modes
│   │   ├── code-review.md
│   │   └── deploy.md
│   └── commands/                # Custom slash commands
│       └── test-suite.md
├── docs/                        # Progressive context
│   ├── architecture.md
│   └── adr/
├── src/
│   ├── auth/
│   │   └── CLAUDE.md            # Local guardrails
│   └── billing/
│       └── CLAUDE.md            # Local guardrails
└── spec.md                      # Feature specification
09.5 — The Claude Configuration Cascade

How Teams Structure .claude/ Across Org, Project & Developer Layers

CLAUDE.md and .claude/ aren't just one file in one folder — they cascade. Org-wide rules, project-specific context, and personal preferences compose automatically as Claude walks up the directory tree. Here's how to architect the layers so team-shared rules stay in git, personal preferences stay local, and nothing gets duplicated.

The .claude/ directory (project layer)

.claude/ at project root · committed to git · shared with team

The project-level .claude/ folder is the control center for Claude Code. It defines instructions, custom commands, permission rules, and specialized agent behaviors for everyone working in the repo.

CLAUDE.md

Central instruction manual. Claude reads it at session start for project context, architecture, and coding standards.

settings.json

Permission controls — which tools (bash, read, write) are allowed, which files or commands are denied, which MCP servers are available.

rules/

Modular, markdown-based instructions. Rules without a paths field apply globally; others only when working in designated directories.

commands/

Custom slash commands (e.g. /project:review). Each markdown file becomes a command, with shell support via the ! prefix.

skills/

Reusable, context-aware workflows. More advanced than commands — auto-invoked by Claude when relevant, not just triggered manually.

agents/

Specialized subagents with defined personas and restricted tool access. Run risky tasks (reviews, audits) in isolated sessions.

The ~/.claude/ directory (user layer)

~/.claude/ · personal to each developer · never committed to git

The global folder holds personal preferences that apply across every project on your machine. It's yours alone — never mandate its contents across a team.

~/.claude/CLAUDE.md

Global coding preferences — "always use TypeScript", "prefer functional style", personal workflow quirks.

~/.claude/projects/

Auto-memory store. Claude persists MEMORY.md per project so knowledge survives across sessions.

commands/ & skills/

Personal commands and skills available everywhere, not scoped to one project. Your personal toolbox.

Org and project .claude/ live in git. Only ~/.claude/ stays local. The config-cascade rule

Cascading rules & precedence

CLAUDE.md files are loaded by walking up the directory tree from the current working directory. Multiple CLAUDE.md files combine — each layer adds context without requiring duplication. When rules conflict, precedence flows:

System policy  ─▶  User (~/.claude/)  ─▶  Project (.claude/)   ← wins
   (floor)         (personal prefs)        (team-shared, highest)

Project-level configuration takes highest precedence, ensuring team-shared rules always apply. System policy sets the safety floor. User preferences sit in between.

Path-Scoped Rules

Rules inside .claude/rules/ can declare a paths: field to apply only when Claude works in specific subdirectories (e.g. src/api/). Rules without paths apply project-wide. Use this to keep CLAUDE.md lean instead of overloading it with conditionals.

Single-project layout

.claude/
├── settings.json            # Permissions (allow bash, read files)
├── CLAUDE.md                # High-level rules & project context
├── rules/
│   ├── api.md               # Rules scoped to src/api/
│   └── tests.md             # Testing conventions
├── commands/
│   └── review.md            # /project:review slash command
├── skills/
│   └── secure-commit/
│       └── SKILL.md         # Auto-invoked commit workflow
└── agents/
    └── code-reviewer.md     # Isolated review subagent

Multi-project org cascade

Three layers — org (shared defaults), product (domain context), developer (personal). Org and product layers live in git; the developer layer stays local.

my-org/                          # Monorepo or org config repo
├── .claude/                     # ━━ ORG LAYER ━━ shared defaults
│   ├── CLAUDE.md                # Org-wide tone, security, standards
│   ├── settings.json            # Default model, shared MCP servers
│   ├── rules/
│   │   └── security.md          # Non-negotiable security rules
│   ├── commands/
│   │   └── review.md            # Org-standard code review
│   ├── skills/
│   │   └── code-style/SKILL.md  # Org coding conventions
│   └── agents/
│       └── sec-audit.md         # Security audit subagent
│
├── product-a/                   # ━━ PRODUCT LAYER ━━ domain context
│   ├── .claude/
│   │   ├── CLAUDE.md            # Architecture, stack, glossary
│   │   ├── rules/
│   │   │   ├── api.md           # REST patterns (paths: src/api/)
│   │   │   └── migrations.md    # DB migration rules
│   │   ├── commands/
│   │   │   └── deploy.md        # Product-specific deploy
│   │   ├── skills/
│   │   │   └── workflow/SKILL.md
│   │   └── agents/
│   │       └── reviewer.md      # Domain-aware reviewer
│   └── src/
│
├── product-b/                   # Different product, own .claude/
│   └── .claude/
│       ├── CLAUDE.md
│       └── ...
│
# ━━ DEVELOPER LAYER ━━ personal (not in git)
~/.claude/
├── CLAUDE.md                    # "Always use TypeScript"
├── projects/
│   ├── product-a/MEMORY.md      # Auto-persisted session memory
│   └── product-b/MEMORY.md
├── commands/
│   └── standup.md               # Personal shortcut
└── skills/
    └── my-workflow/SKILL.md

8 Recommendations for Teams

1. CLAUDE.md is your contract

Org-level sets non-negotiable rules. Project-level extends with domain context. The directory tree walk combines them automatically — never duplicate.

2. Use rules/ for path scoping

Don't overload CLAUDE.md with conditional logic. Put API rules in rules/api.md scoped to src/api/, test rules in rules/tests.md. Modular and clean.

3. Skills are portable packages

Encode repeatable workflows as SKILL.md directories. Any methodology becomes a mountable package any project can adopt. Skills auto-invoke — commands don't.

4. Agents isolate risky tasks

Define subagents for sensitive operations — code reviews, security audits, dependency checks. They run in isolated sessions with restricted tool access.

5. Commands are the team API

Slash commands (/project:review, /project:deploy) make workflows discoverable. Org commands set the floor; projects add domain-specific ones.

6. Let MEMORY.md accumulate

Claude auto-persists session knowledge to ~/.claude/projects/*/MEMORY.md. Let it build context over time and review periodically for accuracy.

7. Version-control shared layers

Org and project .claude/ live in git. Only ~/.claude/ stays local. Skills and agents are versioned and distributed like code.

8. Scope MCP servers per layer

Org settings.json defines shared servers (Slack, Drive). Projects add domain-specific ones. Users add personal tools. Servers compose additively.

Notable settings.json Keys

Beyond permissions and MCP server lists, settings.json accepts several useful configuration keys:

  • prUrlTemplate — Point the footer PR badge to a custom review URL instead of github.com. Useful for GitLab, Bitbucket, or GitHub Enterprise teams.
  • otelHeadersHelper — Path to a script that auto-refreshes OTEL auth tokens (runs every 29 min). See Monitoring section.
  • disableAllHooks — Set true to disable all hooks globally (useful for troubleshooting).
  • allowManagedHooksOnly — When set to true, only hooks distributed via managed settings (MDM/org policy) are allowed to run; user-defined hooks in project and user settings.json are blocked. Useful for enterprise environments that want to control hook execution centrally.
  • skillOverrides — Control per-skill model visibility: {"skill-name": "off"} hides from both model and / menu; "user-invocable-only" hides from model only; "name-only" sends name without description to the model.
  • settings.autoMode.hard_deny — Auto mode classifier rules that block unconditionally regardless of user intent or allow exceptions. Unlike regular deny rules (which the classifier can override when intent is clear), hard-deny rules are absolute. Use the same pattern array format as allow/deny rules in auto mode settings.
Permission Allowlists & settings.local.json

When you select "Always allow" at a Bash tool permission prompt, Claude Code writes that entry to .claude/settings.local.json — the gitignored, project-local layer. This means your personal approvals accumulate per-project without affecting teammates. To bulk-migrate common read-only patterns (grep, git diff, ls) into the allowlist at once, run /less-permission-prompts.

Patterns & Anti-Patterns

✕ Copying CLAUDE.md into every repo

Write once at the org level and inherit via the directory tree walk. Duplication guarantees drift.

✕ Domain rules at the org level

Keep domain context at the project level. Org level is for cross-cutting standards only.

✕ One 2000-line CLAUDE.md

Factor into rules/ with path scoping for modularity. Bloated CLAUDE.md files lose important rules in the noise.

✕ Mandating personal ~/.claude/

Let developers own their personal layer entirely. Enforcement belongs in the project .claude/, not the user's home.

✕ Running sensitive tasks in the main session

Define isolated agents/ with restricted permissions for audits, deploys, and anything touching credentials.

✕ Ignoring MEMORY.md

Stale auto-memory poisons future sessions. Periodically review for accuracy and prune drift.

External Memory: Obsidian & Markdown Vaults

Claude's MEMORY.md is passive — session knowledge auto-persists as the model works. A curated markdown vault (Obsidian, Logseq, plain notes/) is the complement: intentional, human-authored, and portable across tools, models, and years.

Andrej Karpathy popularized this pattern — a long-lived Obsidian vault of notes, decisions, and research that gets selectively fed into LLM sessions as high-signal context. The vault outlives any single model or IDE; the AI is just one of many readers.

Practical integration patterns:

  • Symlink a vault subfolder into the repo (e.g. docs/notes/ → ~/Obsidian/project-x/) so Claude reads it as part of the project tree.
  • Paste relevant notes into context at session start — curated beats comprehensive.
  • Wire an Obsidian / Reflect / Notion MCP server so Claude can search and append notes directly, turning the vault into a read/write memory layer.
  • Treat the vault as source-of-truth; let MEMORY.md churn freely underneath it.
Codex Equivalent

OpenAI's Codex CLI uses the same layered architecture with different filenames: AGENTS.md instead of CLAUDE.md, .rules/ for guardrails, and .agents/skills/ for reusable workflows. The cascade model (org → project → user) is identical. See the Codex Best Practices page for the full mapping.

10 — Integrations

Connect Claude to Your Tools via MCP

Model Context Protocol (MCP) is the universal adapter between AI models and your existing tools — databases, APIs, design tools, project management.

3rd-Party Model Providers

Running Claude Code with Amazon Bedrock or Google Vertex AI? Use the interactive setup wizard: run claude, select "3rd-party platform" from the login screen, and follow the guided setup for authentication, region, and model pinning — no manual env var configuration needed.

Claude Code ──▶ MCP Protocol ──▶ JIRA
                              ──▶ Figma
                              ──▶ Notion
                              ──▶ Azure DevOps
                              ──▶ Google Workspace

Setting Up MCP Connections via /plugin

The fastest way to connect Claude Code to external tools is through the /plugin command. Plugins bundle MCP servers with OAuth — no API tokens or manual config needed.

1

Open the Plugin Browser

In any Claude Code session, type /plugin and press Enter. This opens the interactive plugin browser showing all available plugins.

2

Select a Plugin to Install

Browse the list and select the plugin you want (e.g., Atlassian, Figma, Notion). Press Enter to install it. Claude downloads the plugin and registers its MCP server automatically.

3

Authenticate via OAuth

On first use of a plugin tool, Claude opens your browser for OAuth login (e.g., sign in with your Atlassian, Figma, or Notion account). This is a one-time step — credentials are cached for future sessions.

4

Verify the Connection

Run /mcp to confirm the plugin's MCP server is connected and its tools are available. You should see the server listed with a green status.

# Verify MCP connections
/mcp

# Example output:
✓ plugin:atlassian:atlassian — connected
✓ plugin:Notion:notion — connected
✓ plugin:figma:figma — connected
Plugin vs. Manual MCP Config

Use plugins for Jira, Figma, and Notion — they handle OAuth and updates automatically. Use manual mcpServers config in .claude/settings.json for tools without plugins (Azure DevOps, Google Workspace, custom servers). You can mix both approaches.

Available Integrations

JIRA MCP

▼

What it does: Read, create, and update JIRA issues directly from Claude. Search issues, transition statuses, add comments, and manage sprints without leaving the terminal.

The easiest way to connect is via the Atlassian plugin, which uses Atlassian's hosted MCP endpoint with OAuth — no API tokens needed:

// Plugin .mcp.json (managed automatically)
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "atlassian": {
      "type": "http",
      "url": "https://mcp.atlassian.com/v1/mcp"
    }
  }
}

Setup: Enable the atlassian plugin in Claude Code settings. Auth is handled via OAuth on first use — run /mcp to verify the connection.

Example usage: "Create a JIRA ticket for this bug in the AUTH project" or "Show me all in-progress tickets assigned to me"

Figma MCP

▼

What it does: Read Figma designs, extract component specs, colors, typography, and layout details. Implement designs with 1:1 visual fidelity directly from Figma files.

Enable the Figma plugin in Claude Code settings. It uses Figma's hosted MCP endpoint with OAuth:

// Plugin .mcp.json (managed automatically)
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "figma": {
      "type": "http",
      "url": "https://mcp.figma.com/mcp"
    }
  }
}

Setup: Enable the figma plugin in Claude Code settings. Auth is handled via OAuth on first use — run /mcp to verify the connection.

Example usage: "Implement the login page from this Figma file: [URL]" or "Extract the color palette from our design system"

Notion MCP

▼

What it does: Search, read, create, and update Notion pages and databases. Pull specs from Notion, sync task status, and create documentation.

Enable the Notion plugin in Claude Code settings. It uses Notion's hosted MCP endpoint with OAuth — no API tokens needed:

// Plugin .mcp.json (managed automatically)
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "notion": {
      "type": "http",
      "url": "https://mcp.notion.com/mcp"
    }
  }
}

Setup: Enable the Notion plugin in Claude Code settings. Auth is handled via OAuth on first use — run /mcp to verify the connection.

Example usage: "Find the spec in Notion and implement the user stories" or "Create a Notion page documenting this API"

Azure DevOps MCP

▼

What it does: Manage work items, repos, pipelines, wikis, test plans, and advanced security in Azure DevOps. Microsoft's official MCP server with browser-based OAuth — no PAT needed.

// .claude/settings.json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "ado": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["-y", "@azure-devops/mcp", "your-org-name"]
    }
  }
}

Setup: Requires Node.js 20+. On first tool use, a browser window opens for Microsoft OAuth login — no PAT or env vars needed. Replace your-org-name with your Azure DevOps organization. See microsoft/azure-devops-mcp for details.

Example usage: "List my ADO projects" or "List ADO builds for 'Contoso'" or "Create a work item in the Sprint 12 backlog"

Google Workspace MCP

▼

What it does: Google offers hosted MCP servers for Google Cloud infrastructure (BigQuery, Cloud SQL, GKE, Compute Engine, etc.). Google Workspace products (Docs, Sheets, Drive, Gmail, Calendar) are not yet available as hosted MCP endpoints — use a community server for those.

Google Cloud MCP (official, hosted)

// .claude/settings.json — example: BigQuery
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "bigquery": {
      "httpUrl": "https://bigquery.googleapis.com/mcp",
      "authProviderType": "google_credentials",
      "headers": {
        "x-goog-user-project": "your-project-id"
      }
    }
  }
}

Setup: Install gcloud CLI, run gcloud auth application-default login, then enable the MCP server: gcloud beta services mcp enable SERVICE --project=PROJECT_ID. See Google Cloud MCP docs for full instructions.

Google Workspace MCP (community, self-hosted)

# For Docs, Sheets, Drive, Gmail, Calendar
# github.com/taylorwilsdon/google_workspace_mcp

git clone https://github.com/taylorwilsdon/google_workspace_mcp
cd google_workspace_mcp && cargo build --release
claude mcp add google-workspace -- ./target/release/google_workspace_mcp

Requires a Google Cloud project with OAuth credentials and the relevant APIs enabled. See the project README for setup.

Example usage: "Read the PRD from this Google Doc and create a spec.md" or "Query the BigQuery dataset for last month's revenue"

Tip

You can run multiple MCPs simultaneously. Claude auto-discovers available tools from all connected servers. Jira, Figma, and Notion are available as plugins with hosted MCP endpoints (OAuth, no tokens). Azure DevOps and Google Workspace require self-hosted servers. Check registry.modelcontextprotocol.io for the latest server options.

10.5 — Code Review Agent

Multi-Agent Code Review for Pull Requests

A fleet of specialized agents examines your diff in parallel — catching logic errors, security vulnerabilities, edge cases, and regressions — then posts verified findings as inline PR comments.

How the Multi-Agent Architecture Works

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Stage 1: Parallel Bug Detection                 │
│                                                  │
│  Agent A: Logic errors       ─┐                  │
│  Agent B: Security vulns      │                  │
│  Agent C: Edge cases          ├─► Candidate      │
│  Agent D: Regressions         │   Findings       │
│  Agent E: Type safety        ─┘                  │
│                                                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Stage 2: Verification                           │
│                                                  │
│  Each candidate checked against actual code      │
│  behavior to filter out false positives          │
│                                                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Stage 3: Ranking & Deduplication                │
│                                                  │
│  Results consolidated, ranked by severity,       │
│  posted as inline PR comments                    │
│                                                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Coverage on large PRs 84%

of PRs over 1,000 lines receive at least one actionable finding, averaging 7.5 issues per review.

Source: Anthropic internal telemetry, Claude Code review agent
False positive rate <1%

Verified agents filter noise before anything reaches the PR — comments you see are comments worth reading.

Source: Anthropic internal telemetry, Claude Code review agent

Setup Guide

1

Enable in Claude Admin

Go to claude.ai/admin-settings/claude-code, find the Code Review section, and click Setup. Requires a Claude Team or Enterprise subscription.

2

Install the Claude GitHub App

Grant Contents, Issues, and Pull Requests read/write permissions. Select which repositories to enable — start with 1–2 repos to test.

3

Choose Your Review Trigger

After PR creation only — runs once when opened (recommended to start). After every push — runs on each push, auto-resolves threads when issues are fixed.

4

Create a REVIEW.md

Place a REVIEW.md at your repo root with review-specific rules. This file is read exclusively during code reviews, unlike CLAUDE.md which applies to all Claude interactions.

Example REVIEW.md

# Code Review Guidelines

## Always check
- New API endpoints have corresponding integration tests
- Database migrations are backward-compatible
- Error messages don't leak internal details to users
- No hardcoded secrets or credentials

## Style preferences
- Prefer early returns over nested conditionals
- Use structured logging, not f-string interpolation

## Skip
- Generated files under src/gen/
- Formatting-only changes in *.lock files
- Auto-generated migration files

Reading the Results

Each finding is posted as an inline comment tagged with a severity marker:

Marker Meaning Action
Red circle Bug — fix before merge Address immediately
Yellow circle Nit — minor issue or style suggestion Address at your discretion
Purple circle Pre-existing — bug not introduced by this PR Track for future cleanup
Important

Claude Code Review does not approve or block PRs. Your existing review workflows stay intact. Each comment includes a collapsible extended reasoning section explaining the agent's analysis.

Alternative: GitHub Actions (Self-Hosted)

If you prefer self-hosted review or are on a Pro plan, use claude-code-action on GitHub Marketplace:

# .github/workflows/claude-review.yml
name: Claude Code Review
on:
  pull_request:
    types: [opened, synchronize, reopened]

jobs:
  review:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: anthropics/claude-code-action@v1
        with:
          anthropic_api_key: ${{ secrets.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY }}
          review_mode: true

Alternative: Local Review with Custom Subagents

Create a custom code review subagent that runs locally before pushing:

# .claude/agents/code-reviewer.md
---
name: code-reviewer
description: Reviews code for bugs, logic errors, and security issues
tools:
  - Read
  - Grep
  - Glob
  - Bash
---

Review the current git diff for:
- Logic errors and potential bugs
- Security vulnerabilities (injection, auth bypass, data exposure)
- Missing error handling at system boundaries
- Regressions against existing tests

Only report findings you are confident about.
Use a structured format: file, line, severity, description.

Then run: Use the code-reviewer agent to review my staged changes

When to Use It

Best for

Large PRs, AI-assisted code changes, cross-cutting refactors, PRs from newer team members, codebases with complex architectural invariants.

Less useful for

Tiny formatting-only diffs, instant gating needs (reviews take ~20 min), or as a full replacement for human review — it's designed to complement, not replace.

Pro Tips

Write specific rules in REVIEW.md for targeted findings. Start with one repo and evaluate quality. Use Claude for the first pass, humans for architectural and design decisions. Monitor spend at claude.ai/analytics/code-review — reviews average $15–25 each.

10.6 — Security Review

Multi-Agent Security Review Team

Orchestrate a specialist-library security review team for pre-merge or pre-release hardening. The skill picks a roster based on detected stack signals, spawns specialists in parallel, and consolidates structured findings into a single severity-ranked report. Review only — no fixes.

Invocation

Install from the fsad_playbook repo (see Install the Skill below), then invoke with /sec-review-team <target path> <scope> (scope = all | <subdir> | diff vs main). The -team suffix disambiguates from the built-in /security-review (a single-pass branch review). Pair with /sec-review-fixes to produce fix PRs from the report. Validated on Opus 4.7 against a Tauri + React + Rust target (CBP-060 → 3 high-severity findings after dedupe, zero hallucinated CVEs).

The Specialist Library (13)

The roster is selected automatically from detected stack signals (see stack-signals.md). Four baseline specialists always run; stack-specific additions layer on top.

Specialist Default for Scope
secrets-crypto-auditorall (baseline)Hardcoded secrets, weak crypto, insecure random, key storage, TLS config.
dependency-supplychain-auditorall (baseline)Known CVEs, lockfile integrity, install scripts, typosquats, plugin alignment.
silent-failure-hunterall (baseline)Swallowed exceptions, fail-open error paths, security-relevant silences.
data-exposure-auditorall (baseline)PII storage, log redaction, error-leak patterns, IDOR, outbound-call verification.
auth-authz-auditorwebapp, desktop, backend, multi-userAuth flows, JWT/OAuth, RBAC, IPC command surfaces, Tauri capability grants.
input-validation-auditorwebapp, backend, desktopSQLi, XSS, command/path traversal, deserialization, SSRF, IPC payload validation.
frontend-security-auditorwebapp, desktop-with-webviewCSP, CORS, SRI, cookie flags, postMessage origin, clickjacking, third-party scripts.
prompt-injection-auditorllm-agent, rag, agenticPrompt concat of untrusted input, tool-use role confusion, context poisoning, memory injection.
iac-auditorterraform, kubernetes, helm, cloudformationPublic resources, IAM wildcards, encryption, pod security, IaC hardcoded credentials.
container-runtime-auditordocker, kubernetesDockerfile hardening, base-image CVEs, compose privileges, pod security context.
ci-cd-security-auditorany repo with CIWorkflow triggers, action SHA-pinning, permissions block, secrets-in-CI, artifact integrity.
concurrency-race-auditorbackend, realtime, multi-userTOCTOU, double-check-locking, rate-limit races, session races, transactional integrity.
privacy-telemetry-auditorconsumer-facing webapp/desktopOutbound data, analytics SDKs, cookies, consent flow, cross-border transfer.

Specialist Definitions

Each card mirrors the live specialist file under skills/sec-review-team/specialists/ in the repo. baseline specialists always run; stack-specific specialists join based on stack-signals.md.

secrets-crypto-auditor baseline

▼

Primary scope

Secrets and cryptography.

  • Hardcoded secrets / API keys / tokens / passwords in source (full-tree grep on password|secret|api_key|token|bearer|credentials|private_key).
  • Weak hash algorithms (MD5, SHA1, DES, RC4).
  • Insecure randomness (Math.random() for security IDs; OS-level CSPRNG required for keys).
  • Improper key/token storage (plaintext on disk, unencrypted SQLite, localStorage for secrets).
  • Credentials in logs, error messages, panics, stack traces.
  • unsafe Rust touching key material.
  • TLS/SSL configuration — outdated protocol versions, weak cipher suites, certificate pinning where needed.
  • Key rotation / expiry policy.

Threat model

Frame against the realistic adversary for the stack: disk compromise (stolen laptop, forensics, backup snooping) for local-only desktop; network attacker plus insider for SaaS backends; secrets leaked in build logs and committed .env for the supply chain.

Coverage categories

hardcoded-secrets, weak-crypto, insecure-random, plaintext-key-storage, credential-logging, tls-config, key-rotation

Definition file

# secrets-crypto-auditor

## Primary scope
Secrets and cryptography.

- Hardcoded secrets / API keys / tokens / passwords in source (full-tree grep: `password|secret|api[_-]?key|token|bearer|authorization|credentials|private[_-]?key`).
- Weak hash algorithms (MD5, SHA1, DES, RC4).
- Insecure randomness (`Math.random()` for security IDs, `rand::thread_rng()` where CSPRNG needed; OS-level CSPRNG required for keys).
- Improper key/token storage (plaintext on disk, unencrypted SQLite, localStorage for secrets).
- Credentials in logs, error messages, panics, stack traces.
- `unsafe` Rust touching key material.
- TLS/SSL configuration — outdated protocol versions, weak cipher suites, certificate pinning or its absence where needed.
- Key rotation / expiry policy.

## Threat-model framing
Frame findings against the realistic adversary for the stack:
- **Local-only desktop:** disk compromise (stolen laptop, forensics, backup snooping) — plaintext DB is the top risk.
- **SaaS backend:** network attacker + insider — key storage in secret manager, rotation, scoped IAM.
- **Supply-chain:** secrets leaked in build logs, committed `.env`, compromised CI secrets.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** secrets in code, crypto primitive choice, key storage, randomness.
- **Cross-cuts with:**
  - `dependency-supplychain-auditor` — secrets leaked in CI workflows overlap; coordinate.
  - `privacy-telemetry-auditor` — data-handling compliance overlaps with key-handling; yours is the crypto/key side.

## Brief

> Review secrets and cryptography in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Realistic threat model: `<THREAT_MODEL>`. Check: hardcoded secrets, weak crypto, insecure random, improper key storage, credential logging, `unsafe` Rust touching keys, TLS config, key rotation. Frame findings against the actual threat model.
>
> **Output contract (four files):** `secrets-crypto-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Same format as other specialists — see `auth-authz-auditor.md` for details.
>
> **Confidence on every finding.** Negative findings require searches + search_limits in coverage.jsonl (e.g., "grepped for X|Y|Z, found nothing — would miss credentials assembled at runtime from env + obfuscation").
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only; cite evidence; no invented CVEs.
>
> Report: four output paths + severity count.

## Output files
`secrets-crypto-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`

## Allowed tools
Standard read-only set (see `auth-authz-auditor.md`). `Write` scoped to this specialist's four outputs.

## Coverage categories this specialist owns
`hardcoded-secrets`, `weak-crypto`, `insecure-random`, `plaintext-key-storage`, `credential-logging`, `tls-config`, `key-rotation`

## Scanner integration
- `gitleaks detect` / `trufflehog filesystem` — secrets scanning. Triage hits + find what they miss (runtime-assembled secrets, non-pattern credentials).
- Pass scanner output as context via the pre-pass (CBP-067).

dependency-supplychain-auditor baseline

▼

Primary scope

Supply chain — dependencies, lockfile integrity, install scripts, build trust.

  • npm: package.json, package-lock.json, npm audit if available; pinned vs floating versions; postinstall / install scripts.
  • Cargo: Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock, cargo audit.
  • Python: requirements.txt, Pipfile.lock, poetry.lock, pip-audit.
  • OSV: osv-scanner across any ecosystem.
  • Suspicious packages, typosquats, recently compromised deps.
  • build.rs, postinstall/preinstall scripts, Dockerfile RUN installs.
  • Plugin / core version alignment.

Threat model

Treats package manifests, lockfiles, and install scripts as a third-party trust boundary — known CVEs, typosquats, and post-install hooks compromise the build before any of your code runs. Will not invent CVE IDs if a scanner isn't available; reports tool-availability in coverage.

Coverage categories

known-cves, lockfile-integrity, version-pinning, install-scripts, typosquats, plugin-alignment, build-rs-trust

Definition file

# dependency-supplychain-auditor

## Primary scope
Supply chain. Dependencies, lockfile integrity, install scripts, build trust.

- **npm**: `package.json`, `package-lock.json`. `npm audit` if available. Pinned vs floating versions; lockfile presence + drift. Postinstall / install scripts (`hasInstallScript: true`).
- **Cargo**: `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`. `cargo audit` if installed.
- **Python**: `requirements.txt`, `Pipfile.lock`, `poetry.lock`, `pyproject.toml`. `pip-audit`.
- **OSV**: `osv-scanner` across any ecosystem.
- Suspicious packages, typosquats, recently compromised deps.
- `build.rs`, postinstall/preinstall scripts, Dockerfile `RUN` installs.
- Plugin/core version alignment.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** package manifests, lockfiles, CVE scanning.
- **Cross-cuts with:**
  - `ci-cd-security-auditor` — action-pinning and workflow trust boundaries are theirs; package-dep security is yours.
  - `secrets-crypto-auditor` — secrets in lockfiles / dep configs overlap; credentials in `npm audit` output are theirs to triage.

## Brief

> Review supply chain in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Run available scanners (`npm audit`, `cargo audit`, `pip-audit`, `osv-scanner`). If a scanner isn't available, say so — DO NOT invent CVE IDs. Check pinned-vs-floating, lockfile presence + drift, install scripts, typosquats, plugin-core alignment.
>
> **Output:** `dependency-supplychain-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Scanner output normalized into `findings.jsonl` with `source: "scanner-<name>"`. See other specialist briefs for format.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only; never install anything; no fabricated CVEs.
>
> Report: paths + severity count + tool-availability summary.

## Allowed tools
Standard read-only + `Bash` allowlist including `npm audit`, `cargo audit`, `pip-audit`, `osv-scanner`, `npm ls`, `cargo tree`. `Write` scoped to this specialist's outputs.

## Coverage categories
`known-cves`, `lockfile-integrity`, `version-pinning`, `install-scripts`, `typosquats`, `plugin-alignment`, `build-rs-trust`

## Scanner integration (primary consumer)
All dep/CVE scanners feed this specialist. Pre-pass (CBP-067) runs them; specialist triages + extends.

silent-failure-hunter baseline

▼

Primary scope

Error paths that fail open or mask security-relevant failures.

  • Swallowed exceptions: try{}catch{}, .catch(() => {}), Rust .ok() / .unwrap_or_default() on security-relevant errors.
  • Fallback paths that bypass validation when the primary check errors.
  • Rust match arms with Err(_) => Ok(()).
  • Async promises without .catch; unhandled rejections.
  • unwrap() on user-influenced data.
  • React error boundaries catching but not surfacing state-corrupting errors.
  • Errors around DB migrations, capability checks, session-rotation — not the check itself, the handling of the failure.

Threat model

Security-relevant silences only. A swallowed UI toast error is not a finding; a swallowed DB-migration error that leaves the schema half-applied is. Defers architectural IPC/capability issues to auth-authz-auditor and missing validation to input-validation-auditor.

Coverage categories

swallowed-exceptions, fallback-bypasses-validation, unhandled-rejections, unwrap-on-user-input, error-boundary-absorption, migration-silent-failures

Definition file

# silent-failure-hunter

## Primary scope
Error paths that fail open or mask security-relevant failures.

- Swallowed exceptions: `try{}catch{}`, `.catch(() => {})`, Rust `.ok()` / `.unwrap_or_default()` on security-relevant errors.
- Fallback paths that bypass validation when the primary check errors.
- Rust `match` arms with `Err(_) => Ok(())`.
- Async promises without `.catch`; unhandled rejections.
- `unwrap()` on user-influenced data.
- React error boundaries catching but not surfacing state-corrupting errors.
- Errors around DB migrations, capability checks, session-rotation — not the check itself, the handling of the failure.

## Filter rule
Security-relevant silences only. A swallowed UI toast error is not a finding. A swallowed DB-migration error that leaves the schema half-applied is.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** error-path silences, fail-open patterns in error handling.
- **Defers to:**
  - `auth-authz-auditor` for architectural IPC/capability issues — mention only when compounding a specific error silence.
  - `input-validation-auditor` for missing validation (vs. validation-present-but-swallowed).
- **Cross-cuts with:**
  - `concurrency-race-auditor` — cancellation/race paths with silent failure overlap; coordinate.

## Brief

> Review error paths in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Focus on silences that affect confidentiality, integrity, or availability. Architectural IPC/capability issues are `auth-authz-auditor`'s scope — defer. Missing-validation is `input-validation-auditor`'s scope — defer.
>
> **Output:** `silent-failure-hunter.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Standard format.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only; filter for security-relevance; cite evidence; defer architectural issues to their primary owner with `coverage.status=deferred-to-other-specialist`.
>
> Report: paths + severity count.

## Allowed tools
Standard read-only set. `Write` scoped to four outputs.

## Coverage categories
`swallowed-exceptions`, `fallback-bypasses-validation`, `unhandled-rejections`, `unwrap-on-user-input`, `error-boundary-absorption`, `migration-silent-failures`

## Scanner integration
- None specific. `semgrep` pre-pass rules for "empty catch" patterns may feed this specialist.

data-exposure-auditor baseline

▼

Primary scope

Unintended exposure of sensitive data.

  • PII / sensitive content storage (DB path, encryption-at-rest, cache/temp files, exports).
  • Log redaction (println!, console.log, log::*, tracing::*).
  • Error-message shapes leaking internals (stack traces, file paths, SQL).
  • IDOR / tenant isolation.
  • Clipboard / drag-and-drop lingering data.
  • Export pipeline (PDF/DOCX temp files, cleanup).
  • Outbound network calls — if threat model claims "no network", verify with grep (any hit is a finding).

Threat model

Verifies stated threat-model claims (e.g. "no network") with explicit grep — any deviation is a finding. Owns "what could leak" (storage, logs, errors); defers analytics/consent to privacy-telemetry-auditor and CSP/CORS to frontend-security-auditor when those are in roster, and reclaims them otherwise.

Coverage categories

pii-storage, log-redaction, error-leak, idor-tenant-isolation, clipboard-dnd, export-pipeline, outbound-calls-claimed-none, (conditionally) csp-cors-sri-cookies

Definition file

# data-exposure-auditor

## Primary scope
Unintended exposure of sensitive data.

- PII / sensitive content storage (DB path, encryption-at-rest, cache/temp files, exports).
- Log redaction (`println!`, `console.log`, `log::*`, `tracing::*`).
- Error-message shapes leaking internals (stack traces, file paths, SQL).
- IDOR / tenant isolation.
- Clipboard / drag-and-drop lingering data.
- Export pipeline (PDF/DOCX temp files, cleanup).
- Outbound network calls — if threat model claims "no network", verify with grep (any hit is a finding).

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** data storage posture, log redaction, error-leak patterns, IDOR, outbound-call verification.
- **Defers to:**
  - `frontend-security-auditor` (when in roster) — CSP, CORS, SRI, cookie flags. If frontend-security is NOT in the roster, data-exposure reclaims CSP as fallback.
  - `privacy-telemetry-auditor` (when in roster) — analytics SDKs, consent flow, cross-border transfer. Data-exposure stays on "what could leak"; privacy-telemetry owns "what deliberately leaves + regulatory".

## Brief

> Review unintended data exposure in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Verify stated threat-model claims (e.g. "no network") with explicit grep. If `frontend-security-auditor` is in roster, defer CSP/CORS/SRI/cookie-flags; otherwise cover them. If `privacy-telemetry-auditor` is in roster, defer analytics/consent/cross-border; otherwise cover them.
>
> **Output:** `data-exposure-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Standard format.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only; cite evidence; verify threat-model claims with searches.
>
> Report: paths + severity count.

## Allowed tools
Standard read-only set. `Write` scoped to four outputs.

## Coverage categories
`pii-storage`, `log-redaction`, `error-leak`, `idor-tenant-isolation`, `clipboard-dnd`, `export-pipeline`, `outbound-calls-claimed-none`, (conditionally) `csp-cors-sri-cookies`

## Scanner integration
- Light: `gitleaks` for leaked data in logs; `semgrep` for `console.log(user)` patterns.

auth-authz-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Authentication, authorization, session management, and privilege boundaries.

  • Auth flows (login, signup, logout, password reset, MFA, SSO).
  • Session handling (cookie lifetime, rotation on privilege change, invalidation on logout).
  • JWT / OAuth / SAML — signature verification, audience/issuer checks, token expiry, refresh flows.
  • RBAC / ABAC — role definitions, assignment, enforcement at every protected operation.
  • Privilege boundaries — frontend/webview vs backend/trusted side, Tauri IPC command surface, Electron preload scripts, microservice trust.
  • Capability grants (Tauri capabilities/*.json, browser CSP script-src, OS permissions).
  • Endpoint / IPC command authorization mapping: every protected surface ↔ its required capability.

Threat model

Maps every endpoint, IPC command, or authenticated surface to its required capability. For local-only or single-user apps, the focus shifts from user-level auth to IPC boundaries and capability grants between webview and native side. Architectural IPC/capability issues are this specialist's scope; error-path silences belong to silent-failure-hunter.

Coverage categories

auth-flows, session-handling, jwt-oauth-saml, rbac-abac, privilege-boundaries, ipc-capability-grants

Definition file

# auth-authz-auditor

## Primary scope
Authentication, authorization, session management, and privilege boundaries.

- Auth flows (login, signup, logout, password reset, MFA, SSO).
- Session handling (cookie lifetime, rotation on privilege change, invalidation on logout).
- JWT / OAuth / SAML — signature verification, audience/issuer checks, token expiry, refresh flows.
- RBAC / ABAC — role definitions, assignment, enforcement at every protected operation.
- Privilege boundaries — frontend/webview vs backend/trusted side, Tauri IPC command surface, Electron preload scripts, microservice trust.
- Capability grants (Tauri `capabilities/*.json`, browser CSP `script-src`, OS permissions).
- Endpoint / IPC command authorization mapping: every protected surface ↔ its required capability.
- For local-only or single-user apps: focus on IPC boundaries, capability grants, and privilege boundary between frontend (webview) and backend (Rust/native), not user-level auth.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** IPC command surfaces, Tauri capability grants, session-rotation flows, JWT/OAuth/SAML verification.
- **Cross-cuts with:**
  - `silent-failure-hunter` — error-path silences in auth checks are silent-failure's scope; architectural IPC/capability issues are yours.
  - `input-validation-auditor` — authZ is yours; input validation at auth endpoints is theirs.
  - `data-exposure-auditor` — authentication-required data is yours to gate; leaked-internals patterns are theirs.

## Brief (passed to the Agent)

> Review auth flows, session handling, JWT/OAuth, RBAC, and privilege boundaries in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`.
>
> Map every endpoint / IPC command / authenticated surface to its auth requirement. For local-only / single-user apps, focus on IPC boundaries, capability grants, and the privilege boundary between the webview and backend — NOT on user-level auth (which is N/A). Architectural IPC and capability issues are YOUR scope; error-path silences are `silent-failure-hunter`'s scope — note them only if compounding.
>
> **Output contract (all four files in `<TARGET>/.planning/security-review/`):**
> 1. `auth-authz-auditor.md` — prose findings, human-readable, grouped by severity.
> 2. `auth-authz-auditor.findings.jsonl` — one JSON object per line per finding, conforming to `.claude/skills/sec-review-team/schema/finding.schema.json`. Required fields: `id`, `specialist` ("auth-authz-auditor"), `severity`, `confidence`, `title`, `root_issue`, `file`, `exploit`, `fix`, `evidence`.
> 3. `auth-authz-auditor.coverage.jsonl` — one record per attack-class axis in your scope, conforming to `coverage.schema.json`. For each category, report `status` (`checked-clean` / `checked-issues-found` / `not-checked` / `deferred-to-other-specialist`), `confidence` (`high`/`medium`/`low`), `searches` run, `files_read`, `search_limits`.
> 4. `auth-authz-auditor.status.json` — write at spawn with `{status: "starting", started_at, files_read: 0, findings_written: 0}`; update every ~5 reads; on completion write `{status: "completed", finished_at, ..., severity_counts}`.
>
> **Prose finding format:** `[SEVERITY: critical|high|medium|low] <file>:<line> — <issue>\nExploit scenario: …\nRecommended fix: …\nEvidence: <exact code/config snippet>`. Group by severity, "Scope reviewed" at top, severity counts at bottom.
>
> **Confidence:** set on every finding. `certain` = directly observed, fix mechanical; `likely` = observed + inference; `possible` = indirect evidence or architectural smell; `unverified` = cannot confirm without runtime.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only (tools are allowlisted to prove it); cite concrete evidence; no speculation; if a category is N/A emit a `coverage.jsonl` entry with the searches + search_limits that prove the N/A; don't overlap with other specialists (defer via `coverage.status=deferred-to-other-specialist`).
>
> Report back: absolute paths of the four output files + one-line severity counts.

## Output files
- `auth-authz-auditor.md` (prose)
- `auth-authz-auditor.findings.jsonl` (structured)
- `auth-authz-auditor.coverage.jsonl` (structured)
- `auth-authz-auditor.status.json` (heartbeat)

## Allowed tools
- `Read` — any file under target
- `Grep`, `Glob` — any file under target
- `Bash` allowlist: `ls`, `cat`, `head`, `tail`, `wc`, `find`, `fd`, `rg`, `grep`, `git status`, `git log`, `git diff`, `git ls-files`, `git show`, `git blame`, `jq`
- `Write` — **scoped** to `auth-authz-auditor.{md,findings.jsonl,coverage.jsonl,status.json}` only
- **Denied:** `Edit`, arbitrary `Bash`, `Write` outside output files, `NotebookEdit`, `WebFetch`, `WebSearch`

## Coverage categories this specialist owns
- `auth-flows`, `session-handling`, `jwt-oauth-saml`, `rbac-abac`, `privilege-boundaries`, `ipc-capability-grants`

## Scanner integration
- None specific to this specialist. Scanner pre-pass findings from `gitleaks`/`trufflehog` that hit auth endpoints may be triage candidates.

input-validation-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Injection vectors — tracing every external input to its sink.

  • SQL injection — string-interpolated SQL, template literals, FTS5 MATCH syntax (parameterization alone doesn't save you from FTS5 query-syntax injection).
  • Command / path traversal — fs::*, Command::*, exec, os.system, dialog APIs, archive extraction (zip-slip).
  • XSS — React's unsafe HTML escape hatch (dangerouslySetInnerHTML), innerHTML/outerHTML, Markdown→HTML serializers, template engines with auto-escape disabled.
  • Deserialization — JSON/YAML/bincode/pickle boundaries; prototype pollution.
  • IPC payload validation — backends validating incoming args via typed structs vs trusting the caller.
  • Open-redirect / SSRF — URL parameters reaching fetch/reqwest/curl without host allowlist.

Threat model

Trace every external input to its sink. Defers prompt injection to prompt-injection-auditor when in roster; otherwise reclaims it. Authentication of input sources belongs to auth-authz-auditor; validation of input content is here.

Coverage categories

sql-injection, fts5-syntax-injection, command-injection, path-traversal, zip-slip, xss, deserialization, ipc-payload-validation, ssrf, open-redirect

Definition file

# input-validation-auditor

## Primary scope
Injection vectors — tracing every external input to its sink.

- **SQL injection** — string-interpolated SQL, template literals, FTS5 `MATCH` syntax (parameterization alone doesn't save you from FTS5 query-syntax injection).
- **Command / path traversal** — `fs::*`, `Command::*`, `exec`, `os.system`, dialog APIs, archive extraction (zip-slip).
- **XSS** — `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `innerHTML`/`outerHTML`, Markdown→HTML serializers, template engines with auto-escape disabled.
- **Deserialization** — JSON/YAML/bincode/pickle boundaries; prototype pollution via `Object.assign(target, JSON.parse(...))`.
- **IPC payload validation** — do backends validate incoming args via typed structs or trust the caller?
- **Open-redirect / SSRF** — URL parameters reaching `fetch`/`reqwest`/`curl` without host allowlist.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** SQLi, XSS, command/path traversal, deserialization, IPC payload validation, SSRF.
- **Cross-cuts with:**
  - `prompt-injection-auditor` — when present in the roster, prompt injection belongs to that specialist; defer via `coverage.status=deferred-to-other-specialist`.
  - `secrets-crypto-auditor` — credentials reaching logs from validation bypasses are their scope.
  - `auth-authz-auditor` — authentication of input sources is theirs; validation of input content is yours.

## Brief (passed to the Agent)

> Review injection vectors in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Trace every external input to its sink. Axes: SQL injection (including FTS5 query-syntax), command/path traversal (zip-slip included), XSS, deserialization, IPC payload validation, SSRF/open-redirect. If `prompt-injection-auditor` is in the run's roster, prompt injection is theirs — defer.
>
> **Output contract (four files in `<TARGET>/.planning/security-review/`):** `input-validation-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Conform to `.claude/skills/sec-review-team/schema/{finding,coverage}.schema.json`. See the `auth-authz-auditor` brief for full file-format details — identical contract.
>
> **Prose format:** `[SEVERITY] <file>:<line> — <issue>\nExploit / Fix / Evidence`. Group by severity.
>
> **Confidence required on every finding** (`certain|likely|possible|unverified`). Negative findings (clean/N/A) require searches + search_limits in `coverage.jsonl`.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only (tools are allowlisted); cite evidence; no speculation; emit coverage for every category in your scope.
>
> Report: absolute paths of the four outputs + one-line severity count.

## Output files
- `input-validation-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`

## Allowed tools
Same as `auth-authz-auditor.md` allowed-tools section. `Write` scoped to this specialist's four output file paths only.

## Coverage categories this specialist owns
- `sql-injection`, `fts5-syntax-injection`, `command-injection`, `path-traversal`, `zip-slip`, `xss`, `deserialization`, `ipc-payload-validation`, `ssrf`, `open-redirect`
- If `prompt-injection-auditor` is not in roster: also `prompt-injection`

## Scanner integration
- `semgrep --config auto` — pattern-based catches. Triage scanner hits + find what rules miss.
- `bandit -r` (Python) — SAST for Python.
- `brakeman` (Ruby on Rails).

frontend-security-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Frontend / web-platform security.

  • CSP — header presence, strictness; absence of unsafe-inline, unsafe-eval, remote origins without nonce/hash.
  • CORS — Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * on credentialed endpoints, missing Vary: Origin.
  • Cookie flags — Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict/Lax on session cookies.
  • Subresource Integrity (SRI) — on <script src=> and <link> to third-party CDNs.
  • postMessage / BroadcastChannel — origin verification.
  • Clickjacking — X-Frame-Options / frame-ancestors CSP directive.
  • Third-party script hygiene — analytics, fonts, CDN scripts; async loading patterns.

Threat model

Owns the web-platform attack surface — CSP, CORS, SRI, cookie flags, postMessage, clickjacking — that data-exposure-auditor defers when this specialist is in the roster. Cross-cuts with privacy-telemetry-auditor on third-party script inclusion.

Coverage categories

csp, cors, cookie-flags, sri, postmessage-origin, clickjacking, third-party-scripts

prompt-injection-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

LLM / agentic security.

  • Untrusted input reaching tool-use arguments (direct or via system-prompt concat).
  • Prompt assembly via string concat — f"You are {role}. User: {msg}" patterns.
  • Missing output sanitization — LLM response rendered as HTML, executed as code, or used in SQL unvalidated.
  • Tool-use role confusion — tools giving the model power the user shouldn't have without auth gates.
  • Context poisoning — untrusted RAG ingests without provenance tracking.
  • Memory / skill / system-prompt injection — user content altering agent memory or next-turn system prompts.
  • Indirect prompt injection via external data (web pages, emails, PDFs, Slack) fetched into context.
  • Secret exfiltration via prompts — LLM instructed to echo env vars / config / other users' data.

Threat model

Trace every untrusted input to prompt-assembly sinks. Owns prompt injection when in roster; input-validation-auditor defers. Cross-cuts with data-exposure-auditor (LLM exfil) and secrets-crypto-auditor (leaked keys via prompt-echo).

Coverage categories

prompt-concat-untrusted, tool-use-role-confusion, output-sanitization, context-poisoning-rag, memory-skill-injection, indirect-injection, secret-exfil-via-prompt

Definition file

iac-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Infrastructure-as-code review.

  • Public-by-default resources (S3 buckets, RDS, blob storage) — ACL, bucket policy, encryption-at-rest.
  • Wildcards in IAM / RBAC (Action: "*", Resource: "*", cluster-admin bindings).
  • Missing encryption (TLS in transit, KMS at rest, SSM parameters marked SecureString).
  • Network policies — overly broad 0.0.0.0/0 ingress, missing egress restrictions.
  • Kubernetes pod security — runAsRoot, privileged, allowPrivilegeEscalation, missing readOnlyRootFilesystem, hostPID/hostNetwork/hostIPC.
  • Tagging / naming for audit trail.
  • Hardcoded credentials in *.tfvars, values.yaml, .env.tpl.

Threat model

Reviews Terraform / CloudFormation / Helm / Kustomize / plain-YAML K8s manifests for cluster-level resources and IAM correctness. Defers credentials-in-plain-config to secrets-crypto-auditor via cross-reference; coordinates pod-level security context with container-runtime-auditor.

Coverage categories

public-resources, iam-wildcards, encryption-at-rest, encryption-in-transit, network-policies, pod-security-standards, iac-hardcoded-credentials, tagging-audit-trail

Definition file

container-runtime-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Container and runtime security.

  • Dockerfile — FROM pinning (digest, not just tag), USER not root, COPY not ADD for local, --chown, no secrets in layers.
  • Base image freshness and known CVEs.
  • RUN shell-injection risk via build-args.
  • docker-compose.yml — privileged: true, exposed ports, volume mounts into /etc, /var/run/docker.sock.
  • Kubernetes pod spec — runAsRoot, allowPrivilegeEscalation, capabilities.add, missing readOnlyRootFilesystem, hostPID/hostNetwork/hostIPC.
  • Helm chart defaults that open ports or privileges.

Threat model

Owns Dockerfile, compose, pod spec, and Helm values. Coordinates with iac-auditor (cluster-level resources) and dependency-supplychain-auditor (base-image CVEs).

Coverage categories

dockerfile-hardening, base-image-cves, compose-privileges, pod-security-context, helm-default-exposure

ci-cd-security-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

CI/CD pipeline security.

  • GitHub Actions: pull_request_target on workflows that check out untrusted PR code (classic RCE pattern).
  • Third-party actions not SHA-pinned — prefer @<sha> over @v4.
  • permissions: block — default read-all; explicit escalation only where needed.
  • Secrets exposure — echo ${{ secrets.X }} in logs, env: pass-through to untrusted steps.
  • on: pull_request + secrets reachable from forks.
  • GitLab CI / CircleCI / Jenkins equivalents.
  • Artifact integrity — signed builds, SLSA provenance, cosign attestations.
  • Build-time dep fetch — curl | sh installs, unpinned deps in scripts.

Threat model

CI/CD pipeline trust boundaries — workflow triggers, action SHA-pinning, secrets exposure in logs and forks, permissions hardening, artifact integrity. Cross-cuts with dependency-supplychain-auditor (action-version supply chain) and secrets-crypto-auditor (leaked secrets in CI logs).

Coverage categories

workflow-triggers, action-pinning, permissions-block, secrets-in-ci, fork-secret-access, artifact-integrity, build-time-dep-fetch

concurrency-race-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

Race conditions and concurrency hazards with security impact.

  • TOCTOU: check-then-act without atomicity (if os.path.exists(path): open(path); auth read-then-act without transaction).
  • Double-check-locking anti-patterns.
  • Shared state without synchronization — globals mutated from async handlers, React state writes from overlapping effects.
  • Rate-limit bypass via concurrency — non-atomic counter read+increment.
  • Session fixation / replay — session IDs accepted before validation; concurrent login creating multiple session rows.
  • Transactional integrity — DB writes that should be atomic split across statements; UPDATE balance without row-level locking.
  • Rust Send/Sync misuse — unsafe impl Send/Sync, Arc<Mutex<_>> escape.
  • Async cancellation safety — futures leaving state half-modified if cancelled.

Threat model

Concurrency correctness in security-relevant code paths — money, permissions, sessions, rate limits. Cross-cuts with silent-failure-hunter (swallowed errors in race paths) and auth-authz-auditor (session races) via cross-reference.

Coverage categories

toctou, double-check-locking, shared-state-unsynced, rate-limit-race, session-race, transactional-integrity, async-cancellation

Definition file

privacy-telemetry-auditor stack-specific

▼

Primary scope

What the app phones home about, what data leaves, regulatory (GDPR/CCPA) signals.

  • Outbound data inventory — every fetch/XMLHttpRequest/reqwest/axios destination + payload. Does any include PII?
  • Analytics / tracking SDKs — GA, Segment, Mixpanel, PostHog, Sentry, FullStory. Opt-in vs opt-out. DNT / GPC respected. Load before consent gathered?
  • Cookie inventory — domain, expiry, flags, purpose; categorized as strictly-necessary / functional / analytics / marketing.
  • Storage inventory — localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, cache APIs; client-side PII with retention.
  • Third-party script / iframe inclusion — what domains load at page start; SRI usage.
  • Cross-border data transfer — servers / CDNs / logging endpoints in unexpected jurisdictions.
  • Consent flow — banner presence, actual gating of analytics/marketing SDKs (classic bug: banner shows but scripts load anyway).
  • Retention / deletion — DSAR flow, logs retention visible.
  • Desktop telemetry — Sentry, crash reporters, auto-update pings, product analytics.

Threat model

Owns "what deliberately leaves + regulatory"; data-exposure-auditor owns "what could leak". Catches the classic consent-banner bug where SDKs load before the user clicks accept.

Coverage categories

outbound-destinations, analytics-sdks, cookies, client-storage-pii, third-party-scripts, cross-border-transfer, consent-flow-gating, retention-dsar

Definition file

# privacy-telemetry-auditor

## Primary scope
