- Outbound data inventory — every `fetch`/`XMLHttpRequest`/`reqwest`/`axios` destination + payload. Does any include PII?
- Analytics / tracking SDKs — GA, Segment, Mixpanel, PostHog, Sentry, FullStory. Opt-in vs opt-out. DNT / GPC respected. Load before consent gathered?
- Cookie inventory — domain, expiry, flags, purpose; categorize as strictly-necessary / functional / analytics / marketing.
- Storage inventory — `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, `IndexedDB`, cache APIs; client-side PII with retention.
- Third-party script / iframe inclusion — what domains load at page start; SRI usage.
- Cross-border data transfer — servers / CDNs / logging endpoints in unexpected jurisdictions.
- Consent flow — banner presence, actual gating of analytics/marketing SDKs (classic bug: banner shows but scripts load anyway).
- Retention / deletion — DSAR flow, logs retention visible.
- Desktop telemetry — Sentry, crash reporters, auto-update pings, product analytics.

## Overlap with other specialists
- **Primary owner of:** analytics SDKs, consent, cross-border, regulatory framing.
- **Cross-cuts with:** `data-exposure-auditor` (data-exposure owns "what could leak"; you own "what deliberately leaves + regulatory").

## Brief

> Review privacy and telemetry in `<TARGET>` (scope: `<SCOPE>`). Stack: `<STACK CONTEXT>`. Inventory outbound data, analytics SDKs, cookies, storage, third-party scripts, consent flow, retention.
>
> **Output:** `privacy-telemetry-auditor.{md, findings.jsonl, coverage.jsonl, status.json}`. Standard format.
>
> **Hard rules:** read-only; cite evidence.

## Allowed tools
Standard read-only set. `Write` scoped to four outputs.

## Coverage categories
`outbound-destinations`, `analytics-sdks`, `cookies`, `client-storage-pii`, `third-party-scripts`, `cross-border-transfer`, `consent-flow-gating`, `retention-dsar`

## Scanner integration
- Manual: `npm ls` filter for analytics packages. Future: headless-chrome crawl for runtime cookie/storage enumeration.

Example Invocation

# Confirm + run against a full repo on Opus 4.7
/sec-review-team /path/to/repo all

# Review only the diff vs main (fast — finishes in minutes)
/sec-review-team /path/to/repo "diff vs main"

# Skip confirmation (for CI / scripted runs)
/sec-review-team /path/to/repo all --yes

# After the run, open fix PRs for high/certain findings:
/sec-review-fixes /path/to/repo/.planning/security-review/

The skill produces structured JSONL findings (one file per specialist) plus a human-readable REPORT.md. The JSONL conforms to schema/finding.schema.json and is consumable by CI, the /sec-review-fixes companion, and a future fixtures-based regression harness.

Legacy monolithic prompt (for reference / non-skill environments)

The original single-prompt form the library was factored from. Use this in environments where the skill isn't available — paste into Claude Code, replace <PATH> and the scope placeholder, and adjust the agent roster.

Spin up a security review team for the codebase at <PATH>. Scope: <all backend services | specific dirs | recent diff vs main>.

1. Create a team via TeamCreate named "sec-review" with a task list.

2. Spawn these specialists in parallel via the Agent tool, each with one focused task and writing findings to .planning/security-review/<agent>.md:

   - **auth-authz-auditor** (security-auditor): Review all auth flows, session handling, JWT/OAuth, RBAC, and privilege boundaries. Map every endpoint to its auth requirement.

   - **input-validation-auditor** (pr-review-toolkit:code-reviewer): Hunt injection vectors — SQLi, command injection, SSRF, path traversal, XSS, deserialization, prompt injection in LLM calls. Trace every external input to its sink.

   - **secrets-crypto-auditor** (feature-dev:code-reviewer): Find hardcoded secrets, weak crypto, insecure random, improper key/token storage, logged credentials, secrets in error messages.

   - **dependency-supplychain-auditor** (general-purpose): Audit dependencies for known CVEs (pip-audit / npm audit), pinned versions, lockfile integrity, suspicious packages, and CI/CD trust boundaries.

   - **silent-failure-hunter** (pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter): Catch swallowed exceptions, fallback paths that mask security failures, and error handling that fails open instead of closed.

   - **data-exposure-auditor** (code-reviewer): PII handling, log redaction, response shapes that leak internals, IDOR, missing tenant isolation, overly broad CORS.

3. Each agent must:
   - Read only files in scope
   - Produce findings as: [SEVERITY: critical/high/medium/low] file:line — issue — exploit scenario — recommended fix
   - Cite concrete evidence; no speculation
   - Mark its task complete via TaskUpdate when done

4. After all agents finish, you (lead) consolidate into .planning/security-review/REPORT.md grouped by severity, dedupe overlaps, and propose a fix order. Then shut down the team.

Use the code-review skill to frame the consolidation. Do NOT fix anything in this pass — review only.
How to Adapt

Roster adaptation is now automatic via stack-signals.md: the skill detects your stack (webapp / desktop / iac / llm-agent / container / etc.) and picks a roster. Override at the pre-run confirmation screen — drop or add specialists with a one-liner. For first-time setup, inspect the specialist library under skills/sec-review-team/specialists/ in the repo and the detection rules in stack-signals.md. Scope tightly — a diff-only review finishes in minutes; a full-repo sweep can take 20+ minutes on a large monorepo.

Install the Skill

The skills live in the fsad_playbook repo under skills/. Copy them into your project's .claude/skills/ directory (shared with the team) or ~/.claude/skills/ (global, personal). Once present, invoke with /sec-review-team <path> <scope> from any project.

Install via git sparse-checkout

# Fetch only the skills/ directory from the FSAD Playbook repo
git clone --depth=1 --filter=blob:none --sparse \
  https://github.com/tbeack/fsad_playbook.git /tmp/fsad-playbook
cd /tmp/fsad-playbook
git sparse-checkout set skills/sec-review-team skills/sec-review-fixes

# Copy to your project (team-wide):
cp -r skills/sec-review-team  /path/to/project/.claude/skills/
cp -r skills/sec-review-fixes /path/to/project/.claude/skills/
# or globally (personal use):
cp -r skills/sec-review-team  ~/.claude/skills/
cp -r skills/sec-review-fixes ~/.claude/skills/

rm -rf /tmp/fsad-playbook
11 — Building Your Own Skills

Turn Repeatable Workflows into Reusable Expert Modes

Skills are markdown directories that define specialized workflows. Claude loads them on demand — via slash commands or automatically by matching your task — providing consistency across sessions and team members.

What are Skills?

Skills are directories in .claude/skills/ containing a SKILL.md file plus optional supporting files (templates, scripts, reference docs). They turn tribal knowledge — code review checklists, deployment procedures, estimation frameworks — into repeatable, shareable workflows. Claude can auto-load skills by matching your task to skill descriptions, or you invoke them manually with /skill-name. Plugin skills use a namespace: /plugin:skill-name.

Skill File Anatomy

my-skill/
├── SKILL.md          # Required — skill content
├── reference.md      # Optional — detailed docs
├── examples.md       # Optional — usage examples
└── scripts/
    └── helper.py     # Optional — executable scripts
---
name: my-skill
description: Reviews code for security issues. Use when auditing code.
argument-hint: [file-or-directory]
allowed-tools: Read Grep Bash(git *)
disable-model-invocation: false
---

# My Skill

Review $ARGUMENTS for security issues.
Skill directory: ${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR}

## Process
1. First, do this
2. Then, check that
3. Finally, verify this

Frontmatter Reference (All Fields)

▼
FieldTypeDescription
namestringDisplay name and /slash-command. Defaults to directory name.
descriptionstringWhat the skill does. Claude uses this for auto-invocation matching. Front-load the key use case. Truncated at 1,536 chars (startup warning shown when exceeded).
argument-hintstringHint shown in autocomplete (e.g. [filename], [issue-number]).
disable-model-invocationbooleanIf true, only you can invoke via /name — Claude won't auto-load. Use for side effects (deploy, send).
user-invocablebooleanIf false, hidden from / menu. Claude can still auto-load. Use for background knowledge.
allowed-toolsstring/listTools Claude can use without prompting (e.g. Read Bash(grep:*) Bash(git *)). On native macOS/Linux builds, Grep and Glob are embedded in the Bash tool — use Bash(grep:*) and Bash(find:*) instead.
disallowed-toolsstring/listTools to remove from the model while this skill is active (e.g. WebSearch Bash). Inverse of allowed-tools — use to restrict capabilities for focused or safety-critical skills.
modelstringOverride model when skill is active (e.g. claude-opus-4-6).
effortenumOverride effort level: low | medium | high | xhigh | max. Overrides session default (high for Pro/Max on Opus 4.6/Sonnet 4.6; medium otherwise).
contextstringSet to fork to run in isolated subagent context with fresh context window.
agentstringSubagent type when context: fork (e.g. Explore, Plan).
hooksobjectHooks scoped to this skill's lifecycle (PreToolUse, PostToolUse, etc.). Also fires when the agent is run as a main-thread agent via --agent.
mcpServersobjectMCP server definitions scoped to this skill. Loaded for fork-subagent contexts and for main-thread agent sessions via --agent.
monitorsarrayBackground monitor definitions that auto-arm at session start or when the skill is invoked. Enables plugins to stream events without blocking the conversation. Note: in plugin manifest.json files, declare as "experimental": { "monitors": [...] } — top-level still works but triggers a claude plugin validate warning.
pathsstring/listGlob patterns limiting when skill auto-triggers (e.g. src/**/*.ts).

String substitutions: $ARGUMENTS (all args), $0 / $1 (specific args), ${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR} (skill directory), ${CLAUDE_SESSION_ID} (session ID), ${CLAUDE_EFFORT} (current effort level — lets a skill branch on low / medium / high / xhigh / max).

Project-Level

.claude/skills/skill-name/SKILL.md
Committed to repo. Shared with team.

Global

~/.claude/skills/skill-name/SKILL.md
Personal. Available across all projects.

Plugin Skills

Installed via /plugin. Namespaced as /plugin:skill-name.

Priority order: managed (enterprise) > user (global) > project > plugin

Skill Invocation

Manual

Type /skill-name [args] to invoke explicitly. Arguments available via $ARGUMENTS, $0, $1.

Automatic

Claude matches your task to skill descriptions and auto-loads the relevant skill. Control with disable-model-invocation.

Programmatic

Subagents and API integrations invoke skills via the Skill tool. Claude itself can also discover and invoke built-in commands (like /init, /review, /security-review) this way. Skills can also run in isolated context with context: fork.

Auto-Invocation Tip

Front-load your description with the key use case in the first 50 characters. Claude uses this to decide when to auto-load the skill. Vague descriptions = poor triggering.

Example Skills

Documentation Skill

▼

Auto-generates project documentation from codebase analysis.

---
name: generate-docs
description: Generate project documentation from codebase analysis
argument-hint: [module-or-directory]
allowed-tools: Read Grep Glob
---

# Documentation Generator

## Process
1. Read CLAUDE.md and architecture docs
2. Scan src/ for all public APIs and exported functions
3. For each module:
   - Extract function signatures and types
   - Read inline comments and JSDoc
   - Generate usage examples
4. Create docs/api-reference.md with:
   - Module index
   - Function signatures with descriptions
   - Code examples
5. Verify all links resolve correctly

Story Point Estimation Skill

▼

Estimates story points using Fibonacci scale based on complexity analysis.

---
name: estimate
description: Estimate story points for tasks using Fibonacci scale
---

# Story Point Estimation

## Scale
- 1: Trivial change, single file, < 30 min
- 2: Small change, 2-3 files, < 2 hours
- 3: Moderate, multiple files, half day
- 5: Significant, cross-module, full day
- 8: Large, architectural impact, 2-3 days
- 13: Epic-sized, needs decomposition

## Process
1. Read the task/story description
2. Identify affected files and modules
3. Assess: complexity, unknowns, dependencies, risk
4. Compare to reference stories if available
5. Provide estimate with reasoning:
   - Complexity factors
   - Risk factors
   - Suggested decomposition if > 8 points

Epic to Stories Skill

▼

Breaks down epics into implementable user stories with acceptance criteria.

---
name: epic-to-stories
description: Break down epics into implementable user stories
---

# Epic Decomposition

## Process
1. Read the epic description and acceptance criteria
2. Identify distinct user capabilities needed
3. For each capability, create a story:
   - Title: "As a [role], I can [action]"
   - Description: What and why
   - Acceptance criteria: Testable, specific
   - Dependencies: Which stories must complete first
4. Order stories by dependency graph
5. Flag stories that need design input
6. Estimate each story using Fibonacci scale

## Output Format
For each story:
### Story [N]: [Title]
**As a** [role] **I can** [action] **so that** [value]
**Acceptance Criteria:**
- [ ] Criterion 1
- [ ] Criterion 2
**Dependencies:** [Story refs]
**Estimate:** [points]

Code Review Skill

▼

Structured code review with security, performance, and style checks.

---
name: code-review
description: Structured code review for PRs and code changes
disable-model-invocation: true
allowed-tools: Read Grep Glob
---

# Code Review

## Security (Critical — block merge if failed)
- [ ] No SQL injection vectors
- [ ] No XSS vulnerabilities
- [ ] No secrets/credentials in code
- [ ] Input validation on all external boundaries
- [ ] Auth checks on all protected routes

## Logic
- [ ] Edge cases handled
- [ ] Error handling is appropriate (not swallowed)
- [ ] No race conditions in async code
- [ ] Data validation matches schema

## Quality
- [ ] Tests cover new code paths
- [ ] No dead code introduced
- [ ] Naming follows project conventions
- [ ] No unnecessary complexity

## Output
Provide: ✅ Approve, 🔄 Request Changes, or 🚫 Block
With specific line-level feedback for each issue.

Advanced Skill Patterns

▼

Shell injection — embed live data in skills with !`command` syntax:

---
name: pr-summary
description: Summarize the current PR diff and comments
disable-model-invocation: true
---

PR diff: !`gh pr diff`
Comments: !`gh pr view --comments`

Summarize the changes and highlight any concerns.

Context fork — run a skill in an isolated subagent context:

---
name: security-audit
description: Audit codebase for security vulnerabilities
context: fork
agent: Explore
---

Audit $ARGUMENTS for:
1. Injection vulnerabilities
2. Authentication bypasses
3. Data exposure risks

Tool pre-approval — skip permission prompts for expected operations:

allowed-tools: Read Grep Bash(git *) Bash(npm test)

Extended thinking — include "ultrathink" in skill content to enable deep reasoning.

Supporting files — organize templates, scripts, and reference docs alongside SKILL.md. Access the skill's directory with ${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR}.

Creating Skills with the Skill Creator

You don't have to write skills from scratch. Claude's built-in skill creator walks you through the process interactively.

1

Run the Skill Creator

Launch the interactive skill creator from any Claude Code session.

/skill-creator
2

Describe What You Want

Tell Claude what workflow to encode. Be specific about the domain and the outcome you want. The more context you provide, the better the skill.

"Create a skill for our staging deployment checklist —
it should verify tests pass, check environment variables,
run database migrations, deploy to staging, and run
smoke tests against the staging URL."
3

Iterate on the Draft

Claude generates a skill file and walks you through it. Review each section — refine the steps, add edge cases, tighten the checklist. Treat it like a code review.

Tip

Paste existing runbooks, wiki pages, or checklists into the conversation. Claude will extract the workflow and structure it as a skill automatically.

4

Save and Test

The skill creator saves the directory to .claude/skills/. Test it immediately by invoking the slash command.

# Your new skill is ready to use
/deploy-staging

# List all available skills
/skills

# Iterate on changes without restarting
/reload-plugins
5

Share with Your Team

Commit project-level skills to the repo so every teammate gets them automatically. Keep personal utilities in your global skills folder.

Project-Level (Shared)

.claude/skills/deploy-staging/SKILL.md
Committed to repo. Whole team benefits.

Global (Personal)

~/.claude/skills/my-utils/SKILL.md
Available across all your projects.

Skills vs Commands vs Agents

TypeWhat It IsWhen to Use
SkillMarkdown directory with SKILL.md. Loaded on demand or auto-invoked.Reusable workflows, checklists, domain knowledge. The default choice.
AgentIsolated worker with its own context window and tool access.Parallel work, research, tasks that need context isolation.
Command (legacy)File in .claude/commands/. Replaced by skills.Migrate to .claude/skills/ — commands still work but skills are the standard.

Skills can run in agent context with context: fork — giving you the reusability of skills with the isolation of agents.

Best Practice

Start with one skill for your most repeated workflow. Add more as patterns emerge. A team of 3-4 engineers typically needs 5-8 shared skills to cover their common workflows.

12 — Best Practices & Guidelines

Lessons from Real-World FSAD Adoption

Practical guidance for teams adopting spec-driven agentic development.

Background Context Matters

Avoid over-emphasis on technical capabilities. Insufficient business context hurts initial coding sessions. A holistic approach beyond engineering-only specifications yields best results. Include background details, business context, problems to be solved, and design systems.

Prompting

Be Specific About What, Not How

Tell Claude what outcome you want, not the implementation steps. Let the agent choose the approach based on codebase context.

Provide Business Context

Include business requirements, constraints, and existing patterns. "We need a user preferences API because mobile users complain about notification noise" is better than "add a PATCH endpoint."

Use @ References

Point Claude at specific files with @filename. This ensures the right context is loaded and prevents the agent from searching in the wrong places.

Use Plan Mode First

For complex features, start with --conversation-mode plan. Let Claude analyze and propose before making changes. Review the plan, then execute.

Project Structure

Keep CLAUDE.md Concise

Under 100 lines. If it's too long, Claude may miss critical rules. Move detailed docs to docs/ for progressive loading.

Use Local CLAUDE.md Files

Place module-specific rules near the code they protect. src/auth/CLAUDE.md gets loaded when Claude works in that directory.

Organize docs/ for Progressive Context

Architecture overview, ADRs, and runbooks in docs/. Claude reads them when relevant, not every session.

Version Control Your Prompts

Specs, skills, and configuration are code. Commit them. Review changes in PRs. Roll back if something breaks.

Workflow

Use /clear Between Tasks

Reset context between unrelated tasks. Stale context from a previous task can mislead agents on the current one.

Use /compact When Context Gets Large

Compress conversation history to free up context window space for the current task. Especially useful in long sessions.

Commit Frequently

Small, atomic changes. Each commit should represent one logical unit of work. Makes it easy to revert if an agent goes off track.

Review Agent Output Before Shipping

Agents produce consistent code from specs, but they can hallucinate or over-interpret. Human review is non-negotiable.

Common Pitfalls

Jumping Straight to Code

Without a spec, agents guess at requirements. The result is wasted cycles and code that doesn't match what was needed.

Technical Specs Without Business Context

"Add a PATCH endpoint" tells the agent what to build but not why. Missing context leads to wrong trade-offs.

Not Using Plan Mode

For complex features, jumping straight to implementation means no opportunity to catch misunderstandings before they become code.

Ignoring Context Window Limits

Overloading a session with too many files or too large a scope causes the agent to lose track of requirements mid-task.

Not Reviewing Agent Code

Agents are powerful but non-deterministic. Code that passes tests can still have logic errors, security gaps, or architectural drift.

Bloated CLAUDE.md

Stuffing every rule into CLAUDE.md defeats the purpose. Keep it focused on high-level rules; use docs/ and local CLAUDE.md for details.

12.5 — Model & Effort

Model & Effort

Pick the right model tier for the task, combine them with opusplan, and tune reasoning depth with /effort.

Match model capability to task complexity. Claude Code auto-selects a sensible default, but the best coding results in 2026 come from combining all three tiers — not picking one for everything.

Current model lineup

Capability Opus 4.7 Sonnet 4.6 Haiku 4.5
PositioningFrontier reasoning + agentic codingBalanced daily driverFast, near-frontier
API IDclaude-opus-4-7claude-sonnet-4-6claude-haiku-4-5
Input / Output (per MTok)$5 / $25$3 / $15$1 / $5
Context window1M tokens1M tokens200K tokens
Max output128K64K64K
Adaptive thinkingYes (default xhigh)YesNo (extended thinking only)
Relative latencyModerateFastFastest
Best forLong-horizon agentic coding, large refactors, architecture, RCA80%+ of day-to-day coding, tests, tool useSub-agent work, batch reviews, mechanical edits

Claude Code model aliases

AliasResolves toWhen to use
defaultTier-dependent (Max / Team Premium → Opus 4.7; Pro / Team Std / Enterprise / API → Sonnet 4.6; Bedrock / Vertex / Foundry → Sonnet 4.5)Daily driver if unsure
best / opusLatest Opus (4.7 on API; 4.6 on Bedrock / Vertex / Foundry)Complex reasoning, architecture, gnarly bugs
sonnetLatest Sonnet (4.6 on API; 4.5 on Bedrock / Vertex / Foundry)80%+ of coding tasks
haikuLatest HaikuCheap sub-agent / background work
opus[1m] / sonnet[1m]1M context variantLong-session work across large codebases
opusplanOpus during plan mode → Sonnet during executionRecommended default for non-trivial feature work — ~40% cheaper than pure Opus
 
# Recommended: let Opus plan, Sonnet execute
claude --model opusplan

# Force a specific model mid-session
/model opus         # Switch to latest Opus
/model sonnet       # Switch to latest Sonnet
/model haiku        # Switch to latest Haiku
/model opus[1m]     # 1M context variant for large codebases

# Route sub-agents to Haiku (single biggest cost lever after opusplan)
export CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=haiku

# Set effort level (controls reasoning depth)
/effort max          # Maximum reasoning — complex agentic tasks
/effort xhigh        # Opus 4.7 only — between high and max
/effort high         # Deep thinking — difficult coding problems
/effort medium       # Balanced (default) — speed + quality
/effort low          # Quick responses — simple tasks, subagents
/effort              # No args: opens interactive slider (arrow keys + Enter)
claude --effort max   # Set via CLI flag

# Toggle fast mode (same model, faster output)
/fast on

# Auto-fallback when primary model not found (interactive + print mode)
claude -p --fallback-model sonnet "query"

Best-practice combinations by workload

WorkloadPlanExecuteSub-agents
Greenfield feature, multi-fileOpus 4.7 (xhigh)Sonnet 4.6Haiku 4.5
Targeted bug fix (known file)Sonnet 4.6Sonnet 4.6Haiku 4.5
Architectural refactor (10+ files)Opus 4.7 (max, one-shot)Opus 4.7 or Sonnet 4.6Haiku 4.5
Boilerplate / rename / formatting—Haiku 4.5—
High-volume code review / lint triage—Haiku 4.5 (Batch API)—
Long-session monorepo explorationOpus 4.7 [1m] or Sonnet [1m]Sonnet 4.6 [1m]Haiku 4.5
Production incident / RCAOpus 4.7 (max)Opus 4.7Sonnet 4.6
Cost Optimization Checklist
  • Default to opusplan for coding — ~40% cheaper than pure Opus.
  • Route sub-agents to Haiku via CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=haiku.
  • Enable prompt caching — cache hits are 10% of base input (up to 90% off on repeated system prompts, CLAUDE.md, large docs).
  • Use the Batch API for any non-interactive job — 50% off input and output; stacks with caching.
  • Pin effort to medium on cost-sensitive Sonnet workflows; raise only where needed.
  • Budget for up to 35% more tokens when migrating from Opus 4.6 → 4.7 (new tokenizer).
Key Dates (Apr 2026)
  • April 23, 2026 — Default model for Enterprise PAYG + Anthropic API switches to Opus 4.7.
  • June 15, 2026 — Sonnet 4 (claude-sonnet-4-20250514) and Opus 4 (claude-opus-4-20250514) retire. Migrate to Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7.
  • On Bedrock / Vertex / Foundry, pin model versions explicitly (ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_OPUS_MODEL, etc.) — alias targets move under you.
Default Effort Is Tier-Dependent

As of v2.1.117, the default effort level depends on your subscription tier and model: Pro and Max subscribers on Opus 4.6 or Sonnet 4.6 default to high. All other tiers and models default to medium. The setting is sticky — it persists across sessions. For complex agentic work, architecture decisions, and multi-file changes, use /effort max. If you've changed effort previously, verify you're on the latest Claude Code version and check that your CLAUDE.md and /memory don't have conflicting effort instructions.

Adaptive thinking & effort

On Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6, adaptive thinking is the recommended mode. Instead of manually setting a thinking token budget, Claude dynamically decides when and how much to think based on task complexity. The /effort level is the primary lever that controls thinking depth:

EffortThinking Behavior
maxAlways thinks, no constraints on depth — deepest reasoning
xhighOpus 4.7 only — deeper than high, lighter than max; falls back to high on other models
highAlmost always thinks — deep reasoning on complex tasks
mediumModerate thinking — may skip for simple queries
lowMinimal thinking — skips for simple tasks, prioritizes speed

Toggle extended thinking with Option+T / Alt+T. Setting effort to low effectively minimizes thinking; use this for simple lookups or subagent tasks where speed matters most.

budget_tokens Is Deprecated

The manual budget_tokens parameter for extended thinking is deprecated on Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 and will be removed in a future release. Use adaptive thinking with the effort parameter instead. Older models (Sonnet 4.5, Opus 4.5) still require the manual budget_tokens approach.

13 — Claude Cheat Sheet

Everything You Need in One Place

Keyboard shortcuts, commands, flags, hooks, and configuration reference.

Keyboard Shortcuts

KeyAction
EnterSend message / submit
EscInterrupt / stop generation
Esc EscRewind / restore previous state
Shift+TabCycle permission mode: default → acceptEdits → plan → auto
Ctrl+LForce screen redraw (does not clear input — readline behavior as of v2.1.126)
Ctrl+CCancel current operation (hard stop)
Ctrl+RSearch prompt history across all projects (default). Press Ctrl+S to narrow to the current project or session.
Ctrl+SNarrow Ctrl+R history picker to the current project or session only
Ctrl+TToggle task list. In claude agents view: pin/unpin the selected background session — pinned sessions stay alive when idle and are restarted in place on Claude Code updates.
Ctrl+OToggle normal / verbose transcript
Ctrl+BBackground running tasks
Ctrl+DExit Claude Code
Ctrl+GOpen prompt in external text editor
TabAccept autocomplete suggestion

Ctrl+KDelete from cursor to end of line

Slash Commands

Session, context & history

CommandDescription
/compact [focus]Compress context to save tokens. Optional: tell it what to keep.
/contextVisualize context usage with optimization suggestions
/clearClear conversation history (aliases: /reset, /new)
/resume [session]Resume conversation by ID or name (alias: /continue). Offers to summarize stale large sessions before re-reading them. Paste a GitHub, GitHub Enterprise, GitLab, or Bitbucket PR URL into the search box to jump directly to the session that created that PR. Background sessions started via --bg or agent view now appear in the picker, marked with bg.
/rename [name]Rename session or auto-generate a name
/recapReplay session context — useful when returning to a long-running session. Configurable in /config. Force on: CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_AWAY_SUMMARY=1; opt out: =0 (also disables the default-on behavior on Bedrock / Vertex / Foundry).
/rewindRewind to previous checkpoint (aliases: /undo, /checkpoint). The Rewind menu also offers Summarize up to here — compress earlier context while preserving recent turns, without rolling back state.
/exportExport conversation to file or clipboard
/copy [N]Copy last response to clipboard (or Nth-last)
/diffOpen interactive diff viewer. In the detail view, scroll with arrow keys, j/k, PgUp/PgDn, Space, Home/End (v2.1.149).

Model, mode & usage

CommandDescription
/modelSelect or change AI model for the current session only. Press d in the picker to set a default for new sessions. Startup header shows when the active model comes from a project or managed-settings pin. When ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL points at a compatible gateway, set CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_GATEWAY_MODEL_DISCOVERY=1 to list gateway models in the picker (opt-in as of v2.1.129).
/effort [level]Set effort level: low | medium | high | xhigh (Opus 4.7) | max. Call with no args for interactive slider. Default: high for Pro/Max on Opus 4.6/Sonnet 4.6; medium for all other tiers/models.
/fast [on|off]Toggle fast mode (same model, faster output)
/plan [desc]Enter plan mode with optional starting description
/focusToggle focus view (hides system status lines for a cleaner reading experience)
/permissionsView & edit tool permissions (alias: /allowed-tools)
/usageUnified usage dashboard with three tabs: session cost & token usage, plan usage & rate limits, and daily usage streaks. Includes a per-category breakdown showing what's driving limits usage — skills, subagents, plugins, and per-MCP-server cost (v2.1.149). /cost and /stats remain as typing shortcuts that open the relevant tab.
/usage-creditsView usage credits balance (alias: /extra-usage). Added in v2.1.144.

Permission Modes

Default

Claude asks permission for every tool use. Safest mode. Good for unfamiliar codebases.

Accept Edits

Auto-accepts file reads and edits. Still prompts for shell commands. Good middle ground.

Plan Mode

Read-only. Claude ONLY reads & plans. Won't write or edit files. Perfect for spec creation and analysis.

Auto-Accept

No prompts. Fastest execution. Use when you trust the agent and want maximum speed.

Bypass Permissions

Requires --dangerously-skip-permissions flag. Bypasses prompts for .claude/, .git/, .vscode/, and shell config files. Catastrophic removal commands still prompt. CI/CD and trusted automation only.

Cycle with Shift+Tab: default → acceptEdits → plan → auto → (bypassPermissions if enabled)

Hooks — Event Automation (27 Events)

Core hooks

HookWhen It Fires
SessionStartWhen a session begins or resumes. Return reloadSkills: true in JSON output to re-scan skill directories (skills installed by the hook become available immediately). Set session title via hookSpecificOutput.sessionTitle.
SessionEndWhen a session ends
InstructionsLoadedAfter CLAUDE.md and settings are loaded
UserPromptSubmitBefore your message is processed
StopWhen Claude finishes its complete response. Hook input includes background_tasks and session_crons fields listing active background work and scheduled crons.
StopFailureWhen Claude's response fails
MessageDisplayBefore assistant message text is displayed — hooks can transform or hide the output

Tool & permission hooks

HookWhen It Fires
PreToolUseBefore EVERY tool use — check, audit, intercept
PostToolUseAfter EVERY tool use — lint, format, auto-fix
PostToolUseFailureAfter a tool call fails
PermissionRequestWhen a permission prompt is shown
PermissionDeniedAfter a permission is denied in auto mode

HookWhen It Fires

SubagentStopWhen a subagent finishes. Hook input includes background_tasks and session_crons fields.
TaskCreatedWhen a task is created via TaskCreate
TaskCompletedWhen a task finishes
TeammateIdleWhen a teammate agent is idle

Background

Ctrl+B backgrounds running tasks so you can start another.

14 — Claude Power Usage

Advanced Features for Power Users

Agent teams, worktrees, remote control, batch operations, voice, plugins, and more.

Agent Teams

▼

Multiple Claude instances collaborating on the same codebase. Each agent works independently with its own context, but they share the same repo and can coordinate through files.

  • Frontend + backend agents working in parallel
  • Writer + reviewer pattern: one implements, another reviews
  • Research agent gathers context while implementation agent builds
# Spawn a teammate in its own worktree + tmux pane
claude -w feature-auth --tmux

# Or run teammates in-process (no tmux needed)
claude --teammate-mode in-process

# Reference named agents with @ in your prompt
# Team leader's model and permissions cascade to teammates
🔗
Agent Teams Documentation
Official docs on configuring and using agent teams

Work Trees

▼

Isolated git worktrees for parallel work. Each agent gets its own branch and working directory. Changes merge back when complete.

# Start Claude in a new worktree
claude -w feature-name

# Pair with tmux for side-by-side terminals
claude -w feature-name --tmux

# Each worktree is a full repo checkout
# Agents can work simultaneously without conflicts
# Changes are merged back via standard git merge/PR

worktree.baseRef setting — controls the branch base for all worktrees (--worktree, EnterWorktree, and agent-isolation worktrees). Set in .claude/settings.json:

ValueBranches fromWhen to use
fresh (default)origin/<default-branch>Clean base — agents always start from the canonical remote state, no local noise
headLocal HEADShare unpushed scaffolding — spawned agents inherit commits you haven't pushed yet

worktree.bgIsolation setting — controls whether background sessions run in an isolated worktree or edit the working copy directly. Set in .claude/settings.json:

ValueBehaviorWhen to use
worktree (default)Background sessions run in an isolated git worktree via EnterWorktreeMost repos — prevents conflicts with your working copy
noneBackground sessions edit the working copy directly, no worktree createdMonorepos with non-relocatable build artifacts or tools that cannot run from a worktree path
Tip

Worktrees are automatically cleaned up when the agent completes with no changes. If changes were made, the worktree path and branch are returned. WorktreeCreate and WorktreeRemove hooks fire on lifecycle events.

Hooks

▼

Hooks are event-driven shell commands, HTTP endpoints, prompts, or agents that fire automatically at 26 lifecycle points. Use them for linting, formatting, security enforcement, and observability.

Hooks Deep Dive

See the dedicated Hooks section for the full lifecycle, all 5 hook types, configuration guide, exit code semantics, and practical recipes.

Session Logs

▼

Review what Claude did in past sessions. Useful for auditing agent behavior and learning from successful patterns.

  • Location: ~/.claude/projects/ (session transcripts)
  • Each session is logged with timestamps
  • Includes tool calls, file changes, and conversation flow
  • Useful for debugging "what did the agent do while I was away?"
  • Use /export to save a conversation to file or clipboard
  • Press Ctrl+O to toggle normal / verbose transcript; use /focus to toggle focus view

/insights — Workflow Analysis

▼

Generates a local HTML report analyzing your last 30 days of coding sessions.

  • Report location: ~/.claude/usage-data/report.html
  • Identifies workflow friction and recurring patterns
  • Actionable feedback on prompting effectiveness
  • Tracks which tools and models you use most
# Just type in Claude:
/insights

# Opens a local HTML report in your browser

/loop — Built-in Scheduler (alias: /proactive)

▼

Run commands on a recurring basis. Session-scoped — terminal must stay active.

# Check for new issues every hour
/loop 1h check for new Git issues and triage them

# Run tests every 30 minutes and fix failures
/loop 30m check if the codebase passes all tests, if not, fix it

# Monitor deployment status
/loop 15m check the deploy pipeline status and notify me of failures
Cancel a Wakeup

Press Esc while a /loop is counting down to cancel the pending wakeup without ending the session.

For Persistence

Session-scoped loops stop when the terminal closes. For persistent cloud scheduling, use /schedule to create cron-based tasks that run without an active terminal.

Remote Control & Cross-Device

▼

Control your terminal Claude session from the web, or move sessions between devices seamlessly.

# Enable remote control from claude.ai
/remote-control
/remote-control "My Project"   # With custom name

# Create a web session directly
claude --remote "Fix the login bug"

# Pull a web session into your terminal
/teleport
  • /desktop — continue session in the Claude Code desktop app (macOS/Windows)
  • /mobile — show QR code to continue on mobile
  • Bridge sessions display your local git repo, branch, and working directory

Chrome Integration

▼

Claude can interact with web pages in a Chrome browser — navigate, fill forms, click buttons, extract data, and take screenshots.

# Enable Chrome integration
claude --chrome

# Or configure via slash command
/chrome

# Disable when not needed
claude --no-chrome
Use Cases

Debug frontend issues by viewing the page directly, verify UI changes after edits, scrape documentation, or test form flows end-to-end.

Batch Operations

▼

Orchestrate large-scale changes across your entire codebase with 5–30 parallel agents working simultaneously.

# Rename a function across the entire codebase
/batch rename all instances of getUserData to fetchUserProfile

# Add error handling to all API endpoints
/batch add try-catch error handling to every Express route handler

# Migrate imports across hundreds of files
/batch update all imports from lodash to lodash-es

Each agent works on a subset of files independently. Changes are coordinated and merged back. Best for mechanical, repetitive changes across many files.

Plugins

▼

Extend Claude Code with community and first-party plugins that add skills, commands, tools, and integrations.

# Browse and install plugins
/plugin

# Reload plugins without restarting Claude
/reload-plugins

# Remove orphaned auto-installed plugin dependencies
claude plugin prune
claude plugin uninstall my-plugin --prune  # uninstall + cascade

# Load plugins from a directory or .zip archive
claude --plugin-dir ./my-plugins
claude --plugin-dir ./my-plugin.zip  # .zip archives also accepted

# Fetch and load a plugin from a URL (current session only)
claude --plugin-url https://example.com/my-plugin.zip

# Disable a plugin (blocked if another enabled plugin depends on it — shows chain hint)
claude plugin disable my-plugin

# Enable a plugin (auto-enables all transitive dependencies)
claude plugin enable my-plugin
  • Plugins add skills invokable via /skill-name
  • Executables under bin/ are invokable as bare commands
  • Plugin marketplace for discovery and one-click install
  • Root-level skill shortcut: a plugin with a top-level SKILL.md and no skills/ subdirectory is automatically surfaced as a skill — no directory structure required for single-skill plugins
  • claude plugin details <name> shows the plugin's component inventory: skills, MCP servers, LSP servers, and projected per-session token cost. The marketplace browse and discover panes now show a full component preview (commands, agents, skills, hooks, MCP/LSP servers) before installation, along with per-turn and per-invocation token estimates and when each plugin was last updated — useful for comparing plugin overhead and freshness before committing.
  • Reserved MCP server name: workspace is reserved — an mcpServers entry named workspace will be skipped with a warning at startup; rename it to avoid silent breakage
  • Default folder override warning: if plugin.json explicitly sets a key (e.g. "commands") that matches a default component folder, that default folder is silently skipped. Claude Code v2.1.140 now warns when this happens — visible in /doctor, claude plugin list, and /plugin. If the override is intentional, no action is needed.
  • Dependency enforcement: claude plugin disable is blocked when another enabled plugin depends on the target — the error includes a copy-pasteable chain command showing what to disable first. claude plugin enable automatically force-enables all transitive dependencies.

MCP server alwaysLoad option — set "alwaysLoad": true in an MCP server config to make all its tools skip tool-search deferral and stay permanently available. Useful for servers whose tools you use in nearly every session.

{
  "mcpServers": {
    "my-server": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": ["my-mcp-server"],
      "alwaysLoad": true
    }
  }
}

Monitor and optimize your context window to keep sessions productive as they grow.

# Visualize context usage with optimization tips

/compact

# Auto-compact via hooks when context gets large

  • /compact with a focus string preserves relevant context while trimming the rest
  • Status line can display live context usage with a color-coded progress bar

Monitor Tool — Background Script Streaming

▼

The Monitor tool streams stdout events from a script started with Bash(run_in_background: true). Each line of output arrives as a notification, letting Claude observe long-running processes without blocking the conversation.

# Ask Claude to run tests in the background and watch them
"Run the test suite in the background and tell me when done"
# Claude uses Bash(run_in_background: true) to launch tests,
# then Monitor streams each stdout line back as it arrives
  • Observe CI pipelines, build scripts, and dev servers without blocking
  • React to output in real time — fail fast on errors, summarize on completion
  • Pairs with Ctrl+B for manually backgrounded tasks

Subprocess Sandboxing

▼

Claude Code supports OS-level sandboxing that restricts what Bash subprocesses can read, write, and reach on the network — enforced by Seatbelt on macOS and bubblewrap on Linux / WSL2. Run /sandbox to enable it and choose a mode. Linux and WSL2 users must first install the required packages: sudo apt-get install bubblewrap socat (Ubuntu/Debian) or sudo dnf install bubblewrap socat (Fedora).

Sandbox modes

ModeBehavior
Auto-allowSandboxed bash commands run automatically without approval prompts. Commands that cannot be sandboxed (e.g. need access to non-allowed network hosts) fall back to the normal permission flow. rm targeting critical paths still prompts.
Regular permissionsAll bash commands go through the standard permission flow even when sandboxed — more control, more prompts. The sandbox still enforces the same filesystem and network boundaries.

Key settings.json keys

Setting keyEffect
sandbox.enabledEnable sandboxing (true / false). Equivalent to using /sandbox interactively.
sandbox.filesystem.allowWriteArray of paths (absolute, ~/, or ./ relative to project) that subprocess commands may write to outside the working directory — e.g. ["~/.kube", "/tmp/build"]. Arrays merge across config scopes.
sandbox.filesystem.denyReadBlock subprocess read access to specified paths — e.g. ["~/"] to prevent reading outside the project.
sandbox.filesystem.allowReadRe-allow reads within a denyRead region — e.g. ["."] to permit the current project while blocking the rest of ~/.
sandbox.network.allowedDomainsAllowlist of domains Bash subprocesses may reach. New domain requests trigger a permission prompt; use allowManagedDomainsOnly: true to block unapproved domains automatically.
sandbox.network.deniedDomainsBlock specific domains even when an allowedDomains wildcard would otherwise permit them — useful for blocking telemetry or third-party endpoints in CI.
sandbox.failIfUnavailableSet to true to make sandbox startup failure a hard error instead of a warning — intended for managed deployments that require sandboxing as a security gate.
sandbox.bwrapPath(Linux/WSL only) Custom path to the bwrap (bubblewrap) binary — use when bubblewrap is installed in a non-standard location on managed systems or container base images.
sandbox.socatPath(Linux/WSL only) Custom path to the socat binary — use when socat is installed in a non-standard location on managed systems or container base images.
sandbox.allowUnsandboxedCommandsSet to false to disable the escape hatch — Claude can no longer retry failed commands outside the sandbox. All commands must be sandboxed or listed in excludedCommands.

Hardening env vars (shared environments & CI/CD)

Env VarEffect
CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB=1Enables PID namespace isolation on Linux — sandboxed commands can't see or signal processes outside their namespace
CLAUDE_CODE_SCRIPT_CAPS=NLimits total script invocations per session to N — prevents runaway automation
CLAUDE_CODE_HIDE_CWD=1Hides the working directory path from the startup logo — useful in CI/CD and shared environments where paths should not be exposed
DISABLE_UPDATES=1Completely blocks all update paths including manual claude update. Stricter than DISABLE_AUTOUPDATER (which only prevents auto-updates). Use in managed/enterprise environments to pin a specific version.
CLAUDE_CODE_PACKAGE_MANAGER_AUTO_UPDATE=1On Homebrew or WinGet installations, runs the package manager upgrade in the background and prompts to restart when a new version is ready. Complement to DISABLE_UPDATES — use when you want managed automatic updates.
ANTHROPIC_BEDROCK_SERVICE_TIERSelect Bedrock service tier: default | flex | priority. Sent as the X-Amzn-Bedrock-Service-Tier header. Use priority for provisioned-capacity deployments.
CLAUDE_CODE_FORCE_SYNC_OUTPUT=1Force-enables synchronized output mode for terminals where auto-detection fails (e.g. Emacs eat). Fixes garbled or interleaved output in unsupported terminal emulators.
CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_ALTERNATE_SCREEN=1Opts out of the fullscreen alternate-screen renderer — keeps the conversation in the terminal's native scrollback buffer instead. Useful in tmux, GNU screen, or CI environments where alternate-screen mode interferes with log capture.
CLAUDE_CODE_PLUGIN_PREFER_HTTPSClone GitHub plugin sources over HTTPS instead of SSH — use in environments where a GitHub SSH key is unavailable (CI/CD containers, managed desktops, devcontainers).
ANTHROPIC_WORKSPACE_IDWorkload identity federation — scopes the minted auth token to a specific workspace when a federation rule covers more than one. Set in managed enterprise deployments using identity federation for authentication.
CLAUDE_CODE_OPUS_4_6_FAST_MODE_OVERRIDE=1Pin fast mode (/fast / Option+O) to Opus 4.6 instead of the v2.1.142+ default of Opus 4.7.
CLAUDE_CODE_POWERSHELL_RESPECT_EXECUTION_POLICY=1Opts out of the default -ExecutionPolicy Bypass flag Claude Code passes to PowerShell. Use in environments with enforced PowerShell execution policy (e.g. AllSigned or RemoteSigned) where bypass is not permitted.
CLAUDE_CODE_USE_POWERSHELL_TOOL=0Disables the PowerShell tool entirely. The tool is now enabled by default on Windows for Bedrock, Vertex, and Foundry users — use this opt-out in environments where PowerShell access is prohibited.
CLAUDE_CODE_STOP_HOOK_BLOCK_CAP=NMaximum number of consecutive stop-hook blocks before the turn ends with a warning. Default: 8. Raise the cap if your stop hook legitimately needs more retry cycles; set to 1 to fail fast.
Best practice

Start with /sandbox in Auto-allow mode and a narrow allowedDomains list. Add allowWrite paths only for tools that genuinely need them (e.g. ~/.kube for kubectl). Use deniedDomains to close loopholes left by broad wildcards, and set allowUnsandboxedCommands: false in managed environments where no escape hatch is acceptable.

Prompt Caching & KV Cache

▼

Claude maintains a key-value (KV) cache of computed attention states for the leading prefix of each conversation. When a new request shares the same prefix as a cached one, Claude skips recomputing that prefix — delivering faster responses at roughly 10% of the regular input-token cost for cached tokens.

The cache is prefix-based: content must appear at the beginning of the prompt to be cacheable. The first matching request in a window incurs a small cache-write overhead; every subsequent request within the TTL window is a cheap cache read.

What gets cached in a Claude Code session

  • System prompt injected by Claude Code at session start
  • CLAUDE.md files loaded from the project and user config
  • Large read-only documents attached at the top of the conversation
  • Accumulated conversation history prefix (up to the last assistant turn)

TTL configuration

Env VarEffect
ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1Opt into 1-hour prompt cache TTL (API key, Bedrock, Vertex, Foundry). Improves hit rate for long or repeated sessions.
FORCE_PROMPT_CACHING_5M=1Force 5-minute TTL regardless of other settings.

Choosing your TTL

TTLBest for
5 min (default)Short one-off sessions; acceptable when sessions rarely outlast 5 minutes; lowest storage overhead.
1 hourLong coding sessions, nightly CI pipelines, any workflow where Claude re-reads the same large codebase context repeatedly. Enable whenever a session is likely to outlast 5 minutes or runs on a schedule.

Persisting your TTL setting

Set the env var once in a .env file at the project root — Claude Code reads this file automatically at startup:

# .env  (project root — add to .gitignore)
ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1

For a global default across all projects, export from your shell profile (~/.zshrc or ~/.bashrc):

export ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H=1
Structuring Prompts for Maximum Cache Hits
  • Stable content first — system prompt, CLAUDE.md, and large reference docs must form a leading prefix; dynamic content (task description, user message) goes last.
  • Multi-user / CI deployments — use --exclude-dynamic-system-prompt-sections to strip per-user dynamic injections so the shared system-prompt prefix is cacheable across all users.
  • Observe cache activity — OTEL events include cacheRead and cacheCreation token breakdowns under claude_code.token.usage so you can confirm cache hits in your monitoring.
15 — Hooks

Hooks — Automation & Guardrails

Automate workflows, enforce policies, and extend Claude Code with event-driven hooks. Models forget. Hooks don't.

Hooks are user-defined shell commands, HTTP endpoints, LLM prompts, or agents that execute automatically at specific points in Claude Code's lifecycle. They fire deterministically — unlike instructions in CLAUDE.md that Claude may or may not follow, hooks always execute when their event triggers.

Hook Lifecycle

▼

Hooks fire at specific stages of Claude Code's execution. Understanding the lifecycle helps you choose the right event for your hook.

SessionStart
  │
  ├── [Per-turn loop]
  │     UserPromptSubmit
  │       │
  │       ├── [Agentic loop]
  │       │     PreToolUse → PermissionRequest → Tool executes
  │       │       │                                   │
  │       │       │                          PostToolUse / PostToolUseFailure
  │       │       │
  │       │     SubagentStart → SubagentStop
  │       │     TaskCreated → TaskCompleted
  │       │
  │       Stop / StopFailure
  │
  ├── [Async events]
  │     FileChanged, CwdChanged, ConfigChange
  │     WorktreeCreate / WorktreeRemove
  │     PreCompact / PostCompact
  │     Notification
  │
  SessionEnd
26 Hook Events

See the Cheat Sheet for the full reference table of all 26 hook events organized by category.

Five Hook Types

▼

Each hook type suits different use cases. Most teams start with command hooks and add others as needs grow.

TypeHow It WorksBest For
commandRuns a shell command. Receives JSON on stdin, returns results via exit code + stdout.Linting, formatting, blocking unsafe commands, logging
httpSends JSON POST to an endpoint. Headers support $ENV_VAR interpolation.Remote validation, audit logging, webhook integrations
promptSends a single-turn prompt to Claude. Returns a yes/no decision as JSON.Security review of commands, content policy checks
agentSpawns a subagent with tool access (Read, Grep, Glob). Full agentic evaluation.Complex verification, multi-file checks, code analysis
mcp_toolInvokes an MCP tool directly. Specify the server and tool name; Claude passes the hook context as input.Calling external services, Slack/Jira notifications, custom audit systems via MCP

Command hook (shell form):

{
  "type": "command",
  "command": "\"$CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR\"/.claude/hooks/lint.sh",
  "timeout": 30
}

Command hook (exec form — no shell, no quoting needed):

{
  "type": "command",
  "command": "/path/to/hook.sh",
  "args": ["--flag", "$CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR"],
  "timeout": 30
}

The args array spawns the command directly via execve — no shell is involved, so path placeholders and special characters never need quoting. Prefer exec form when your hook path or arguments contain spaces or env vars.

HTTP hook:

{
  "type": "http",
  "url": "http://localhost:8080/hooks/pre-tool-use",
  "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer $MY_TOKEN" },
  "allowedEnvVars": ["MY_TOKEN"]
}

Prompt hook:

{
  "type": "prompt",
  "prompt": "Is this shell command safe to execute? $ARGUMENTS"
}

Agent hook:

{
  "type": "agent",
  "prompt": "Verify this edit doesn't break any imports. $ARGUMENTS"
}

MCP tool hook:

{
  "type": "mcp_tool",
  "server": "slack",
  "tool": "send_message",
  "input": { "channel": "#dev-audit", "text": "Tool use: $TOOL_NAME" }
}

Configuration

▼

Where to put hooks (by scope):

LocationScope
~/.claude/settings.jsonAll projects (local to your machine)
.claude/settings.jsonSingle project (shareable, committed to git)
.claude/settings.local.jsonSingle project (gitignored, personal)
Managed policy settingsOrganization-wide (admin-controlled)
Plugin hooks/hooks.jsonActive when plugin is enabled
Skill/Agent YAML frontmatterActive while component runs

Settings.json structure:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "if": "Bash(rm *)",
            "command": "\"$CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR\"/.claude/hooks/block-rm.sh",
            "timeout": 30,
            "statusMessage": "Checking command safety..."
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Matcher patterns:

PatternEvaluated AsExample
"*", "", or omittedMatch allFires on every event
Letters, digits, _, | onlyExact or pipe-separated listBash or Edit|Write
Contains other charactersJavaScript regex^Notebook, mcp__.*

Conditional if field — narrows which tool calls trigger a hook:

  • "Bash(git *)" — only git commands
  • "Bash(rm *)" — only rm commands
  • "Edit(*.ts)" — only TypeScript edits
  • "Write(*.md)" — only Markdown writes

Environment variables available in hooks:

VariableDescription
CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIRProject root directory
CLAUDE_ENV_FILEFile path for persisting env vars (SessionStart, CwdChanged, FileChanged)
CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOTPlugin installation directory
CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATAPlugin persistent data directory
CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_IDCurrent session ID — also injected into the Bash tool subprocess environment, enabling scripts to correlate tool runs with the active session without parsing hook JSON input
CLAUDE_EFFORTActive effort level (low | medium | high | xhigh | max) — injected into Bash tool subprocess commands so scripts can branch on effort without parsing hook JSON. Also available as effort.level in the JSON delivered to hook stdin.

Exit Codes & Decision Control

▼

Exit codes determine whether a hook blocks execution or just logs a warning. Getting this right is critical for policy enforcement.

Exit CodeBehavior
0Success — stdout parsed for JSON output. Most events: written to debug log. Exceptions: UserPromptSubmit and SessionStart show stdout as context.
2Blocking error — blocks tool call, rejects prompt, prevents stop, or blocks compaction (depending on event). Stderr is fed back as the error message.
Any otherNon-blocking error — stderr shown as warning in transcript. Execution continues normally.
Exit 2 Is the Only Blocking Code

Exit 1 is non-blocking — it logs an error but doesn't stop anything. You must use exit 2 to enforce policy. This is the most common mistake when writing hooks.

PreToolUse permission decisions — control what happens to the tool call:

DecisionEffect
allowSkip permission prompt, execute immediately
denyBlock the tool call entirely
askPrompt the user to confirm
deferPause for later (non-interactive -p mode only)

When multiple hooks fire, precedence is: deny > defer > ask > allow.

PreCompact blocking — prevent context compaction from running:

MethodEffect
Exit with code 2Block compaction; stderr shown as reason
Return {"decision":"block"}Block compaction via JSON decision output

PostToolUse output replacement — replace the tool's output before Claude sees it:

{
  "hookSpecificOutput": {
    "updatedToolOutput": "Sanitized or transformed output here"
  }
}

Return this JSON from a PostToolUse hook (exit 0) to substitute the tool result Claude receives. Works for all tools — use it to redact sensitive output, normalize formats, or inject additional context.

PostToolUse continueOnBlock — soft block: feed the rejection reason back to Claude instead of ending the turn:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": ".claude/hooks/check-output.sh",
            "continueOnBlock": true
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

When "continueOnBlock": true is set, a hook that exits 2 (block) feeds its rejection reason back to Claude as a tool result and the turn continues — Claude can retry or adjust. Without this option, a block ends the turn immediately. Use continueOnBlock for "soft guard" patterns where you want Claude to self-correct rather than halt.

Hook terminalSequence — emit terminal escape sequences (desktop notifications, window title, bell) from any hook:

{
  "terminalSequence": ""
}

Return a JSON object with a terminalSequence key from any hook (exit 0). Claude Code writes the value directly to the terminal — even when there is no controlling TTY. Common sequences: "" rings the terminal bell; "]2;Task done" sets the window title. Use this to provide ambient feedback without wrapping Claude Code in a shell script or requiring a controlling terminal.

Practical Recipes

▼

Real-world hook examples you can add to .claude/settings.json.

Security — block destructive commands:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "if": "Bash(rm *)",
            "command": "echo 'Destructive command blocked' >&2 && exit 2"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Quality — auto-lint after file edits:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Edit|Write",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "npx eslint --fix \"$CLAUDE_FILE_PATH\" 2>/dev/null || true"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Workflow — read-before-edit reminder:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Edit",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "echo 'READ-BEFORE-EDIT: Read the file first before editing.'"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Policy — prompt-based security review:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "prompt",
            "prompt": "Is this shell command safe to execute? Command: $ARGUMENTS",
            "timeout": 30
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Environment — auto-setup via SessionStart:

#!/bin/bash
# .claude/hooks/session-setup.sh
if [ -n "$CLAUDE_ENV_FILE" ]; then
  echo 'export NODE_ENV=production' >> "$CLAUDE_ENV_FILE"
  echo 'export PATH="$PATH:./node_modules/.bin"' >> "$CLAUDE_ENV_FILE"
fi
exit 0
{
  "hooks": {
    "SessionStart": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "\"$CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR\"/.claude/hooks/session-setup.sh"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Observability — log all MCP operations:

{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "mcp__.*",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "echo \"$(date): MCP operation\" >> ~/mcp-audit.log"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Hook Best Practices

▼
  • Keep hooks fast — especially SessionStart, which runs on every session launch
  • Use exit 2 to enforce — exit 1 is non-blocking and won't stop anything
  • Test with --debug — see full hook execution details in the debug log
  • Use the if field — narrow trigger conditions to avoid unnecessary process spawns
  • Use async: true — for fire-and-forget operations like logging
  • Reference scripts via $CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR — always quote for paths with spaces
  • View hooks with /hooks — read-only browser showing all configured hooks by source
  • Disable all with "disableAllHooks": true — in settings.json for troubleshooting
15.5 — HTTP Hooks & Managed Web Agents

Cloud Integrations

Connect Claude Code to external services via HTTP hooks, and deploy Claude as a fully managed cloud agent for long-running autonomous work — no agent loop required.

HTTP Hooks — Webhook Integrations

▼

HTTP hooks ("type": "http") POST Claude Code lifecycle events as JSON to any HTTP endpoint. Unlike command hooks, the receiving server doesn't need to be co-located with the developer — enabling team-wide shared audit services, remote approval gateways, and webhook-based integrations with Slack, PagerDuty, GitHub Actions, and custom dashboards.

HTTP hook schema:

{
  "type": "http",
  "url": "https://your-service.example.com/hooks/tool-use",
  "headers": {
    "Authorization": "Bearer $HOOK_TOKEN",
    "X-Team": "platform-eng"
  },
  "allowedEnvVars": ["HOOK_TOKEN"],
  "timeout": 10
}

Header values support $VAR or ${VAR} interpolation. Only env vars listed in allowedEnvVars are expanded — all others remain empty strings.

Recipe 1 — Team audit log service (PostToolUse):

Every tool call across your team's Claude Code sessions is POSTed to a shared logging endpoint. The body is the standard PostToolUse JSON (session_id, tool_name, tool_input, tool_output). Index this into Elasticsearch, Datadog, or any observability stack.

{
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash|Edit|Write",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "http",
            "url": "https://audit.internal/api/claude-events",
            "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer $AUDIT_TOKEN" },
            "allowedEnvVars": ["AUDIT_TOKEN"]
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Recipe 2 — Slack notification on task completion (Stop):

Post a message to your team's Slack channel whenever Claude finishes a turn. The Stop event fires each time Claude stops responding — pair with a Slack incoming webhook URL for real-time task completion alerts.

{
  "hooks": {
    "Stop": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "http",
            "url": "$SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL",
            "headers": { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
            "allowedEnvVars": ["SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL"]
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

Recipe 3 — External approval gateway (PreToolUse):

Before allowing destructive Bash commands in a regulated environment, POST to an approval service. The service evaluates the command against policy and returns a JSON decision body. Blocking requires a JSON response body — an HTTP 4xx status alone won't stop the action.

// Hook config
{
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "http",
            "url": "https://policy.internal/approve",
            "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer $POLICY_TOKEN" },
            "allowedEnvVars": ["POLICY_TOKEN"],
            "timeout": 15
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

// Your server returns this JSON to block:
{
  "hookSpecificOutput": {
    "hookEventName": "PreToolUse",
    "permissionDecision": "deny",
    "permissionDecisionReason": "Command violates SOC2 policy: rm -rf on /data"
  }
}

// Or this JSON to allow:
{
  "hookSpecificOutput": {
    "hookEventName": "PreToolUse",
    "permissionDecision": "allow"
  }
}
HTTP Hooks — What to Watch Out For
  • Blocking requires JSON body, not HTTP status. Return a 2xx response with the appropriate hookSpecificOutput JSON. A 403 or 500 causes a hook error — execution continues, it does not block.
  • Always list env vars in allowedEnvVars. Any $VAR in headers not in this list expands to an empty string — no error, just silent auth failures.
  • Set timeout explicitly for fast gates. Default is 10 minutes. An approval gateway that hangs will block Claude for 10 minutes if you don't specify a lower timeout (e.g., "timeout": 10 seconds).
  • Non-2xx responses cause hook errors — execution proceeds. If your server returns a 500, Claude Code logs a hook error but does not block the action. Design your server to return 2xx with a deny body, not an HTTP error code.

Managed Web Agents — Cloud-Hosted Agent Runtime

▼

Managed Web Agents is Anthropic's cloud-hosted agent infrastructure. Instead of building your own agent loop, tool execution layer, and container sandbox, you get a fully managed environment where Claude can run Bash commands, read and write files, search the web, and call MCP servers — with session state persisted server-side.

Messages APIManaged Web Agents
What it isDirect model promptingPre-built, configurable agent harness in managed infrastructure
Best forCustom agent loops, fine-grained controlLong-running tasks, async work, minimal infrastructure
You buildAgent loop, tool execution, sandbox, stateJust your agent definition and task inputs
Session stateYou managePersisted server-side, fetchable in full

Four core concepts:

ConceptDescription
AgentModel + system prompt + tools + MCP servers + skills. Define once, reuse by ID across many sessions.
EnvironmentCloud container template: pre-installed packages (Python, Node, Go…), network access rules, mounted files.
SessionA running instance of an agent in an environment, performing a specific task and streaming results via SSE.
EventsMessages exchanged between your application and the agent — user turns, tool results, status updates. Fetched on demand or streamed live.

Python quickstart:

import anthropic

client = anthropic.Anthropic()  # SDK sets managed-agents beta header automatically

# 1. Create the agent once — reuse agent_id across sessions
agent = client.beta.managed_agents.agents.create(
    model="claude-opus-4-7",
    system="You are a code review specialist. Analyze PRs for bugs and security issues.",
    tools=[{"type": "bash"}, {"type": "web_search"}, {"type": "file"}],
)

# 2. Create a cloud environment with the packages you need
env = client.beta.managed_agents.environments.create(
    packages=["python3", "git", "ripgrep"],
    network={"allowed_domains": ["github.com", "api.github.com"]},
)

# 3. Start a session for a specific task
session = client.beta.managed_agents.sessions.create(
    agent_id=agent.id,
    environment_id=env.id,
)

# 4. Send a user message and stream the agent's work via SSE
with client.beta.managed_agents.sessions.stream(
    session_id=session.id,
    message="Review the changes in github.com/myorg/repo/pull/42 and flag any critical issues."
) as stream:
    for event in stream:
        print(event)  # Tool calls, text output, status updates

# See https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents for full API reference

When to use Managed Web Agents:

ScenarioWhy Managed Agents Fits
CI pipeline code reviewLong-running (minutes), triggered on PR open, needs git access and web search — container environment handles all dependencies
Scheduled maintenanceCron triggers a session; agent runs dependency updates, generates changelog, opens PR — no server to keep alive
Customer-facing AI featuresProduct creates a session per user request; agent completes the task asynchronously; results fetched via event history API
Bulk data processingFan out dozens of parallel sessions, each processing a different file or data chunk; all managed at the platform level
Managed Web Agents — What to Watch Out For
  • Beta: behavior may change. All endpoints require the managed-agents-2026-04-01 beta header. The Anthropic Python/TypeScript SDKs set this automatically via client.beta.managed_agents.
  • Rate limits apply at the org level. 300 requests/min for create endpoints (agents, sessions, environments), 600 requests/min for read endpoints. Fan-out architectures need explicit rate management.
  • Not a replacement for Messages API when you need control. If you need custom tool schemas, precise streaming behavior, or sub-turn decision logic, build your own loop with the Messages API instead.
  • Declare packages in the Environment config. Container environments don't include every tool by default — specify packages explicitly or your agent's Bash commands will fail with "command not found".
  • Outcomes and multi-agent features are research preview. Requires a separate access request at claude.com/form/claude-managed-agents.
  • Cost is runtime hours + tokens. Sessions in beta are billed at $0.08/runtime hour plus standard model token costs. A 24/7 agent costs ~$58/month in runtime before any token charges.

Novel Patterns & Real-World Use Cases

▼

Real adoption patterns from teams running HTTP hooks and Managed Web Agents in production.

Hookdeck — Inbound webhook bridge via Claude Channels:

Hookdeck's integration uses Claude Code's Channels feature (a specialized MCP server that emits notification events) to push inbound webhooks from GitHub, Stripe, or any external service directly into an active Claude Code session. The pattern: external service → Hookdeck → Claude Code Channel → Claude processes the event. Useful for having Claude react to production events (new PR, failed payment, deploy failure) without polling.

# Claude Code Channels receive external webhooks via Hookdeck CLI
hookdeck listen 3000 claude-code-channel

# Claude Code picks up events streamed from the channel MCP server
# and can take action — file edits, git operations, notifications

Sentry — Bug to PR, fully autonomous:

Sentry deployed a Managed Web Agent that takes a flagged error from their issue tracker, clones the relevant repo in a managed container, reproduces the bug, writes a fix, runs the test suite, and opens a pull request — all without human involvement. The agent uses Bash, file ops, and web search tools. Sessions run for 5–15 minutes per issue.

Notion — Parallel delegated tasks:

Notion runs dozens of parallel Managed Agent sessions simultaneously — one per delegated task from a user's workspace. Tasks include writing and shipping code, creating Slack channels, researching competitor sites, and generating project timelines. Session event history is persisted server-side so Notion can retrieve results asynchronously and surface them in the UI without a persistent connection.

Async hooks for non-blocking background work:

Async hooks (released January 2026) run in the background without blocking Claude's execution. Use them for slow operations — sending telemetry, triggering CI, updating dashboards — where you don't need the result to inform the next step. Configure with "async": true in the hook definition.

{
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "http",
            "url": "https://telemetry.internal/events",
            "async": true
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

External policy engine with PermissionRequest hook:

Teams in regulated environments (fintech, healthcare) route all permission prompts through a centralized policy engine via HTTP hook on the PermissionRequest event. The engine checks the tool call against compliance rules and returns an allow/deny decision — replacing the interactive dialog with deterministic policy enforcement. Because the hook fires before the user sees the dialog, the policy engine's decision is silent and auditable.

Use HTTP Hooks for Team-Wide Policies, Command Hooks for Personal Automation

HTTP hooks make sense when the policy, rule, or notification target is shared across multiple developers. Command hooks are better for personal automation (formatting, linting) that doesn't need a server. The two types compose — a single event can have both.

16 — Claude Monitoring

Track Usage, Costs & Tool Activity

Monitor Claude Code across your organization with OpenTelemetry. Track token spend, session activity, tool usage, and developer productivity — then pipe it into the observability stack you already use.

Quick Start

▼

Enable OpenTelemetry export with a handful of environment variables. Set these before running claude:

# 1. Enable telemetry (required)
export CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY=1

# 2. Choose exporters (both optional — configure what you need)
export OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp       # otlp | prometheus | console | none
export OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp          # otlp | console | none

# 3. Point at your collector
export OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=grpc
export OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://localhost:4317

# 4. Auth header (if your collector requires it)
export OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS="Authorization=Bearer your-token"

# 5. Run Claude Code
claude
Debugging Tip

Use OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=console with OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL=1000 to see metrics printed to stdout every second while you verify your setup.

Available Metrics

▼

Claude Code exports 8 time-series metrics. The cost and token counters are the ones most teams start with.

MetricDescriptionUnit
claude_code.cost.usageSession cost (approximation — use API console for billing)USD
claude_code.token.usageTokens used, broken down by type: input, output, cacheRead, cacheCreationtokens
claude_code.session.countCLI sessions startedcount
claude_code.active_time.totalActive time (user interactions + CLI processing)seconds
claude_code.lines_of_code.countLines added or removedcount
claude_code.commit.countGit commits created via Claudecount
claude_code.pull_request.countPRs created via Claudecount
claude_code.code_edit_tool.decisionTool permission accept/reject decisionscount

All metrics carry standard attributes: session.id, organization.id, user.account_uuid, and terminal.type. Token and cost metrics also include model.

Events & Logs

▼

When OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER is configured, Claude Code emits structured events for every prompt, tool call, and API request. Events share a prompt.id so you can trace all activity back to the prompt that triggered it.

EventFires WhenKey Attributes
claude_code.user_promptUser submits a promptprompt_length, prompt (opt-in)
claude_code.toolTool call trace spanagent_id, parent_agent_id — background subagent spans nest under the dispatching Agent tool span for correct trace hierarchy
claude_code.tool_resultTool finishes executingtool_name, success, duration_ms, tool_use_id, tool_input_size_bytes
claude_code.api_requestAPI call to Claude completesmodel, cost_usd, input/output tokens
claude_code.api_errorAPI call fails (after retries)error, status_code, attempt count
claude_code.tool_decisionTool permission accept/rejecttool_name, decision, source, tool_use_id
claude_code.skill_activatedSkill is invokedskill_name, invocation_trigger (user-slash | claude-proactive | nested-skill)
claude_code.at_mention@-mention is resolvedmention_target, resolution_result
Opt-in Detail Levels

OTEL_LOG_USER_PROMPTS=1 includes prompt text. OTEL_LOG_TOOL_DETAILS=1 adds tool parameters (bash commands, MCP names, file paths). Both are redacted by default for privacy.

Enterprise Configuration

▼

Admins can push OTel settings to all users via managed settings (MDM-distributed) so individual developers don't need to configure anything.

// Managed settings file — distributed via MDM
{
  "env": {
    "CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY": "1",
    "OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER": "otlp",
    "OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER": "otlp",
    "OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT": "http://collector.example.com:4317"
  }
}
  • Multi-team segmentation — use OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES="department=eng,team.id=platform,cost_center=123" to tag metrics by team
  • Dynamic auth — set "otelHeadersHelper": "/path/to/script.sh" in settings.json to auto-refresh tokens (runs every 29 min)
  • Cardinality control — toggle OTEL_METRICS_INCLUDE_SESSION_ID, OTEL_METRICS_INCLUDE_VERSION, and OTEL_METRICS_INCLUDE_ACCOUNT_UUID to tune storage vs. granularity
  • Survey re-opt-in — set CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_FEEDBACK_SURVEY_FOR_OTEL=1 to re-enable the session quality survey for enterprises capturing responses through OpenTelemetry (the survey is suppressed by default when OTEL is active to avoid polluting telemetry pipelines)

Distributed Traces (Beta)

▼

Link every user prompt to the API requests and tool executions it triggers as a single distributed trace. Requires both telemetry flags:

export CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY=1
export CLAUDE_CODE_ENHANCED_TELEMETRY_BETA=1
export OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp
  • Bash subprocesses inherit a TRACEPARENT env var for W3C trace context propagation — but OTEL_* configuration vars (exporter, endpoint, headers) are intentionally not inherited, so OTEL-instrumented apps run via the Bash tool use their own OTEL config and won't accidentally pick up the CLI's OTLP endpoint
  • Span content redacted by default — set OTEL_LOG_TOOL_CONTENT=1 to include tool I/O (truncated at 60 KB)
  • Visualize in Jaeger, Zipkin, Grafana Tempo, or any OTel-compatible backend

Recommended Backends

▼
SignalOptions
MetricsPrometheus, ClickHouse, Datadog, Honeycomb
Logs / EventsElasticsearch, Loki, ClickHouse, Datadog
TracesJaeger, Zipkin, Grafana Tempo, Honeycomb

For DAU/WAU/MAU metrics, choose a backend that supports efficient unique-value queries (e.g., ClickHouse, Honeycomb).

Privacy & Security

▼
  • Opt-in only — telemetry requires explicit CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRY=1
  • No code content — raw file contents and code snippets are never included in metrics or events
  • Prompts redacted — only prompt length is recorded unless OTEL_LOG_USER_PROMPTS=1 is set
  • Tool inputs redacted — tool parameters excluded unless OTEL_LOG_TOOL_DETAILS=1 is set
  • Trace content redacted — span I/O excluded unless OTEL_LOG_TOOL_CONTENT=1 (truncated at 60 KB)
  • Email included with OAuth — user.email is sent when authenticated via OAuth; configure your backend to filter if needed
ROI Measurement Guide

Anthropic provides a ready-to-use monitoring starter kit with Docker Compose configs, Prometheus + OTel setup, and Linear integration templates for automated productivity reports.

github.com/anthropics/claude-code-monitoring-guide
