OpenAI's Coding Agent

Codex Best Practices

Getting productive with OpenAI's open-source coding agent alongside Claude Code.

01 — Getting Started

Codex CLI Best Practices

From installation to agentic workflows with OpenAI's Codex.

Codex CLI is OpenAI's open-source coding agent — a terminal UI (TUI) that reads files, writes code, and runs shell commands in a sandbox. It defaults to gpt-5.5 and supports multiple model providers including OpenAI, Azure, Ollama, Bedrock, and more. If your team uses OpenAI models or wants multi-provider flexibility, Codex is the FSAD-compatible agent to learn.

Tool-Agnostic by Design

FSAD is a methodology, not a tool. While this playbook focuses on Claude Code, the same principles — spec-driven development, markdown layer, agentic workflows — apply to Codex, Gemini CLI, and other coding agents. This section helps teams that use Codex either as their primary tool or alongside Claude Code.

Claude Code vs. Codex CLI

ConceptClaude CodeCodex CLI
Project instructionsCLAUDE.mdAGENTS.md
Config directory~/.claude/~/.codex/
Config formatsettings.json (JSON)config.toml (TOML)
Default modelClaude Sonnet / Opusgpt-5.5 / codex-mini
Approval modesDefault, Plan, BypassAuto, Read-only, Full Access
SandboxPer-tool permissionsread-only / workspace-write / full-access
MCP supportClientClient + Server
IDE extensionsVS CodeVS Code, Cursor, Windsurf
Skills directory.claude/skills/.agents/skills/
Multi-agentSubagents (Task tool)Agent threads (max_threads config)
Hooks27 event types (SessionStart, PreToolUse, MessageDisplay, Stop…)8 event types (SessionStart, UserPromptSubmit, PreToolUse, PermissionRequest, PostToolUse, Stop, PreCompact, PostCompact)
Plugin marketplaceclaude plugin installcodex plugin install / /plugins TUI
Permission modelPer-tool allow/deny + 5 approval modesNamed permission profiles with filesystem + network policies

Getting Started with Codex

1

Install Codex

Install globally via npm or Homebrew:

# npm
npm install -g @openai/codex

# Homebrew (macOS)
brew install --cask codex
2

Authenticate

Codex supports two authentication methods. On first run it will prompt you to log in with your ChatGPT account, or you can set an API key:

# Option A: ChatGPT account (prompted on first run)
codex

# Option B: API key
export OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-...
3

Create AGENTS.md

This is Codex's equivalent of CLAUDE.md — your project instructions file at the repo root:

# AGENTS.md

## Project Overview
E-commerce platform built with Next.js 14, TypeScript, and PostgreSQL.

## Tech Stack
- Frontend: Next.js 14, React, Tailwind CSS
- Backend: tRPC, Drizzle ORM
- Database: PostgreSQL 16
- Testing: Vitest, Playwright

## Conventions
- Use server components by default
- All database queries go through Drizzle ORM
- Run `pnpm test` before committing
- Follow existing patterns in src/
4

First Run

Start Codex with a simple task to verify everything works:

# Interactive TUI
codex

# Or start with a prompt
codex "describe this project and suggest improvements"

AGENTS.md: Project Instructions

▼

Codex merges instructions from multiple AGENTS.md files in a top-down hierarchy. Later files override earlier ones:

# Priority order (highest → lowest):
1. ~/.codex/AGENTS.override.md   # Global overrides (highest priority)
2. ~/.codex/AGENTS.md            # Global defaults
3. <repo-root>/AGENTS.md        # Project-level instructions
4. <cwd>/AGENTS.md              # Sub-directory specifics

Key differences from CLAUDE.md:

  • Override files — AGENTS.override.md takes highest priority, useful for personal preferences that override team defaults
  • Size limit — 32 KiB by default (configurable via project_doc_max_bytes in config.toml)
  • Disable flag — Skip with --no-project-doc or CODEX_DISABLE_PROJECT_DOC=1
Dual-Tool Teams

If your team uses both Claude Code and Codex, maintain both CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md in the same repo. Keep shared conventions (tech stack, testing rules, coding standards) consistent across both files.

Running Both Tools

Many FSAD teams use both Claude Code and Codex. Keep a CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md in your repo with shared conventions. Use each tool's strengths: Claude Code for Anthropic model quality, Codex for OpenAI model access and multi-provider flexibility. The FSAD workflow — spec → plan → implement → review — works identically regardless of which agent executes.

02 — Project Anatomy

Codex Needs 4 Things

The instructions, the skills, the guardrails, the context. Same principles as Claude — different file names.

Key Insight

Prompting is temporary. Structure is permanent. A well-organized project gives Codex context automatically — every session, every agent thread, every teammate.

Pillar 1: AGENTS.md = Repo Memory

▼

Contains WHY (purpose), WHAT (repo map), HOW (rules + commands). Codex reads this file automatically at the start of every session. The 4-level hierarchy lets you layer global defaults with project-specific overrides.

# AGENTS.md

## Project Overview
E-commerce API — Node.js + PostgreSQL. Handles product catalog,
orders, and payments.

## Tech Stack
- Runtime: Node.js 20, TypeScript
- Database: PostgreSQL 15 + Prisma ORM
- Testing: Vitest, coverage > 80%

## Commands
- `npm run dev` — start dev server
- `npm test` — run tests
- `npm run lint` — lint + format

## Rules
- Never modify migration files after they've been applied
- All API endpoints require authentication middleware
- Use Zod for request validation, not manual checks
Tip

Codex merges AGENTS.md from 4 levels: ~/.codex/AGENTS.override.md (highest) → ~/.codex/AGENTS.md → repo root → current directory. Max 32 KiB by default. For hooks and tool policies, the equivalent cascade runs through config.toml — add a requirements.toml at system/org level to enforce constraints users cannot override.

Pillar 2: .agents/skills/ = Reusable Expert Modes

▼

Turn common workflows into skills. Codex skills live in .agents/skills/ and use a SKILL.md file. Each skill can include scripts, references, and assets alongside the markdown.

# .agents/skills/code-review/SKILL.md

# Code Review

## Process
1. Check for security vulnerabilities (OWASP Top 10)
2. Verify error handling covers edge cases
3. Check test coverage for new code paths
4. Review naming conventions match project style
5. Verify no secrets or credentials in code

## Checklist
- [ ] No SQL injection vectors
- [ ] Input validation on all endpoints
- [ ] Tests cover happy path + error cases
- [ ] No console.log statements left behind

Pillar 3: .rules Files = Guardrails

▼

Codex uses the Shell Tool MCP with .rules files for deterministic guardrails — allowing, prompting, or forbidding specific shell commands. Models forget. Rules don't.

# .rules — Shell command policies

allow npm test
allow npm run lint
allow npm run build
prompt npm install *        # Ask before installing packages
forbid rm -rf *             # Never allow recursive delete
forbid git push --force     # Never allow force push
Important

Rules intercept execve(2) calls, so they know the full path of executed programs. This is more granular than Claude's hooks — you control command execution at the shell level.

Pillar 4: docs/ = Progressive Context

▼

Architecture overview, ADRs, runbooks. Don't bloat AGENTS.md — put detailed docs in files Codex can read when needed. Same pattern as Claude.

docs/
├── architecture.md        # System design overview
├── adr/                   # Architecture Decision Records
│   ├── 001-use-prisma.md
│   ├── 002-auth-strategy.md
│   └── 003-caching-layer.md
├── runbooks/              # Operational procedures
│   ├── deploy.md
│   └── incident-response.md
└── api/                   # API documentation
    └── endpoints.md

Codex discovers and reads these files on demand when working in related areas. The key is progressive context — only loaded when relevant.

Pillar 5: Local AGENTS.md for Risky Modules

▼

Place small AGENTS.md files near sharp edges. Codex sees gotchas exactly when working in that directory.

# src/auth/AGENTS.md

## Critical: Authentication Module

- NEVER modify the JWT signing key rotation logic
- Token refresh uses sliding window — do not change to fixed expiry
- Rate limiting is per-IP, configured in middleware (not here)
- All changes to this module require security review
- Test with: `npm test -- --grep "auth"`

Good candidates: src/auth/, src/billing/, src/persistence/, infra/

Project Structure Map

my-project/
├── AGENTS.md                    # Repo memory (auto-loaded)
├── .codex/
│   └── config.toml              # Project settings
├── .agents/
│   └── skills/                  # Reusable expert modes
│       ├── code-review/
│       │   └── SKILL.md
│       └── deploy/
│           ├── SKILL.md
│           └── scripts/
│               └── check-staging.sh
├── .rules                       # Shell command guardrails
├── docs/                        # Progressive context
│   ├── architecture.md
│   └── adr/
├── src/
│   ├── auth/
│   │   └── AGENTS.md            # Local guardrails
│   └── billing/
│       └── AGENTS.md            # Local guardrails
└── spec.md                      # Feature specification
03 — Integrations

Connect Codex to Your Tools via MCP

Add MCP servers with the codex mcp add command or edit config.toml directly.

Codex CLI ──▶ MCP Protocol ──▶ JIRA
                             ──▶ Figma
                             ──▶ Notion
                             ──▶ Azure DevOps
                             ──▶ Google Workspace

Setting Up MCP Connections via codex mcp

Codex uses the codex mcp CLI to manage MCP servers. For HTTP servers (Jira, Figma, Notion), use --url. For stdio servers (Azure DevOps, community tools), pass the command after --.

1

Add an MCP Server

Use codex mcp add to register a server. For hosted HTTP endpoints, use --url:

# HTTP server (hosted endpoint)
codex mcp add atlassian --url https://mcp.atlassian.com/v1/mcp

# Stdio server (local process)
codex mcp add ado -- npx -y @azure-devops/mcp your-org-name

# Stdio server with env vars
codex mcp add my-server --env API_KEY=xxx -- npx -y @some/mcp-server
2

Authenticate via OAuth

For servers that support OAuth, run codex mcp login. A browser window opens for authentication:

codex mcp login atlassian
3

Verify the Connection

Use /mcp inside the Codex TUI or codex mcp list from the terminal to confirm servers are connected:

# In the Codex TUI:
/mcp

# From the terminal:
codex mcp list
4

Manage Servers

Use codex mcp subcommands to manage your connections:

codex mcp list              # List all configured servers
codex mcp get <name>        # Show server details
codex mcp remove <name>     # Remove a server
codex mcp login <name>      # OAuth login for a server
codex mcp logout <name>     # Clear OAuth credentials
CLI vs. Config File

Use codex mcp add for quick setup — it writes to ~/.codex/config.toml automatically. Edit config.toml directly when you need advanced options like startup_timeout_sec, enabled_tools allowlists, or project-scoped servers (.codex/config.toml in trusted projects).

Available Integrations

JIRA MCP

▼

What it does: Read, create, and update JIRA issues directly from Codex. Search issues, transition statuses, add comments, and manage sprints.

# Add via CLI
codex mcp add atlassian --url https://mcp.atlassian.com/v1/mcp
codex mcp login atlassian
# Or in ~/.codex/config.toml
[mcp_servers.atlassian]
url = "https://mcp.atlassian.com/v1/mcp"

Figma MCP

▼

What it does: Read Figma designs, extract component specs, colors, typography, and layout details.

# Add via CLI
codex mcp add figma --url https://mcp.figma.com/mcp
codex mcp login figma
# Or in ~/.codex/config.toml
[mcp_servers.figma]
url = "https://mcp.figma.com/mcp"

Notion MCP

▼

What it does: Search, read, create, and update Notion pages and databases.

# Add via CLI
codex mcp add notion --url https://mcp.notion.com/mcp
codex mcp login notion
# Or in ~/.codex/config.toml
[mcp_servers.notion]
url = "https://mcp.notion.com/mcp"

Azure DevOps MCP

▼

What it does: Manage work items, repos, pipelines, wikis, test plans, and advanced security. Microsoft's official MCP server with browser-based OAuth.

# Add via CLI (stdio server)
codex mcp add ado -- npx -y @azure-devops/mcp your-org-name
# Or in ~/.codex/config.toml
[mcp_servers.ado]
command = "npx"
args = ["-y", "@azure-devops/mcp", "your-org-name"]

Requires Node.js 20+. Browser-based OAuth on first use. See microsoft/azure-devops-mcp.

Google Workspace MCP

▼

What it does: Google offers hosted MCP servers for Cloud infrastructure (BigQuery, Cloud SQL, GKE, etc.). Workspace products (Docs, Sheets, Drive) are not yet available as hosted endpoints — use a community server for those.

Google Cloud MCP (official, hosted)

# Add via CLI — example: BigQuery
codex mcp add bigquery --url https://bigquery.googleapis.com/mcp
# Or in ~/.codex/config.toml
[mcp_servers.bigquery]
url = "https://bigquery.googleapis.com/mcp"

Requires gcloud auth application-default login and gcloud beta services mcp enable SERVICE. See Google Cloud MCP docs.

Google Workspace MCP (community, self-hosted)

# Add via CLI (build from source first)
codex mcp add google-workspace -- /path/to/google_workspace_mcp

Requires building from source (Rust) and a Google Cloud project with OAuth. See taylorwilsdon/google_workspace_mcp.

Codex as MCP Server

Unlike Claude Code, Codex can also act as an MCP server (codex mcp-server), allowing other agents to invoke Codex as a tool. Full docs at developers.openai.com/codex/mcp.

Codex Chrome Extension

OpenAI released a Codex Chrome extension (May 2026) that gives Codex direct browser access — a different integration model from MCP. Instead of routing through an API, Codex reads and acts on the current browser context.

CapabilityDetails
Parallel tab operationRuns across multiple tabs simultaneously without taking over the browser
Per-site access controlUsers grant Codex access on a site-by-site basis — not all-or-nothing
Browser context injectionCodex can read page content, fill forms, and interact with web-based tools (Jira, Figma, Notion, Linear) without an MCP server
Chrome Extension vs. MCP

Use the Chrome extension for web-based tools where you need direct page interaction (reading tickets, filling fields, navigating UI). Use MCP for tools with stable APIs — MCP is more reliable for structured data and automated pipelines. Both can be active simultaneously.

04 — Code Review

Built-in Code Review with /review

Codex includes a native code review command that analyzes diffs without modifying your code.

How /review Works

The /review slash command analyzes your current diff (staged and unstaged changes) and provides feedback on bugs, security issues, code quality, and style — without making any modifications to your files.

Usage

1

Start a Codex Session

Launch Codex in your project directory:

codex
2

Run /review

Type /review to analyze your current changes. Codex examines the diff and provides structured feedback:

# Review current working tree changes
/review

# Or ask for a specific review focus
"Review the changes in src/auth/ for security issues"
3

Act on Feedback

Review the findings and ask Codex to fix specific issues, or continue working on your own.

Claude Code Comparison

Claude Code uses a multi-agent fleet for code review (multiple specialized agents in parallel). Codex's /review is single-agent by default but can leverage multi-agent threads if configured. Both approaches catch bugs — Claude's is more comprehensive, Codex's is faster for quick reviews.

05 — Building Skills

Turn Repeatable Workflows into Reusable Expert Modes

Codex skills are markdown-driven workflows stored in .agents/skills/ — each skill gets its own directory with a SKILL.md file.

What are Codex Skills?

Skills are markdown instructions that Codex follows when invoked. Unlike Claude's flat .claude/skills/ files, Codex skills use a directory structure: each skill has a folder containing SKILL.md plus optional scripts, references, and asset files.

Skill Directory Structure

.agents/skills/
├── code-review/
│   └── SKILL.md              # Skill instructions
├── deploy/
│   ├── SKILL.md              # Skill instructions
│   ├── scripts/
│   │   └── check-staging.sh  # Helper scripts
│   └── references/
│       └── deploy-checklist.md
└── estimate/
    └── SKILL.md

Example: Deployment Skill

Deploy Skill — SKILL.md

▼ 
# Deployment Skill

## Overview
Guided deployment to staging and production environments.

## Process
1. Run the test suite: `npm test`
2. Check for uncommitted changes: `git status`
3. Run the staging check script: `./scripts/check-staging.sh`
4. Build the production bundle: `npm run build`
5. Deploy to staging: `npm run deploy:staging`
6. Run smoke tests against staging
7. If smoke tests pass, deploy to production

## Safety Rules
- NEVER deploy directly to production without staging
- Always check the deploy-checklist.md reference
- Abort if any test or smoke test fails
Best Practice

Start with one skill for your most repeated workflow. Codex skills can include shell scripts and reference docs alongside the markdown — use this for deployment checklists, migration procedures, or incident response runbooks.

06 — Guidelines

Lessons from Real-World Codex Adoption

Practical guidance for teams using Codex for spec-driven agentic development.

Prompting

Be Specific About What, Not How

Tell Codex what outcome you want, not the implementation steps. Let the agent choose the approach based on codebase context.

Provide Business Context

Include business requirements, constraints, and existing patterns. "We need a user preferences API because mobile users complain about notification noise" is better than "add a PATCH endpoint."

Use Tab for File References

Press Tab to fuzzy-search files by name (prefix with @). This ensures the right context is loaded and prevents Codex from searching in the wrong places.

Use /plan Mode First

For complex features, start with /plan. Let Codex analyze and propose before making changes. Review the plan, then let it execute.

Project Structure

Keep AGENTS.md Focused

Stay under the 32 KiB limit. Put detailed docs in docs/ for progressive context — don't bloat your project instructions file.

Use Skills for Repeatable Workflows

If you find yourself giving the same instructions across sessions, encode them as a skill in .agents/skills/.

Use Local AGENTS.md for Sensitive Modules

Place small AGENTS.md files near auth, billing, and infrastructure code. Codex sees the guardrails exactly when working in that directory.

Use /compact to Manage Context

Long conversations eat context. Use /compact to summarize the conversation and free up tokens for the agent to work with.

Anti-Patterns to Avoid

Over-Permissive Sandbox

Using danger-full-access outside of containers defeats the security model. Stick with workspace-write for development.

Bloated AGENTS.md

Stuffing every rule into AGENTS.md defeats the purpose. Keep it focused on high-level rules; use docs/ and local AGENTS.md for details.

Ignoring Session Resume

Codex saves sessions automatically. Use codex resume to pick up where you left off instead of re-explaining context from scratch.

Not Using /compact

Long conversations degrade agent performance. Use /compact regularly to summarize and free context tokens.

Security Review

The sec-review-team skill is model-agnostic — it spawns Claude Code agents internally to run a multi-specialist security review, but its output (REPORT.md + FINDINGS.jsonl) is a workflow any team can adopt regardless of which CLI they use day-to-day. Codex teams can run the review via Claude Code and apply the fixes directly in their Codex workflow.

07 — Hooks

Hooks — Automation & Guardrails

Event-driven shell commands that fire deterministically at specific points in the agent loop. Models forget. Hooks don't.

Hooks inject scripts into Codex's agentic loop at named lifecycle events. Unlike instructions in AGENTS.md that the model may or may not follow, hooks always execute when their event triggers — making them ideal for audit logging, policy enforcement, and automated validation.

Generally Available — May 2026

Codex Hooks reached General Availability on 2026-05-14. They are production-ready with no feature flag required — [features] codex_hooks = true is the default and stable for use in production pipelines, enterprise environments, and automated workflows.

Hook Event Types

EventWhen it firesCan block?Claude Code equivalent
SessionStartSession begins or resumesNo — adds context onlySessionStart
UserPromptSubmitUser submits a promptYes (exit 2)UserPromptSubmit
PreToolUseBefore Bash, apply_patch, or MCP tool runs; hooks can inject additional context into the turn by returning a context field in JSON outputYes (exit 2 or deny JSON)PreToolUse
PermissionRequestWhen Codex seeks approval (shell escalation, network)Yes — allow / denyPermissionRequest
PostToolUseAfter tool completes (including failed runs)Yes (exit 2 — replaces output)PostToolUse
StopWhen a turn completesYes — can request continuationStop
PreCompactBefore context compaction runsYes (exit 2 blocks compaction)PreCompact
PostCompactAfter context compaction completesNoPostCompact

Configuration

Enable hooks with [features] codex_hooks = true (default). Define hooks inline in config.toml or in a separate hooks.json. Project-scoped hooks go in .codex/config.toml and only load in trusted projects.

config.toml hook syntax

▼ 
# ~/.codex/config.toml  (global) or .codex/config.toml (project)
[features]
codex_hooks = true

# Fire before every Bash call
[[hooks.PreToolUse]]
matcher = "^Bash$"

[[hooks.PreToolUse.hooks]]
type          = "command"
command       = "/usr/bin/python3 ~/.codex/hooks/check-command.py"
timeout       = 10
statusMessage = "Checking command safety"

# Fire after any apply_patch / Edit / Write
[[hooks.PostToolUse]]
matcher = "apply_patch"

[[hooks.PostToolUse.hooks]]
type    = "command"
command = "/usr/bin/python3 ~/.codex/hooks/audit-log.py"

Exit Code Semantics

Exit codeMeaning
0 + valid JSONProcess output per event schema (allow, deny, inject context, request continuation)
0 + plain textAppend as developer context (SessionStart, UserPromptSubmit)
0 + no outputPass-through — hook ran successfully, no action taken
2Block / deny; read reason from stderr; exact behavior depends on event type

Recipes

Recipe: Block dangerous shell commands (PreToolUse)

▼ 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import json, sys, re

hook_input = json.load(sys.stdin)
command = hook_input["tool_input"].get("command", "")

if re.search(r"rm\s+-rf|sudo\s+rm|DROP\s+TABLE", command):
    print(json.dumps({
        "hookSpecificOutput": {
            "hookEventName": "PreToolUse",
            "permissionDecision": "deny",
            "permissionDecisionReason": "High-risk command blocked by policy"
        }
    }))
sys.exit(0)

Recipe: Detect API keys in prompts (UserPromptSubmit)

▼ 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import json, sys, re

hook_input = json.load(sys.stdin)
prompt = hook_input.get("prompt", "")

if re.search(r"sk-[a-zA-Z0-9]{20,}|AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}", prompt):
    sys.stderr.write("Possible API key or AWS credential detected in prompt\n")
    sys.exit(2)  # blocks the prompt submission

Recipe: Audit-log every tool call (PostToolUse)

▼ 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import json, sys, datetime, pathlib

hook_input = json.load(sys.stdin)
log_entry = {
    "ts":      datetime.datetime.utcnow().isoformat(),
    "session": hook_input["session_id"],
    "tool":    hook_input["tool_name"],
    "input":   hook_input["tool_input"],
}

log_path = pathlib.Path.home() / ".codex" / "audit.jsonl"
with log_path.open("a") as f:
    f.write(json.dumps(log_entry) + "\n")

# Exit 0 with no output = pass-through
Claude Code Comparison

Codex has 6 hook event types; Claude Code has more (26 total events including SubagentStart, TaskCreated, FileChanged, PreCompact, etc.). Both use the same input-via-stdin / exit-code pattern. If you're migrating hook scripts, the core logic is portable — only the event names and JSON shape differ slightly.

08 — Codex Cheat Sheet

Everything You Need in One Place

Keyboard shortcuts, commands, flags, configuration, and environment variables.

Keyboard Shortcuts

KeyAction
EnterInject instructions into current turn
Ctrl+CClose session / cancel

Alt+.Raise reasoning level for current model

Slash Commands

CommandAction
/planEnter plan mode — propose before implementing
/reviewAnalyze diffs without modifying code
/goalCreate a persisted goal; browse discoverable goals in TUI. Goals pause across session resume by default — opt back in from the picker. Supports multi-day duration tracking.
/forkClone conversation to a new thread
/sideOpen an ephemeral branch for a focused follow-up; doesn't pollute main context
/newStart a fresh conversation within the same CLI session
/compactSummarize conversation to free context tokens
/diffShow current changes (staged and unstaged); workspace-aware — diff is scoped to your project root
/modelSwitch model and/or reasoning level mid-session
/fastToggle fast mode (on / off / status)
/agentSwitch between active agent threads
/pluginsBrowse, install, and manage marketplace plugins
/appsBrowse and insert available connectors
/ideInject IDE file/editor context into the current session (workspace-aware)
/mcpList configured MCP tools
/permissionsChange approval mode mid-session
/initGenerate an AGENTS.md scaffold for the current project
/vimToggle Vim modal editing in the composer. Set vim_mode = true in config.toml to enable by default.
/keymap [debug]Remap TUI keyboard shortcuts interactively. Use /keymap debug to inspect raw terminal key events — helpful when Vim mode or chord bindings don't register.

/hooksBrowse and toggle configured hooks from the TUI without editing config files
/personalitySet agent personality (friendly / pragmatic / none)
/copyCopy the latest completed response to clipboard (also Ctrl+O)

/debug-configPrint configuration layers and policy diagnostics
/exitClose session

CLI Flags

FlagPurpose
--model / -mSelect model (e.g., gpt-5.5, codex-mini-latest)

--approval-mode / -aSet approval level (auto, read-only, full-access)
--quiet / -qMinimal output for scripting
--jsonJSON Lines output for machine consumption; includes reasoning-token usage
-iAttach image / screenshot (multimodal)

--notifyDesktop notifications when tasks complete
--no-project-docSkip AGENTS.md loading
--output-schemaEnforce JSON Schema on response (use with exec)
--ephemeralNo session persistence (use with exec)
codex updateUpdate Codex CLI to the latest version
codex remote-controlStart a headless, remotely controllable app-server — a simpler entrypoint than configuring the full app-server stack. Allows external clients to drive a Codex session via the protocol API. Added in v0.130.0.
codex doctorRun support-ready diagnostics across runtime, auth, terminal, network, config, and local state. Added in v0.131.0.

09 — Power Usage

Advanced Features for Power Users

Multi-agent workflows, hooks, plugins, permission profiles, Bedrock, session management, CI/CD, multi-provider models, and Codex Cloud.

Multi-Agent Workflows

▼

Codex supports experimental multi-agent threads with configurable concurrency and specialized roles.

# config.toml
[agents]
max_threads = 6
max_depth = 1
job_max_runtime_seconds = 3600

Built-in roles:

  • default — Standard agent behavior
  • worker — Focused task execution
  • explorer — Read-only codebase analysis
  • monitor — Long-running observation (up to 1-hour windows)

Batch processing: spawn_agents_on_csv reads a CSV and spawns one worker per row — useful for bulk migrations and parallel test generation.

Conversation Forking

▼

Use /fork to clone the current conversation into a new thread. Useful for exploring alternative approaches without losing your main context.

# In a Codex session:
/fork

# Codex creates a new thread with the full conversation history
# You can explore a different approach in the fork
# Use /agent to switch between threads

Vim Editing Mode

▼

Codex v0.129.0 adds modal Vim editing in the composer. Toggle it mid-session with /vim, or enable it permanently in config:

# ~/.codex/config.toml — enable Vim mode globally
vim_mode = true

# Or toggle mid-session
/vim

In Vim mode the composer supports standard motions (hjkl, w/b/e, 0/$, dd, yy, p, visual selection). Vim-specific keybindings are scoped separately from the global TUI keymap so your /keymap overrides don't collide with Vim operators.

Use /keymap debug to inspect what key events your terminal is sending — helpful when certain Vim chords don't register correctly.

Session Management

▼

Codex saves sessions automatically. Resume previous work without re-explaining context. The redesigned resume/fork picker lets you select, resume, or fork any saved session in a single UI — no need to know the session ID upfront. Renamed threads appear as name (thread-id) in resume hints for easy identification, and you can paste text directly into the picker search box to filter sessions.

# Show session picker (redesigned resume/fork picker in v0.129.0)
codex resume

# Resume the most recent session
codex resume --last

# Resume in headless exec mode — keeps session context, enforces JSON output
codex exec resume --output-schema schema.json "continue analyzing endpoints"

# Run a throwaway session (no persistence)
codex exec --ephemeral "quick fix: update version in package.json"

# Update Codex CLI to the latest version
codex update

# Raw scrollback mode — scroll through session output without affecting
# the composer; configure the keybind via /keymap

CI/CD Integration

▼

Use codex exec for headless execution in pipelines. Combine with --json for machine-readable output.

# Headless execution — no TUI, streams to stderr
codex exec "fix all lint errors and run tests"

# JSON Lines output — includes reasoning-token usage
codex exec --json "analyze this codebase for security issues"

# Structured response with schema enforcement
codex exec --output-schema schema.json "list all API endpoints"

# Resume a prior session in headless mode — preserves context, enforces schema
codex exec resume --output-schema schema.json "continue from last checkpoint"

# Autonomous execution via permission profile (replaces deprecated --full-auto)
codex exec --approval-mode never "update dependencies and fix breaking changes"

# Quiet mode for scripting
codex exec -q "generate changelog from recent commits" > CHANGELOG.md

# Throwaway session (no session history saved)
codex exec --ephemeral "check for unused imports"

Codex Access Tokens

▼

Codex access tokens let ChatGPT Enterprise workspace members run fully non-interactive Codex workflows without manual browser-based authentication. Tokens carry the user's ChatGPT workspace identity and are ideal for scripts, schedulers, and private CI runners that can't go through an OAuth flow.

Who can create tokens: ChatGPT Enterprise workspace owners must first authorize members to generate tokens via the workspace admin settings. Once authorized, members create tokens in their account settings and reference them at runtime.

# Set the access token via environment variable
export CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN=your-token-here

# Non-interactive execution — no browser prompt
codex exec --approval-mode never "run all tests and report failures"

# Use in a CI runner (GitHub Actions example)
env:
  CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN }}
Treat Like an API Key

Access tokens grant full Codex execution rights under your workspace identity. Store them in secrets managers (AWS Secrets Manager, GitHub Secrets, HashiCorp Vault) — never commit to version control or log in CI output. Rotate tokens if exposure is suspected.

Use cases: nightly background refactors, automated spec-to-code pipelines, private on-premise CI runners that need Codex without a browser session. Pairs well with --approval-mode never permission profiles for fully autonomous runs.

Multi-Provider Models

▼

Codex supports models from multiple providers. Configure in config.toml or use the --model flag.

ProviderExample ModelsConfiguration
OpenAIgpt-5.5, codex-mini-latestDefault — uses OPENAI_API_KEY
Azure OpenAIDeployed modelsAzure endpoint + API key
OllamaLocal modelsLocal endpoint (http://localhost:11434)
Geminigemini-2.5-proGoogle AI API key
Mistralmistral-largeMistral API key
DeepSeekdeepseek-chatDeepSeek API key
OpenRouterAny hosted modelOpenRouter API key
Profiles

Use --profile to switch between configurations. Example: codex --profile local for Ollama, codex --profile cloud for OpenAI.

Permission Profiles

▼

Permission profiles replace the old binary sandbox flags with named, reusable policies for filesystem and network access. Define them in ~/.codex/config.toml and apply with --approval-mode or default_permissions.

Built-in ProfileFilesystemNetwork
:read-onlyRead everywhereNone
:workspaceWrite to project root; deny *.env / *.secretsRestricted
:danger-no-sandboxUnrestrictedFull

Define a custom profile:

# ~/.codex/config.toml
[permissions.myproject.filesystem.":project_roots"]
"." = "write"
"**/*.env" = "none"       # deny reads of .env files
glob_scan_max_depth = 3

[permissions.myproject.network]
enabled = true
mode = "limited"

# Apply as default
default_permissions = ":myproject"
Enterprise: requirements.toml

Admins can enforce which profiles are allowed and deny filesystem paths via requirements.toml — an admin-managed policy file that users cannot override. Install it at a system path or distribute via MDM.

Amazon Bedrock

▼

Codex has first-class Amazon Bedrock support via AWS SigV4 signing and standard AWS credential resolution. Useful for teams with existing AWS infrastructure or data-residency requirements.

# ~/.codex/config.toml
[model_providers.amazon-bedrock.aws]
profile = "my-aws-profile"   # or use env vars
region  = "us-west-2"

# Credentials via standard AWS env vars also work:
# AWS_PROFILE=my-profile AWS_REGION=us-west-2 codex

AWS account state (available models, quota) is surfaced to app clients. Pairs well with permission profiles to restrict what Bedrock agents can touch. In addition to standard AWS credential env vars and named profiles, aws login console-login credentials (from aws login profiles) are supported for Bedrock auth as of v0.130.0.

/goal Workflows

▼

Persisted goals let you define a long-running objective that survives context compaction. Goals are now discoverable — browse all active goals from the TUI picker. When you resume a session, goals are paused by default so they don't carry over unintended work; opt back in from the picker. Goals show clear validation feedback and support multi-day duration tracking.

# In a Codex session — set a persistent goal
/goal

# TUI picker: Create / Pause / Resume / Clear / Browse all goals
# Goal persists across /compact; pauses on resume (opt back in from picker)
# Multi-day duration shown in goal status output

Goals are ideal for large refactors or multi-day feature work. The paused-across-resume default prevents stale goals from leaking into new sessions — always check the picker when resuming long-running work.

Plugins

▼

Codex plugins extend the agent with bundled tools, skills, and hooks. Browse the marketplace in the TUI or install from the CLI. Plugins can be scoped globally or to a trusted project.

# In the TUI: open the plugin marketplace
/plugins

# Install a plugin from the marketplace
codex plugin install <plugin-name>

# Remove a plugin
codex plugin remove <plugin-name>

# Share a plugin with your workspace
codex plugin share <plugin-name>

# Sync remote plugin bundles
codex plugin sync

Plugin scoping:

  • Global (~/.codex/) — available in every session
  • Project (.codex/) — loaded only in trusted projects
  • Workspace-shared — install once, available to teammates via workspace share link; admin-disabled plugins are flagged in /plugins

Plugins can bundle skills (SKILL.md), hooks, and tool allowlists in a single installable unit. Plugin-bundled hooks are enabled/disabled via the plugin's configuration. As of v0.130.0, the plugin details view (accessible from /plugins) shows all bundled hooks alongside skills and tools. As of v0.131.0, plugin workflows gained marketplace CLI commands (codex plugin share, version-aware publish and checkout), clearer shared-workspace buckets, and plugin hooks are now default-enabled on install.

Claude Code Equivalent

Codex plugins map to Claude Code's plugin system — both use a marketplace model with skill + hook bundling. The key difference: Codex plugins install via codex plugin install; Claude plugins install via claude plugin install.

Monitoring & Observability

▼

Codex exports structured telemetry via OpenTelemetry and provides machine-readable exec output for pipeline integration.

# ~/.codex/config.toml — OTEL export
[otel]
environment = "prod"
exporter    = "otlp-http"

[otel.exporter.otlp-http]
endpoint = "http://otel-collector:4318"
protocol = "json"

What gets traced:

  • Tool calls (tool name, input size, output size)
  • Code-mode transitions and session lifecycle
  • Multi-agent thread relationships (rollout tracing)
  • Reasoning-token usage (via codex exec --json)
Audit Logging via Hooks

For teams without an OTEL collector, use a PostToolUse hook to append tool calls to a local log file. This gives you a full audit trail with zero infrastructure overhead — see the Hooks section for a recipe.

Codex Cloud & Mobile

▼

Run Codex tasks in the cloud instead of locally. Access via the CLI or the web interface at chatgpt.com/codex.

# Manage cloud tasks
codex cloud

# Launch a desktop app version
codex app

Cloud execution is useful for long-running tasks, team-shared environments, and when you want to offload compute from your local machine.

ChatGPT Mobile App Connection

Since May 2026 you can control a local Codex session from the ChatGPT mobile app (iOS / Android). Useful for monitoring long-running goals, submitting follow-up prompts, or checking progress while away from your desk.

StepAction
1Start the Codex desktop app on your Mac and sign in with your ChatGPT account
2Open the ChatGPT mobile app on iOS or Android (same account)
3Navigate to the Codex section and select the active Mac session to connect
4Submit prompts, review output, or pause/resume goals from mobile
Best Use Case

Pair mobile control with /goal workflows. Start a multi-day goal on your Mac, then monitor and steer it from your phone — without needing to sit at a desktop between turns.

Full Stack Agentic Development

Codex Best Practices Guide — for R&D teams adopting FSAD.

Measuring Success

KPIs to Measure Impact

Key metrics to track adoption, productivity, and ROI of agentic development.

01 — FSAD Metrics

Measuring FSAD Success

What to measure to demonstrate the value of agentic development to your organization.

Highest Priority

Token Consumption by User

Track per-developer AI usage to identify adoption patterns and cost centers. High consumption with high output signals effective use; high consumption with low output signals coaching opportunities.

Total Monthly Epics / Tickets

Volume of work items completed, comparing pre- vs. post-FSAD adoption. Expect a meaningful increase as AI agents accelerate implementation cycles.

Total Monthly Story Points

Throughput measurement for sprint-over-sprint comparison. Track velocity trends after FSAD adoption to quantify productivity gains.

Total PRs / Commits

Development velocity indicator. AI-assisted workflows typically produce higher commit frequency with smaller, more focused changesets.

Maintenance vs. New Product Development

Breakdown of time and code produced by maintenance vs. new product development. Measures whether AI frees engineering capacity for innovation over sustaining work.

Total $ Spend by AI Tool

Cost tracking per tool (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, etc.) for ROI analysis. Correlate spend against velocity and output metrics to demonstrate return on investment.

Other Metrics

AI Coding Tools WAU

Weekly Active Users across AI coding tools. Measures adoption breadth across the engineering organization.

Lines of Code Produced

AI-generated vs. human-written code split. Track the ratio over time to understand how AI contribution evolves.

AI Commits vs. Acceptance Rates

How often AI-generated code is accepted vs. revised. A quality signal — high acceptance rates indicate well-configured agents and effective specs.

Start Small

Don't instrument everything on day one. Token consumption + story points alone can demonstrate ROI within a single sprint. Add granularity as your FSAD practice matures and leadership asks deeper questions.

v2.60.0 · 2026-05-15

Self-contained natural language search. Replaced the plain String.includes() keyword filter with a two-tier search system that requires zero setup for recipients. Tier 1 — MiniSearch v7.1.1 (MIT, ~19 KB, fully inlined): BM25-ranked fuzzy search with prefix matching and field weighting (title ×3, label ×2, text ×1) — typo-tolerant queries like "effor level" and "kv cach" return correct sections immediately with no CDN dependency. Tier 2 — Transformers.js semantic layer (progressive enhancement): lazy-loads Xenova/all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (~24 MB, browser-cached after first load) and embeds 219 pre-extracted section/collapsible text chunks at runtime; queries ≥3 words or containing ? blend keyword and cosine-similarity semantic results, with semantic matches badged ✦; embedding vectors cache in IndexedDB for instant load on subsequent sessions. Build pipeline: scripts/build-embeddings.py (stdlib-only Python) extracts chunks from the source HTML; scripts/build-dist.py now calls the embeddings script and injects PLAYBOOK_EMBEDDINGS directly into the dist artifact. Graceful degradation: if CDN is unreachable, Tier 1 answers immediately. A status indicator in the search popover shows "Loading smart search…" / "Smart search ready" / "Using keyword search".
